PART ONE

CHAPTER Ponderables, Pranks and Poetry 

MAN, together with his contemporaries, is likely to take more or less for granted the character of the times in which he and they live. This is why the philosopher, who does not take for granted, is such an astonishing being when he turns his mind to his own era. He does not amaze his own era; he astonishes only succeeding generations. Scientists or artists may inflame the imagination of the society they inhabit, but ordinarily the philosopher, like the saint, acquires stature only when he dies. 

A man may take his own environment for granted; but if, in the course of his life, or after his death, he becomes important to society or to certain individuals, this society or these certain persons cannot, and should not, accept his early life without fixing it in every group, large or small, with which he has come into contact. 

From the above the application is made that, although the subject of this biography may, quite naturally as a child, have been incurious about the world in which he lived, we must not display this same lack of curiosity. We do not know what these very first reactions were. His parents, living as they did near the frontier, were too busy to keep journals, and we have no reported expression from him. We do, however, know a great deal about the young, and the older, man. And since he was so conspicuously a man of his time, it is up to us to have a look at the world into which he was born - the conditions of his country, his state, his community, and his family.

[4] 

However uninformed we may be of this little boy's first impressions of the world, we must not assume our lack of information to be total for the years immediately following his early youth. For 

Ben Munson, as a man just turned twenty, was already at grips with the larger problems of humanity. Not only did he struggle with them, he met them lucidly, intelligently, and with the manifest ardor of adult enthusiasm for understanding the values of life an enthusiasm that increased as he grew older, and which he never abandoned. 

From this fact one is justified in assuming that even as a young child he must have been serious and studious, extremely alert to the echoes from the great world of relative culture beyond and below the upward-swinging course of the Ohio River. This seems a safe assumption, because a good deal of what a man thinks with clarity at twenty-one, he has at least mulled over between the ages of ten and fifteen.

Ben Munson had a balanced nature, capable of the utmost seriousness, and just as prone to fun and foolishness. This is proved by the circumstances of his life, by the way he altered these circumstances, and by the kind of man the boy turned out to be. No man who achieved such public and private success could be possessed of any but a well-distributed endowment. Although he was intensely civic-minded and worked arduously at pioneering many sound enterprises (through them making one of the large fortunes of his time and region), he was a perfect husband and the kindest of fathers; one who seized every opportunity to write charming verses as an accompaniment to gifts for his wife and his children on the occasion of anniversaries and birthdays. 

The following is a good example of his more serious attempts at poetry (although probably the thing that most endeared him to his family and friends was his completely original vocabulary of epithets and picturesque figures of speech):

Ponderables, Pranks and Poetry 

[5] 

LINES IN CONTEMPLATION OF ORTHIS LYNX-A SILURIAN FOSSIL 

Let us wander in the distance 

Up the fading stream of time 

And from these obtain assistance 

In deciphering things sublime. 

Let us ask you when fair Nature 

Smiled upon your living form, While you played a happy creature 

Undisturbed by wind or storm. 

Tell us how the seas have shivered 

Since you covered up your head, Teach us how the land has quivered, Since you made your stony bed. 

Yes, I'll tell you all the ages 

That have passed like visions swift, And the noisy quaking stages 

That have caused my tomb to shift.

Read the history that is printed On the rocks that lie around, Ask each stone whose face indented 

Tells the age where it was found. 

Call my brothers and relations

Who have deeper beds than mine 

And whose ages by their stations 

You can easily divine. 

Ask the mountains and the hillsides, Ask the chasms of the streams,

Count the marks of ancient full-tides 

Where the high cliff proudest seems. 

Read the footprints of the ages

In the rocks on every shore, In that history's countless pages 

Find your answer ever more.

[6] 

Time's too short for me to tell you 

All the wonders I have seen 

Since I last heard life's reveille 

On old ocean's foaming green. 

Go your way and leave me resting

In my bare and ancient bed 

You can learn by Nature asking 

Wondrous histories of the dead. 

In spite of the astonishing activity he displayed in the pursuit of his ambitions, Ben Munson knew the value of relaxation; he knew how to play, even in the sense in which we moderns use the word. He was an indefatigable hunter and fisherman. In the hundreds of hard trips that he took over Texas and the Southwest he was often in grave personal danger, both from nature in the guise of storms and swollen creeks and rivers, and from the Indians who had little semblance of being civilized in the wilder parts of the State sixty or more years ago. He made these arduous journeys as often as possible by rail, but at the end of the line literally so, in many cases he struck out on horseback or in a buggy with single horse or team. None of this fazed him. He wrote humorous letters home of the dangers he ran, and belittled his hardships. 

Nowhere in his almost incredibly voluminous correspondence does he display a trace of that heavy-footed pedestrianism which would have been so easily excusable in a man of such varied and important activities. Even in his business letters he seems to delight in the frequent use of novel and picturesque words. He was full of fun; and like most successful men he was a private tease. 

These qualities of the adult man must have been in the essential character of the child. 

---

​CHAPTER 2 The World Into Which 

Ben Was Born

HAT WAS the state of the wider world into which William 

Benjamin Munson was born, on the seventh of January, 1846? How was it to be during the years in which he passed his early childhood?

In Europe, Russia groaned under Czars who let their samovars boil over with ideas of aggression against Turkey. The boatmen heaved-ho on the Volga in stolid anonymity, until they got a song about themselves. This may have aroused their self-consciousness to the point of making them want to rise out of the proletariat into the bourgeoisie - a desire Ben Munson would have been far more sympathetic with than the unconquerable political ague that shook the boatmen's grandchildren in 1918. 

In England, Lord Palmerston was boldly steering into an alliance with France to protect Turkey from the Bear. There had been a ghastly famine in Ireland, and the import duties on corn were removed in England during the year of Ben's birth. The 

British were annexing the Punjab in India, and growing mad enough in the process to do what they did to the Sepoys in 1857.

The French had been mourning the death of Napoleon for twenty-five years. Louis Philippe, the Citizen King, was sending his armies to conquer Algeria, and was getting prepared for his abdication in 1848. Louis Napoleon became president that same year, and just about the time little Ben was reaching school age

[8] 

the nephew of the great Corsican had himself plebiscited onto the Imperial throne as Napoleon the Third. 

Poor Maximilian, fourteen years old in 1846, had hardly heard of Mexico, and had no thought at all of being stood up and shot in that unpredictable country in the year Ben was hard at work in college, and there proving to his professors that they knew less about mathematics than he did. 

Carlotta, sound of mind and the idol of papa Leopold, scarcely could have been dreaming that one day France would crash in ignominy, and that gossip would connect her blood to the debacle in the person of one of the Third Republic's generals. 

Germany, as we have known it, was not yet Germany; merely separate states. But the terrible seed must already have been sown in that fertile soil, for it takes years to mature such twisted oaks of infamy as have grown up and shadowed into darkness the whole of Europe; whose poisonous pollen has been blown wide in our world, to fall on, and cause a smarting in Ben's own grandchildren. 

And in Ben's country of the United States, what was going on, and what had recently happened? 

William Henry Harrison had died early in office, in 1841; and the Whigs had been as politically disappointed in his successor, Vice President John Tyler, as Will Rogers said Herbert Hoover was with all the people he fished out of the lower reaches of the flooded Mississippi: when he had got them dried off, and on their feet, he found that they were all good Democrats at heart. 

Under Tyler the country had split wide open over the annexation of Texas; but a treaty had been signed with the new republic in 1844. The year of Ben's birth there was war with Mexico. That same year, at President Polk's request, Congress appropriated an amount of money for the prosecution of the war that would now provide for no more than a pair of trim little destroyers. Mexico ceded New Mexico and Upper California for a sum roughly equal

The World Into Which Ben Was Born 

[9] to the assessed valuation of Denison, Texas; and the great western movement was on. Iowa came into the Union in '46; the flood of 

Irish immigrants poured in to take over the police forces of the country and some of the fattest political jobs. Elias Howe patented the sewing machine. Gold was discovered in California just as young Munson was beginning to talk, and Wisconsin was admitted to the Union that same year of 1848. 

Everywhere people were talking of the slave question, and the Territory of Oregon was established without slavery. But what the people of the region of Ben's birthplace, Fulton County, Illinois, were saying about the slavery issue was entirely different from the talk to be heard a way off to the Southwest, in Grayson County, Texas, where Ben Munson was to live out his life, to prosper, and at last to die.

Although Ben's father was a down-Easterner, born and reared in Vermont, his mother was a Kentuckian, and Ben was named for her cousin (on her mother's side), the distinguished Judah Benjamin. This man was often referred to as "the brains of the 

Confederacy," and served Jefferson Davis' provisional govern ment as Secretary of War (1861-62) and Chief Secretary of State 

The proclivities of at least two of the Munson children — and we assume of them all, for there was no apparent dissension were Southern. The eldest son, J. T., who was five years Ben's senior, joined the Confederacy in 1864, and Ben himself wanted so desperately to further the Southern cause that he would slip out in the night and aid Confederate prisoners to escape. Little did the fifteen-year-old farm boy realize that, at the same time he was so determinedly and courageously releasing prisoners, an Alabamian was working his heart out to keep horses under the 

Southern Cavalrymen and clothes upon their backs; turning the broad hall of his home into a workshop where the local tailor was

[10] 

feverishly cutting out a gray uniform, and where a bevy of women were ceaselessly stitching, mending and knitting so that the boys who had been harbored for a day or two could go out to meet the enemy not only more rested than when they staggered in but definitely better covered. Among the women was Mary Ella 

Newton, a little blond girl of ten, who was making herself useful by keeping the ladies' needles threaded.

A brilliant and courageous lawyer-statesman casting his dice with the Confederacy; an Illinois boy setting Confederate prisoners free into the night with the excited whisper of "Go! Go now!"; a little girl in Alabama lending a hand with the mending of gray uniforms, and her father flattening himself against the mane of Jeff Davis, his huge iron-gray steed, running through and around 

Union soldiers with the whispered command, "Now go, Jeff! Go, go hard!" Stout hearts all, united in a lost cause; but stout hearts persist and meet again to make strong unions.

It appears that it is to his mother that Ben owed his fondness for study, his ambition for an education, and his taste for the sciences. It is true that his father furthered his desire for higher schooling, but when the time came for him to embark on a college career, he chose his mother's home state, and the Agricultural and Mechanical College at Lexington. Ben's mother had much to do with this choice. All of her children testified moreover that, as is not unexpected, it was their mother who nurtured and cultivated the gentler sides of their natures, especially their love for botany and flowers - a taste which Ben displayed throughout his life. 

But what was the condition of life and politics in the state where 

Ben was born? Illinois, with her earliest American settlers coming from the southern states, had been admitted to the Union in 1818. Her first school law was enacted in 1825, although it was not until Ben was nine years old (1855) that her second and existing school law was passed. An era of public improvements

The World Into Which Ben Was Born 

[11] was followed by the panic of '42 when the State Bank* suspended payments of specie. Two years before Ben's birth Joseph Smith, the founder of Mormonism, had been murdered by a wild mob in the Carthage prison. When Ben was two the Constitution of 

1848 abolished slavery and by the time he was four Congress ceded many public lands to clear the way for the building of the Illinois Central Railroad; in fact steam railroads as a means of transportation became one of Illinois' chief interests and by 1924 she ranked second only to Texas in this field. *A charter incorporating the State Bank of Illinois was passed in 1821 and continued for ten years. State Bank Bills were issued to $500,000 but depreciated to 

30 cents on the dollar. After the expiration of this charter another State Bank, with a capital of $1,500,000, was created. The principal bank was at Springfield with a branch at Vandalia. In 1842 both the State Bank at Springfield and the 

Bank of Illinois at Shawneetown became bankrupt, and the following year both were forced into liquidation and the State Bank at Springfield was given four years to wind up its affairs.

CHAPTER "By Their Fruits 

Ye Shall Know Them"

ULTON COUNTY, where Ben was born, lying as it does above a FULTON COUNTY, Where Ben was the state, and bounded for thirty-odd miles on the east by the Illinois River, was largely settled by New Englanders. This was true of the whole northern section, whereas, earlier, it was Southerners who had come into the lower part of the state. This difference in origins was responsible for the fact that slavery was still an active issue — although illegal - until the Civil War.

When we recall that early in the nineteenth century western Illinois was a wilderness, we have to admire the conditions of life that this Munson family made for themselves on the very outskirts of civilization. 

Ben's paternal grandfather was Theodore Munson, born in 

Dover, New Hampshire, in 1775. He married Lydia Philbrook, eight years his junior, in the place of his birth, when he was twenty-nine. A lumberman in New Hampshire, he became a tradesman in Illinois, where he removed with all his family, two daughters and four sons, in 1831. Theodore Munson died in 

Rushville, Illinois, in 1839, at the age of sixty-four. His wife, Ben's grandmother, outlived him twenty-seven years, dying at Macomb,

Illinois, in 1866, the year young Munson entered college in Kentucky.

The second child of this couple was William Munson, born in

"By Their Fruits Ye Shall Know Them" 

[13] Wakefield, New Hampshire, in 1808. He was Ben's father. From the genealogical papers it appears that William Munson, at the age of twenty-one, left New Hampshire two years before his father moved west. It seems likely that William Munson went out by way of Kentucky and there met his future wife, Maria Linley, in the town of Tolesboro. At any rate, five years after his father had settled in Illinois, we find William Munson back in Tolesboro, where he married Maria Linley, April 24, 1836. They were a devoted, happy couple and achieved a singular, almost psychic union.

William Munson and his wife, Maria, settled in Fulton County not far from the town of Astoria. He was a prosperous farmer. 

To this marriage were born six children, the fourth of whom was William Benjamin, the subject of this biography. The three earlier children were: Louisa Elizabeth, born in 1840; Joseph 

Theodore, 1841; and Thomas Volney, 1843. Both of these brothers shared actively in the later life of their younger brother Ben. 

The two youngest children were girls – Tryphena Maria, born in 1848, and Mary Genevra, born in 1853. 

In 1848, two years after Ben's birth, his father succumbed to the lure of the great Southwest, and Texas in particular. He sold the land on which he had expended so much time and labor in clearing and cultivating, and prepared to set out. We can imagine the excitement of this man, his wife, and the three older children at the idea of removing to the state that had just surrendered its sovereignty of a Republic for a safer but less independent place in the Union; a region where the Indians were still untamed, and where the memory of the Alamo and San Jacinto was still green in the minds and hearts of the people who called themselves Texans.

But for William Munson and his wife this long and arduous journey was destined to be postponed for three decades. His health

DOORWAY OF OLD HOMESTEAD IN ILLINOIS

"By Their Fruits Ye Shall Know Them" [ 15 ] 15] suddenly broke, and he had to give up all notion of leaving the country where he was established. It is a tribute to his courage, and doubtless to his wife's courage also, that he bought a virgin tract of timber, 320 acres of it, adjoining the partially improved farm that he had just sold, and set once more to work at the job of felling trees and preparing his land for field crops, and garden and orchard. He built a new house for his family, and constructed it of the logs he himself had hewn.

There was an abundance of hazelnuts which the family gathered in the autumn, and which they consumed during the long winter evenings before the open fireplace. Ben helped at this task of harvesting nuts, although to a young child, such an undertaking is nothing more than great fun. He also, with a young boy's love of sweets, haunted the sugar camp that was set up during earliest spring in the great groves of maples. It was these trees that supplied the Munson family with syrup and sugar for a whole year, until such time as the sap would run again. 

Ben's mother, a really remarkable woman, although born to the more refined and civilized life of Kentucky, put her shoulder stoutly to the task of buoying up a temporarily ailing husband, attending to a family of growing children, and yet finding time to see to it that she had the finest orchard in all that part of Illinois. 

All the better-known fruits grew in this orchard: pears, cherries, apples, quince, plums, grapes, and peaches. It was her job, too, to see that such of these fruits as would keep were stored away safely in the big cellar for the winter's use, and until spring and early summer marked the ripening once more of the earlier varieties. 

The closest good market was Chicago, 150 miles away to the northeast, and almost everything that the family needed was produced on their farm. Since they owned cows they had all the milk and cream and milk-products that they wanted. One of Ben's

[16] 

favorite tricks when he was a boy was to get a big red apple, dip it in the heavy, rich cream and eat it, of course without the peeling. 

For this he was spanked.

On the farm there were all kinds of livestock, including sheep. 

They also had a great variety of fowls, with turkeys and guineas contributing their special voices to the more ordinary farm hubbub. The proclivities that Ben's mother had for housekeeping were further shown in the great quantities of preserves and jellies that she put up; by the bushels of fruit and vegetables that she canned; and by the fruits that she dried. From the sheep that the family owned, Maria Munson spun yarn; and Ben recalled that on many an evening the sound of the spinning-wheel was a droning and sweet music in the house. The mother herself then wove the yarn into cloth, and this cloth provided the woolen clothes for the whole family.

Ben's father made a further contribution to keeping the family clothed and shod by the dexterous use he made of a shoemaker's last and a few odd awls. With this last and these tools he managed to keep the children's shoes in repair until he made one of his infrequent trips to Chicago, where he was in the habit of hauling his surplus supply of bacon. There were evidently some sharp traders in the livestock business already in Chicago at that early day, for Ben's farmer-father was paid only three or four cents a pound for what undoubtedly was a superior brand of bacon.

The flowers that grew in such abundance about the farm not only served as decoration for the interior of the house, but also as specimens for the mother's early lessons in botany which she gave her children.

The future proved her a fine teacher; for so successfully did she nurture the inquisitiveness of the young minds in her family that this intellectual curiosity continued to flourish and expand long

"By Their Fruits Ye Shall Know Them" [17] after the physical processes had slackened and begun to wither. 

The boys particularly always regarded Life with the incredulous excitement of the scientist. The second son, Thomas Volney, became so absorbed in his love of plants that upon reaching maturity he specialized in viticulture, and reached great distinction in this field. Maria Linley herself lived long enough to see the 

French Government, in the year 1888, send a special envoy, Monsieur Pierre Viala, to "Vol's" home to confer upon him the Legion of Honor,* and the title "Chevalier du Mérite Agricole." Ah, how

Maria Linley would have blessed the French school children who, in answer to the question, "who saved the vineyards of France?" would chorus in their piping treble: “Monsieur T. V. Munson de la ville de Denison, dans le Texas!"

Ben dearly loved this brother, and their deep attachment, as well as the effect of the mother's teachings on both boys, is clearly illustrated in the simple dignity of Vol's funeral service. He wrote his own funeral oration, a tribute to knowledge, which was read by R. S. Legate, a warm personal friend, and used in place of the conventional funeral service. The final two paragraphs adequately mirror the beauty of the whole. Listen well, for they are the words of Time. A mother is teaching her child; the child is a splendid man; the man has passed into the Future. 

Now may good will, peace and good works abide among you. Mourn not for me. Only those of faith mourn disconsolately, for belief lacks knowledge, and this causes sorrow. Return to your pursuits as joyfully as though you had planted a seed that will, perennially, spring up in beautiful trees and flowers. Be always as beautiful as trees, and flowers, and loving birds, and you will be happy. 

If you must call Nature God, then I am a son of God, and you are all my brothers and sisters, and you must, also, be sons and daughters of God. In this sense was Jesus a Son of God, and in no other, and in this sense is He our elder brother. Then, all should live as brothers and sisters, and not as * The second Membership ever granted to an American, the first Membership being granted to Thomas Alva Edison in 1878.

THE THREE MUNSON BROTHERS IN MIDDLE LIFE: 

T. V., J. T. AND W. B.

"By Their Fruits Ye Shall Know Them" 

[19] hating enemies. To bless each other as brothers and sisters is, then, to be blessed of God. In no other way can this blessing be had. 

Farewell. 

Ben insisted upon writing and himself delivering the final tribute at the grave of this beloved brother. His family and friends protested: "No, Ben; that's too much to expect of any man. You're so deeply affected by his death, you'd break down and never get through your speech." "If Ingersoll could do it for his brother," replied Ben, “I can certainly do it for mine!"

And he did it. In a clear, firm voice he spoke the following words: 

Dearest brother, we now surrender you back to the bosom of the great universe into whose mysteries during life you so loved to delve. Your life has been an inspiration to your family and friends, and will be an example to all as the happy results of a life well spent. 

You fell asleep like a child on its mother's bosom, without a struggle, your every feature indicating the satisfaction of having earned the plaudit of your fellow men. "Well done, thou good and faithful servant." 

Your heart was loving and tender as the flowers you grew, your resolution in the discharge of duty as strong as the oak and firm as steel; your energy was tireless, your patience most wonderful; your character and conduct spotless and clean; your love of nature was surpassed only by your love of man; you found your greatest happiness in the bestowal of lasting benefits upon your fellow men.

You will live in the moral fibre of your posterity, in the impress your life has made upon those who knew you, in the finer fruit of the vines you created, and in the wider intelligence you fostered. 

And so, in his own courage, Ben paid Vol the highest possible tribute.

The botanical teachings of Maria Linley fostered a love for trees and plants in J.T. too, and successive generations have laughed over the story of this crusty old bachelor's painstakingly digging borers out of a tree on the parking of the Episcopal Church. The young minister of the church was new and unacquainted with

[20] 

the fact that the old gentleman automatically doctored not only every tree and shrub he saw needing it, but particularly the "Episcopal trees," for he resided in the rooming house adjacent to the church, and he considered every tree and shrub within sight of this domain to be his own particular little parish. The minister, somewhat alarmed at seeing a stranger (J.T. was not an Episcopalian; in fact it is doubtful that at that time he darkened the doors of any church) digging in the trees, walked up behind him and said rather sharply: "What are you doing there?" J.T., not caring particularly to whom he was speaking, snapped back: "It seems to me that any damned fool could see what I'm doing!"

The same varieties of flowers that grew around the Illinois farmhouse also supplied rich pasturage for the bees, and thus honey and wax were added to the family's store of necessities, and increased still further that sense of independence that the family enjoyed. 

In all this development of family life, and in the life that the children lived with the home as its hub, too much emphasis cannot be laid on the role their remarkable mother played. There was never an idle moment for her. Between the more pressing needs of this rugged and strength-taking life, there were the children to train, to talk to, to encourage in this taste and discourage in that. 

Into the training of each one she put a special ardor and an understanding of that child's nature. The result was that each one grew up with, and kept on developing, a strong individuality; and 

Maria Linley's efforts were richly rewarded in the character and accomplishments of these children, especially of the three boys. 

There is ample proof that she must have encouraged in Ben that taste for mathematics and pure knowledge that showed in him at a very early age. This proof, however, is more negative than positive, in that it resides in this: on a farm and woodland place of 320 acres there would always have been plenty that she,

"By Their Fruits Ye Shall Know Them" [21] as a busy and alert-eyed mother and helpmeet, could have found for each of the children to do. If she had not been the extraordinary woman that she was, she could have kept young Ben at tasks far removed from mathematics and philosophy. But the taste for both these subjects grew in him, and it is thus certain that they flourished with the mother's encouragement. 

There was a neighborhood school that the Munson children attended. While Ben was still in this little country school, and long before he had completed the all-too-limited course of study that it offered, he had developed such a passion for mathematics and astronomy that he was already busy gauging the distance from the earth to the sun and the moon from observations of their relative positions. It is no wonder then that, after he had finished at the country school, his father sent him to college for one year at Abington, Illinois. This was a great satisfaction to Ben's mother; and it was a source of great disappointment to her that her husband could no longer afford, after one year, to send Ben away for further educational advantages. 

William Munson thought he saw a way out of the dilemma, and he hit upon a plan that would prove the mettle of his sons: he gave to each one of the three boys acreage out of his big farm and told them that, from then on, they need work only for themselves, and that they might do what they willed with the fruits of their labors. 

Ben seized on this opportunity. He planted his plot in wheat, and secured for himself a teaching position in the country for the winter. With the savings that he laid away from his teaching, and with the proceeds from the wheat which he harvested and sold the following summer, he set his heart resolutely on a real college education to begin the next autumn. With the little capital that he had collected he chose the Agricultural and Mechanical branch of the University of Kentucky, and set out for Lexington in the early fall of 1866.

CHAPTER College-An Adventure in Living 

EN'S DEPARTURE for Lexington marks his first real entrance into Bthe competitive world of men, money and ideas. From the very first step in what begins now as his exciting and successful progress through life he conducted himself in a distinguished manner. 

The circumstances of his life in Lexington were not easy. He no doubt had saved enough money to live more comfortably in Lexington than he did live, but he was conservative and did not believe in putting himself without emergency resources.

T.V. and Ben were always very close. The former joined Ben in college, and the two boys set themselves the not-easy task of keeping bachelors' quarters. They kept their own rooms, made their beds, and even did their own cooking. Ben reported that their diet could have been more varied, since neither one was an experienced cook, but it seems to have been sufficiently stable to keep them going. They had bread and butter, mush and milk, potatoes, and — when they felt like splurging a little — meat. By being careful and cutting corners, they were able to keep their board down to one dollar a week for each of them.

In addition to the work they had to do in their own quarters, they were required by the College - it was Government-sponsored — to work two hours a day for the school. If this and their classwork and study did not keep them busy enough, they were allowed to work as much as ten hours for the College on Saturday, and for this labor they received twenty cents an hour. It is certain that both brothers availed themselves of this from time to time,

[23] because both were industrious, and because they felt that they could use the extra money.

Very early Ben brought himself to the attention of the professors and the President of the College. Both Ben and his brother had had teaching experience, and this quickly made them leaders in their classes. As a matter of fact, their proficiency was such that they soon received a flattering offer from the school authorities: they were told that they could teach two hours a day in the College, and receive forty cents an hour for this work. Of course they accepted, but continued nevertheless to put in the extra hours of work on Saturday at twenty cents per hour.

They were seriously bent on having an education. The twoweeks vacation which all the students were to have for Christmas, the two Munson boys employed in cutting wood. They were paid a dollar a cord for this, and each boy cut two cords a day. In this way they added almost thirty dollars apiece to the sum of their assets. Moreover, their finances were prospering in other ways. 

Before they had come away to school that first early autumn they had planted another crop of wheat. This ripened and returned a good yield, bringing a fair price, so that when they returned to school for the second year they felt that they were in easy enough circumstances to board. This they did, and acquired more time for study and reading.

Encouraged in his studies by the extra time that their lack of household duties afforded them, Ben decided that he would finish the four-year course in three years. He distinguished himself particularly in mathematics, and never went unprepared to class. 

It appears not to have strained him to do the senior and junior mathematics in one year, for he stood near the head of the class. 

During this last year there occurred an incident that was characteristic of him, both as to his moral courage, and to his exactness of information and his own confidence in this exactness.

[24] 

A student had put a problem on the blackboard and the solution had been accepted by the professor - a man of resounding reputation in his field, and one who had written and published a complete course in mathematics, running the gamut from arithmetic to astronomy. Ben, summoning his courage, called the attention of the professor to the fact that there was an error in the solution; and when he did so he was astonished to see that the teacher had not been aware of the mistake. The gentleman kept his wits about him and did not give way to a burst of temper. 

Instead he looked gravely at the problem for several minutes. "We will take up this same problem tomorrow," he said. 

At the end of the class-period Ben was a hero. The students gathered around him in wonder. "Didn't you know," they said, "that you've just about knocked the old professor out? That is his solution to the problem, and it has been copied and accepted as correct by mathematics pupils for the past fifteen years?"

They went on to tell him that no solution of this problem appeared in the text, and that this was the first time the correct solution had ever been given. Ben began to wonder. Had he perhaps done wrong to call the attention of the man to the error?

But, anyhow, he had been true to himself, and that was to count then, and later.

The professor had the last and gracious word. The next day, and before the whole class, he admitted that the heretoforeaccepted solution of the problem was wrong, and that Ben's was correct. It would be difficult to say which man was the greater hero.

On another occasion Ben took issue with his professor on the soundness of the nebular hypothesis, and the professor later acknowledged that young Munson's argument was unanswerable. 

Ben showed that the nebular hypothesis did account for and

[ ] involved the revolution of the various planets and satellites on their axes, and that this axis revolution simply represented the difference in velocity between the portion of the ring thrown off from the revolving sun or nebulous planet representing the extreme outside, and the velocity of that portion of the ring which represented the interior side; and as these rings were of vast thickness this difference in velocity was maintained in the sphere by the more rapid-moving particles occupying the outside of the equatorial regions of the sphere, and by the slower-moving particles taking their position in the interior of the sphere, so that the velocity of each particle could be maintained; and this difference of velocity between the outside and inside particles of the sphere was satisfied by the revolution of the body on its axis. The professor was forced to admit that the argument was unanswerable, and that the nebular hypothesis was a vindication rather than a contradiction of the fact of the revolution of the various planets and satellites upon their axes.

It must not be supposed that all of young Munson's time was taken up with the sciences, working out of classes, and teaching. 

Ben, even as a young man, had broad interests, and he concerned himself with the more important questions of civilization. The proof of this lies largely in two manuscript papers. The first is his affirmative argument and rebuttal in the debate on the question: "Are the human races of a common origin?" The second paper is his affirmative in the debate: "Would the annexation of Cuba to the United States be beneficial?" Both of these papers are rich mines, and since the firsthand source material for these early years is otherwise rather scanty, it will be well to quote at length from these two manuscripts. They are written in his fine, clear hand. 

Some of the arguments depend for their effectiveness on the inadequacy of the "negative"; there are enthusiastic, boyish assumptions.

[ ] 

At first thought we might be led to believe that an argument on the kinship of the human race would lie comfortably within the realms of Ben's well-loved sciences, but, as the reader will see, the debater branched out, and crushed his opponent with every means in his power. From the argument on the human race: these problems, like many which have preceded them, are gradually being overcome by the victorious march of science as, guided by the light of revelation, she presses boldly on in the search of truth.

This passage is interesting because of the statement that science is guided by the light of revelation, a point which he leans on later in the discussion, and which would appear to place the writing of this paper prior to the time when doubts as to revealed religion and the mysteries seem to have assailed him. allow me to state that in affirming all mankind to be of a common origin, I by no means assert that all are fitted for the same social and politi- · · cal relations. He who argues that all men should occupy the same civil and social position because they are of common origin, is as unreasonable as he who should contend that the Bulldog should be used as a fox-hound, or that the surly Mastiff should take the place of the lap-dog, because they are of common stock. No! the circumstances under which they have been placed have prepared each for a particular sphere in life. Then let it be understood that this question is entirely independent of political issues, and we shall enter upon its consideration. 

Ben views the problem as being divided into two grand divisions: the one as a purely scientific problem, and the other as a matter of revelation, namely the Bible. though the power of God is infinite, a rigid economy is everywhere displayed. In no case has there been a greater expenditure of power than was necessary to accomplish a given result. If it can be shown that the copper color, high cheek bones, and savage character of the American, and the fair complexion, fine sensibilities and noble intellect of the Caucasian, are all reconcilable with the doctrine of common origin, then will my proposition be established. . . . How unlike the original types are the countless varieties of dahlias which now bloom in your beautiful gardens.

[27] This latter was a fine sop to the "ladies and gentlemen" of Lexington, and there is no doubt but they were convinced that he was referring to their particular gardens. Audiences are like that. 

Place the Lap-dog and the stately Newfoundland side by side, measure their facial angles, compare their cranial measurements, and tell me if the differences are not far greater than those existing between any two human beings on earth . . . dispositions and habits of life are widely removed from each other. The characteristics of the surly Bulldog, the sportsman's pointer, and the shepherd's companion are much more distinct than those of the Negro, Indian, and White man; yet all naturalists agree that all the varieties of the dog come from a common stock. 

This was a telling blow for his side, because the "Blue Grass" was dog-conscious. They knew what pointers were. They would have liked it better if he had spoken of fox-hounds again. The next statement probably did not unduly excite the Kentuckians, but it has interest for us. ... ... No discerning eye can fail to distinguish between an Englishman and a German, an Irishman and a Frenchman yet these nations have been distinct but a few centuries. They all follow like habits of life, they live in similar climates and have the same religion. If nations in similar climates, possessing equal intelligence and the same religion, can undergo such marked modifications in a few hundred years, and that too while continual intercourse is going on between them; is it not reasonable to suppose that during the long periods of time and under the influence of entirely different climates, religions and habits of life, far greater variations would be the result? It would be foolish to suppose otherwise.

This last statement, flung out with boyish certainty, is sure to have cooked Mr. C. Blakemore's goose. No polite audience wants to be guilty of a foolish supposition."

The Great Celtic, Teutonic, and Slavonic waves of emigration flowed from the tablelands of central Asia and spread themselves over the whole of Europe. Comparative philology leads to the same conclusion. 

It is not certain how the audience reacted to this last thrust. At best it seems to have been a bit below the belt. Even assuming the

[ ] 

culture then existing in Kentucky, and the presence in the debating hall of a sprinkling of professors, it is safe to say that most of the audience did not even know what he was talking about. 

Though they are bound to have been impressed with Ben's erudition, doubtless they squirmed in their seats. ... Climate has an effect upon the physical features, and it has a selecting influence . . . from a colony of people placed in an unfavorable climate, those most unsusceptible to modifications would sicken and die from being in a climate to which they were not adapted. 

If Mr. Blakemore asked why the Indians of North America were not notably lighter than the natives of Central and South America he was well answered in a passage from Ben's rebuttal. ... The color of the Esquimaux can be easily explained, for in the first place they are by no means so dark as is generally supposed. Sir John 

Richardson, whose veracity cannot be questioned, asserts that when freed from the smoke and dirt with which they are usually encrusted, they are almost white. The slight differences which exist between them and the 

Europeans is attributable entirely to their manner of life, and the chafing icy blasts to which they are exposed.

Ben re-enforced his linguistic argument. the affinity of their languages shows that all the various tribes from Guatemala to Upper Canada, and from the Atlantic to the Pacific, are of common stock. But more than this they are clearly proven to be of common origin with the inhabitants of the Old World. Of about five hundred words discovered among the savage languages of America nearly two hundred are identical with words of the Eastern Continent; and of these upwards of one hundred coincide with words found in Asia and Australia; the remainder being about equally distributed between Europe and Africa, showing conclusively that all races of man are related. 

From this point on, Mr. Blakemore's decision to remain in the running must have been dictated solely by a code of personal honor and a feeling of loyalty for Ole Kentuck. He knew in his heart that he was vanquished. And he was yet to have flung reverently at him the one great authority that no one could deny: the

[ ] argu- Bible. If Blakemore and Munson met in later life Ben, like the honest man he was, would have retracted much of his later ment - - probably to the confusion of his erstwhile opponent. 

But Mr. Blakemore was to have a few moments more in which to cling to his tenuous position. 

The great temple of Balenquè so closely corresponds with that of Boro-Bodo on the Island of Java as to place beyond reasonable debate the common origin and purpose of both. Besides a complete coincidence in architecture, the walls of both are adorned with mytho-hieroglyphical tablets in which the head of the Asiatic Elephant is conspicuous; and in the sanctuaries of both the god Buddha is represented in colossal dignity. 

Though the "honorable opponent" may have bridled at the suggestion that his debate was unreasonable, he had to sit and take it; and he could not stop his ears to the good lesson that was coming.

The moral sensibilities, taste, faith, conscience, love and devotion, though sometimes obscured by sin, are nevertheless the occupants of every human heart. The wisest sage that earth can boast has not one faculty or sensibility which is not possessed by the meanest savage. The universal belief in a superior power and sacrificial offerings for the atonement of sin, is enough of itself to prove the unity of the human races. . . Turning to the Bible.. ... 

Clench your fists, Mr. Blakemore, and if you have some ammonia salts concealed about your person now is the time to whisk them out and under your nose, for you are about to receive a broadside. that Book of Books and fount of knowledge, does not its radiant beam shed a ray of light upon this important problem? Happily God has not left man's erring judgment to decide so vital a question. The declarations of holy-writ in decided terms attest the great truth which is affirmed before you tonight. Beginning with the Mosaic account of creation, we find only a single pair mentioned as the parents of the whole human family. 

But my opponent may contend that there might have been others of whom no account was given. But, says Moses, Adam called his wife's name Eve because she was the mother of all living. Who can assert in defiance of

[30] 

this positive declaration that the human family is not a unit? It would be accusing God's inspired pensman of giving an imperfect account of creation, if not of a palpable falsehood. . . . All races of men are the descendants of Noah. I ask the gentleman of the negative to reconcile his view of the question with these declarations, Genesis VII, 21st verse. . . . Upon this question the New Testament is no less decisive than the Old. Acts 

XVII, 26th verse, Paul declares that "God hath made of one blood all the nations of men for to dwell on all the face of the earth. 

It is safe to assume that by this time Blakemore had forgotten not only his honor but also his loyalty to his alma mater, and had fallen in a conquered faint to the floor. Ammonia and the more gentlemanly virtues would have been unavailing in the face of such arguments. But the pitiless "affirmative" was not disposed to let the "negative's" form lie in peace.

In attempting to dodge my argument drawn from a comparison of European nations, my opponent asserts that they are an amalgamation of several primitive types. Thus, ladies and gentlemen, making you out a set of mongrels.

Whether, in this particular debate, the audience was to decide the merits of the arguments is not known; but if other, appointed judges had had any thought of giving the decision to the "negative" they would have been shouted down in violent acclamation for the "affirmative." 

If Blakemore really put his foot into the gluey treachery of that word "amalgamation" - and there is no reason to doubt Ben's quotation - he was guilty of one of the most monumental faux pas that ever a debater made. Where on the whole face of the earth could the allegation of mongrelism have been more instantly insulting than in Lexington, Kentucky-the very center of the tradition of blue grass, of unadulterated mint juleps from the best Bourbon, of pure-bred women, and thoroughbred horses?

In the warmly enthusiastic crowd that surged about the victor only the closest could hear the final words.

[31] Lastly he attempts to carry his point by accusing me of a strange relationship. Let us compare our doctrines in this respect. My opponent asserts that the Caucasian is the only cosmopolite, and that he is but the mongrel resultant of several distinct types. If mongrelism is the cause of his being cosmopolite, it follows that there must be in his veins the blood of the Icelander and the Japanese, else he could not endure the torrid sun and polar snows. But again, this so-called mongrel race is the highest and noblest of all mankind; then must the gentleman be an advocate of the doctrine of miscegenation.

So much for the debate on the races of mankind. On the back of this argument and rebuttal, written in Ben's hand, and bearing no quotation marks, is a poem that is too good not to be put down here.

While we talk of Woodland's fading Trees 

Remember those which still their verdure hold The pine, the spruce which checks the cutting breeze 

Of hoary-headed savage winter cold.

How cheerful, when all else is brown and curled 

Do these proud kings majestic look, Symbols of that eternal, blissful world 

Which is revealed in God's own glorious Book. 

As these of forest life are bright ensigns 

When all around them seems so cold and dead, So man's majestic, buoyant spirit shines

When life and all its worldly hopes have fled. 

While nature speaks thus pleasantly and mild, With accents sweet to merry Woodland boys, Still other scenes more boisterous, gay or wild, Add much to swell her many welcome joys. 

Each morn the bugle's shrill and stirring notes 

Relieve each weary mattress of its load, And thus the health and strength of youth promotes 

And renders smooth the student's rugged road. 

The college bell with old familiar voice 

Sends loud and long the welcome breakfast call, Then many empty stomachs glad rejoice

As quick they march towards the dining hall.

[32] 

In the second debate, Ben shared with J. W. Ogden (or Ogdon) the affirmative of the question: Would the annexation of Cuba to the United States be beneficial? The negative was represented by J. Leet and D. H. Clark. Ben's argument deserves to be quoted in full; it is a document.

The question before us for discussion tonight is one which, for many years, has attracted the attention of our statesmen; but never has it been more interesting and important than now. While Spain is convulsed by a violent revolutionary spirit, and while her colonies, who have long borne with impatience her despotic yoke, are riotous for liberty and independence; the easy terms upon which the island of Cuba can be obtained makes the question doubly pertinent.

The question is, would the annexation of Cuba to the U.S. be beneficial? Would the acquisition of an island unparalleled in beauty, fertility, and the value of its productions, and occupying a position which completely commands the navigation of the Gulf of Mexico, be advantageous to the U.S.? Would the removal of the tyrannical chains which now bind the inhabitants of Cuba, and the substitution in their stead of the blessings of republican institutions and privileges, be beneficial to the dejected and down-trodden people of that lovely isle?

We answer most unhesitatingly in the affirmative, and in so answering, we believe that we speak the sentiments of this intelligent audience. As the question is stated in simple and unequivocal language, a special definition of terms may be omitted; yet it may be well first to inquire to what parties the word “beneficial” applies. As we are discussing the questions as citizens of the U.S., our first inquiry is, would the annexation of Cuba be beneficial to us as a nation? Secondly, as the people of that island would, in case of an annexation, become a part of our nation, we must inquire whether such a course would promote their prosperity. As to whether it would be beneficial to the third party, Spain, we are not concerned; but will leave that part of the question for her to discuss. Let it also be distinctly understood that we do not argue that the annexation is practicable. We do not propose to prove that the annexation could be effected at a cost which would make it a profitable investment; but we do propose and expect to prove that, if it could be effected without expense or at a reasonable cost, it would be beneficial to Cuba, as well as to ourselves. 

My first argument shall be based upon the importance of the acquisition viewed in the light of its commercial and agricultural resources. Although

College [33] 

An Adventure in Living we now possess a vast extent of territory, inexhaustible in its sources of wealth; although the Texan who enjoys the golden fruit of the orange as he reclines beneath its pleasant shade on the banks of the Rio Grande, and the hardy Minnesota trapper who ensnares the beaver on Lake Superior's icy shore, breathe a common air; although the merchant of San Francisco, and the fisherman on the coast of Maine, separated by a rich and varied landscape of three thousand miles, own and honor our common flag; yet, we need the ever-blooming island of Cuba with her tropical products to complete our list, and place us in that perfectly independent position which would satisfy our ideal of a mighty republic. The coffee, the mahogany, and the rich and various fruits of Cuba are staples which we will and must have. 

Then would it not be wise in us to place that valuable island under our own control, and thereby reap the rich harvests which would follow from such a course; instead of depending upon the caprice of a foreign power with whom we may, at any time, be involved in war. Nor is the independence the only advantage which we would enjoy. The enormous duties which now oppress our laboring classes, and discourage the producers of Cuba would be removed, and thus both parties would be greatly benefited. 

The policy which would reject such an advantage would be like that of the farmer who, lacking but one small though necessary element in the formation of a grand estate, should blindly refuse to supply the deficiency. Considering the island in the light of its military position, as a key to the naval operations of the Great Gulf, the importance of its possession becomes apparent to all.

The increasing animosity existing between the republican and monarchical principles of government may, before we are aware, ripen into a bloody and decisive conflict. Hence, political foresight demands that we avail ourselves of that prize which, from its easy defence, its safe harbors and its commanding position, would, in case of a conflict, become a most painful thorn in the bosom of our enemies.

While other nations less powerful than ourselves possess islands and dependencies in all quarters of the globe, shall we take no share in so essential an element of national greatness? Shall we allow a weak and diseased 

European monarchy longer to possess an island which lies upon our very borders, and which by nature was intended as our adjunct? No; such would be the policy of a drone, and not that of a true worker in the great cause of democratic and national advancement. 

Our next argument shall be based upon the importance of extending the influence of democratic institutions and principles of government. The more extended the scope of democratic rule, and the wider the diffusion

[34] 

of its glorious principles, the greater will be the check upon the oppressive and unjust measures of monarchical powers. The liberal policy which is now adopted in England and other European powers, is in a great measure due to the influence of the United States. Then, whatever will tend to increase her influence and extend her power, will not only be beneficial to ourselves, but it will be a blessing to the world at large. 

But my opponents may contend that our own people enjoy less liberty at the present time than the subjects of our monarchical neighbors. [An obvious reference to the troubles of reconstruction in the South.] I grant that the American Eagle has, in a fit of madness, torn and disfigured her own nest, and stained her talons in the blood of her most likely nestlings. 

Yet I know of no way so well calculated to remove the foul blot as to send the once glorious bird out in quest of game, and thus calm the feelings and unite the interests of her irritated and distempered brood. 

It may also be argued by my worthy opponents that the extension of territory has, in general, been prejudicial to the interests of other nations. 

In answer to such an objection, I would remark that while many nations have been ruined by a sudden and premature extension of territory, yet, on the contrary, a natural and gradual growth has, in almost every instance, proved highly beneficial. Where would have been the greatness of the 

Roman name had it not been for her wise policy in extending her territory? It is true she fell at last; not from the magnitude of her dominions, but from the internal weakness which is universally begotten by age. For nations, like everything else, must pass through all stages of life. Born in pain and blood, in childhood pure and healthy, in manhood strong and vigorous, though often scarred by wickedness and folly, in old age diseased and tottering; and at last, when their days are numbered, they suddenly fall into an unavoidable grave. Then, from the mere fact of a nation's fall, we must not conclude that it was due to any particular cause. To demonstrate the beneficial results which follow from a judicious extension of territory, we have but to compare the governments of England and France. 

The former, originally consisting of the small island of Great Britain, now commands the respect and admiration of the world; and it may be truly said that the sun never sets on her dominions. While the latter, which originally enjoyed far superior advantages, by adopting a narrow contracted policy, is now upon the very verge of destruction, having no safety valve by which she can be relieved of her riotous and dangerous overplus of population.

Considering the question with respect to the interests of Cuba, it seems almost unnecessary to discuss a proposition so self-evident. The willingness

[35] which the Cubans have ever manifested to throw off the Spanish yoke, and unite with us in enjoying the blessings of democratic institutions, shows beyond a doubt that such a union would promote their prosperity. Their interests are wholly American, and directly opposed to those of Europe. 

Hence, their normal condition is that of an adjunct to some American power. Cuba must ever look to the U.S. for her supplies of bread, and here she can ever find a ready market for her surplus products. In short, her every interest demands that she be annexed to the United States. 

But the Cubans have motives far more weighty than commercial interest prompting them to break the Spanish chains. Never was a land so rich and so beautiful exposed to so many humiliations and bitter wrongs. Again and again her noble patriots have been either totally disregarded, or answered by additional insult. The time has come at last when such abuse could no longer be borne. Like true heroes, the Cubans are now asserting their rights by force of arms. The Spanish authorities are already trembling under the assaults of their musketry. Have we no Lafayette who is willing to employ his talents in the cause of Cuba's freedom?

Annexation to the U.S. is their most cherished hope. That, and that alone, can save them from horrid slavery and utter ruin. 

There are one or two important points to signalize in these two debates. The first is the versatility of interest, and the second is the solid information that backed up this versatility. Had Ben's life later taken a different turn, it is safe to say that he would have been a very fine representative of that old-time scholar of broad interests who was not unfamiliar in the heyday of the nineteenth century. In those days a man, to be considered educated, had to know, and know well, a great many different and often unrelated things. Ben kept this extraordinary versatility all his life, but as he grew older the accent shifted; and it is precisely this shifting of accent that marks him as being a man pre-eminently of his time. 

The library facilities back at his home in Illinois could have been no more than meagre; and it seems reasonably safe to assume that they were hardly full in the college he attended. There were, of course, scattered over the pre-War South, a great many fine private libraries. These were gathered to suit the tastes of the

[36] 

collector, and also in virtue of the very sound idea that if children do not receive a large portion of their education at home, they will never be able to get it anywhere else. Only the changing notions of what constitutes an education can be urged against this now almost untenably esoteric doctrine.

The very fact of his undertaking to sustain a side in these two debates proves the solidity of his versatility. He was a mathematics and engineering major; but in the debate on races he evidences familiarity with archaeology, anthropology, linguistics, history, and the Bible. That some of his information is faulty, incomplete, or incorrect, is beside the point. We know, or can know, much more about those subjects now than was known then. The freedom with which he cites authorities (not quoted directly in the excerpts given here) shows a background of familiarity. He puts out all this information with a certain affection, not at all in the stilted, cut-and-dried fashion of a college boy who has "read up" on a subject, to be prepared on a particular occasion. 

As indicated in some of the comments on excerpts from his race argument, one of the most interesting features of his speech is his reliance on the Bible, especially the Old Testament, as a source of historical truth. We already know that, at some time after these two debates, he pulled completely away from sectarianism, and was no longer able to accept what Christians think of as revealed truth; for he was unable to reconcile this truth with scientific fact as he knew it. But, in his mature life, he recognized that the 

Church was on the side of orderly, responsible moral conduct, and he made regular contributions of money to all the leading denominations. Furthermore, if we apply to Ben Munson's life the tests by which we ordinarily establish the Christianity of a life, we find that he measured up. The virtues are there; the name that would identify them is unimportant. 

Ben's arguments for the annexation of Cuba were so well

MORRISON COLLEGE BUILDING, IN WHICH THE COMMENCEMENT OF 1869 WAS HELD 

(Courtesy of the University of Kentucky)

[38] 

worked out that they have been quoted in full. In them he showed a knowledge of past and contemporary history, plain and commercial geography. He showed a sound understanding of national psychology when he suggested that, if we were to be drawn into a war with Spain through our efforts to free Cuba from the Spanish yoke and bring it into the American Union, such a war would have a salutarily unifying effect on our country - riven as it was immediately after the Civil War, when the embittered South was struggling up under the scourge of poverty and the abuses of Reconstruction.

There is interest and irony attached to his one mention of Texas in the Cuba speech. It is interesting because it indicates that his mind may already have been dwelling on Texas. He remembered, of course, that his whole family, back in the forties, had almost gone there. Ben's mention of the State is a purely romantic one, because it is seriously to be doubted if there was much "pleasant shade" beneath orange trees in the Rio Grande Valley.

The irony of this reference to the State springs from the fact that the Rio Grande Valley is about the only section of Texas where he did not operate. Millions of acres in the north, the east, the central, the southwest, and the Panhandle sections of the State passed through his hands; but none in what is now spoken of as 

The Valley. 

The reference to Minnesota is prophetic, for, later on, Ben cleared a handsome profit from an investment there.

In June of 1869, three years after his matriculation, Ben completed the four-year college course. This gained him the distinction of being the first graduate of the Agricultural and Mechanical College of the University of Kentucky. In addition to the courses which he took leading to the degree of Bachelor of Science, he completed special courses in civil engineering, for it was the career

[39] of engineering that he planned to embrace when he had finished with his schooling. When he was given his diploma he was offered a position of teaching mathematics in the College, but since he had compressed the four years into three with the idea of getting out sooner into the competitive world, he turned down the offer, though not without appreciation of the compliment they had paid him.

During college the subjects that interested him most were geology, botany, chemistry, and mineralogy. He became absorbed in the theory of evolution, and maintained this absorption throughout his life. Ben could not reconcile the teachings of religions based on mythology with the facts of nature and the discoveries and tenets of science. All of his family were members of the Christian Church, although in that day it was known as Campbellite. 

Young Munson felt that the ideas he held so deeply on the subject of creation and its evolution were inconsistent with conventional church membership; and so he did the only thing that his conscience would allow him to do - he severed his connection with the church. He was clear on his own position, and he must have been something of a proselyter for, during the course of the years, he separated his whole family, including his parents, from their church allegiance, and did his best to establish them in that way of thought and ethics which was for him a very real religion. 

For him, the essence of this religion was to be governed by moral principles involved in the relations of men with each other, and their higher ethical duties to self, family, neighborhood, state and nation; including also all those virtues which religions claim have been given to men by divine inspiration. To state this another way: his code of moral and ethical conduct was formed from a mature system for the preservation of the ego, so long as this wise and adult system included the preservation of the community in which he lived, and, more widely, should comprehend the many

VANINGEN SNYDER MECHANICAL DEPARTMENT OF THE AGRICULTURAL AND MECHANICAL COLLEGE, KENTUCKY UNIVERSITY (Courtesy of the University of Kentucky)

[41] relations arising between the individual and his community. It was his further belief that the inherited impulses formed the strongest force in securing and fixing moral conduct in the individual, and he believed these impulses to be man's inheritance down the ages, when humans developed from low animal standards to those of the highest civilized man. He thought that these inherited moral impulses should be very carefully noted and nurtured; because it was these that have preserved man through the long waning night of his emergence into the comparative light. 

The above ideas he came to rather early in life, and as he grew older they were to be more deeply entrenched in his thinking. It is interesting to speculate how this man, who, from multiple evidence, put into practice this sane and wise philosophy, would have felt about Voltaire's: "They must be men because they kill each other, and they must be civilized because they kill each other brutally."

CHAPTER 

Kentucky Mountain Trip

JUNE, 1869 

E NOW Come to that period of Ben's life when the world was to be bucked, but before we launch him in that active, restlessly building life there is an interlude of delightful escapism. 

There is no doubt that he felt he had a vacation coming to him because, for more than three years, he had been doing the hardest kind of mental and manual labor. Maybe he had a presentiment which, if there is anything at all in presentiments, should overtake many men who have made conspicuous successes in the moving world; maybe he knew that, once this summer of '69 was ended, he would never again be free in the sense that he was free in June of that year. It is true that, later in his life, his cup was to brim with success and happiness, and the contents of this cup were to be seasoned with many splendid vacations; but these later times of comparative freedom were vacations from something that constantly tugged at him, at his conscience, his sense of duty, his affections, and that inevitably were to pull him back into the accelerated tempo of his life.

But this June of 1869, although there was much behind him, there was no definite thing before him that called him insistently on to the game. The personal, mental, and technical equipment he had largely gathered to himself, and he felt that he was trained for the battles to come. He was like a swimmer who, having completed a long and arduous course of training for a protracted swim

Kentucky Mountain Trip [43] 

- - June, 1869 – through rough and cold water, sits down and lazes for a moment at the water's edge before plunging into what will be the test of his life, his abilities, his wits and his stamina. 

This carefree interlude was to take the form of a summer trip that he made together with his brother, T.V., and a young man who must remain nameless for us because his identity has been lost. Although we do not know who this friend was, he has left behind an account of their journey and has called it, “Mountain Trip with the Munson Boys in June 1869." It is more likely that he was a college-mate than that he was a friend who had come on from Fulton County, Illinois, to join them. He was literate, interested in the sciences, a rather careful observer, and even engaged now and then in something that we can call by no other name than that of style. It is a great pity not only that the author of this charming travelogue is lost to us, but also that we lack the latter pages of his work. The whole thing is charming, full of turns of phrase that breathe of the time in which it was composed, and it cannot be omitted from this biography. There are, too, as the reader will see, some amusing comments on persons, places and customs, and the existing fragment is quoted in its entirety. 

TUES. 15 T. V. Munson, W. B. Munson and myself resolved that we would take a trip in the mountains on foot for the purpose of recuperating our health, gathering botanical and geological specimens and seeing the country. With this resolution in view, we packed and prepared everything yesterday and started this morning early for the Covington depot. Before we got there we were very much afraid the cars had left us, but after we got there our patience was completely worn out waiting for the train to start. I never went to a depot yet without being too late or a great deal too soon. This eternal perplexity and bore is because the town clocks never agree with the R.R. time. After passing two or three miserable, dilapidated little stations, which were as bad as the country surrounding them seemed to be good, we arrived at Paris, the county seat of the famous Bourbon. 

The smoke stacks of distilleries were the only thing that looked like manufactories of any kind. Paris looks like a pretty place from the cars, but as

[44] 

you get up into it its beauty vanishes. A beautiful, clear and solid sheet of water that falls over a mill-dam is the most interesting thing in the place. 

We took a spring wagon to Millersburg. Saw Mr. Vincent - gave him Mr. 

Montell's compliments. Walked on towards Blue Lick, at which place we arrived about 3 p.m. Bourbon county is beautiful - many evidences of wealth are to be seen on every side. As you approach Blue Lick the country becomes more hilly and more picturesque. A handsome suspension bridge spans the muddy Licking at the Licks. A few houses, old and tumble-down, tell of the former glory of the place. The remains are here of a magnificent hotel which was burnt during the war and which accommodated 700 visitors. We drank our fill of the spring (barrels of this water are constantly shipped from here to all parts of the country) and as they wanted to charge us three dollars for staying in a little shanty, we moved on up into Fleming Co. On our way we took in the celebrated Blue Lick Battlefield. I found an arrowhead upon it. Culled several flowers and cut a cedar stick as mementoes. Found lodging (75cts) at a very pleasant farm house way up in the Fleming hills, 5 miles from Blue Lick. 

WEDS. 16 Took a wholesome breakfast and started for Elizaville. At ten are off any regular road, down in a deep hollow and lost? The hills around here are awful high and steep, but are cultivated, every one, almost. 

While ever and anon as you pass along the smoke of a solitary cabin is to be seen rising from an abyss at their bottom - generally in a narrow hollow between two hills near which is to invariably be found a good spring and the branch which bears its waters to the Licking. Ben wonders how they manage to gather their crops on these steep hillsides where no wagon can venture. I suggested that they commence at the top of the hill, proceeding downwards, and as they shucked the corn simply tossed it to the bottom. (Land worth $30 per acre.) After much wandering around among awfully steep hills first on a dirt road, then on a path and then on a turnpike we finally arrived at Upper Blue Lick. To my notion it is a much prettier place and contains as good if not better water than the other, but it is owned by an old fogy. Before getting to the Lick, however, we took dinner near 

Fleming Creek and when we got back to the creek I went in swimming and carried Vol across on my back. From the Lick we made our way up the river to a place called "Hawkins Mill" where we remained all night. ($1) 

Went swimming in the Licking. Today we saw some of the most beautiful landscapes that my eye ever rested upon, but found little of importance in a geological point of view, remaining all day in the lower Salurian Formation, the same that we have had since we left.

Kentucky Mountain Trip - June, 1869 

[45] 

THURS. 17 Got started at half past five. Went up to Sherburn Mills, a pretty hamlet, the location of a good bridge across the Licking. Here we met two local geologists who are evidently considered great men by the natives and whose fame doubtless extends several miles into the surrounding hills - each seemed jealous of the other's reputation and each knew about as much about geology as a jackass. One acknowledged the scientific erudition of the other, said he even had books on geology but "then when he tells you anything he gets into the damnedest tangle you ever seed or hyeard tell of." 

They detained us an hour and a half with their gas - then pushed on to Owingsville up hill and down hill, finding lots of fossils coming up to the Devonian Formation we finally reached the place at 4 p.m. It is a right pretty town away up on top of a hill - the highest around - a young mountain. The horizon in the distance, to the East, exhibits a beautiful range of mountains and in fact high hills seem to make a circle of twenty miles around forming a basis of which Owingsville, the county seat, is the center. The seat of justice placed on a pinacle from which the officers of the law can overlook the entire county (of Bath) with a glass, and see any rows or disorders that may be going on. Called on Mrs. Young, a sister of 

Miss Lotte Bell. Saw a man riding a velociped! Got our boots blacked and shaved by a miserable barber and after putting on a clean shirt pushed on two miles and a half towards Mount Sterling, where we got in for the night with an old gent who lives in a very fine, but as yet unfinished, brick house. (50cts) Slept badly. 

FRI. 18 - Walked to Mount Sterling, Montgomery County, before dinner on a pike. On the way saw coal oil indications - the oil seeping out of the rock. At Mt. Sterling we shipped a box of fossils. Met Thornby in a printing office - The Sentinel. Met a Mr. Turner who knew something of geology in fact he had a quack chemist working for him with a blow pipe on some dirt found on Licking, which contains alum and borax. Took our snack and some ice cream and lemonade — moved on South, riding part of the way in a wagon through the levee near which Sam McKee was raised to the house of an old gent named Spratt 71/2 miles south of Mt. Sterling. Before getting here we got into the subcarboniferous formation - found sulphur, fool's gold, laurel, etc. Good place to stay-milk and honey (25cts) The old gent has two sons-in-law in Pettis Co. He was a "constitutional union man,” had a son in the Union army but he "would have went in on the other side had he known how things were going to turn out." He thinks the negro a gorilla. In sustaining this point he unconsciously ran into Darwinism still he is a Christian, and actually believes that the world was made in six 

---

[46] 

days and nights. At all events he was a clever old gent and was, as he says, "Kentucky all over.” His fine ham and excellent biscuit were only equalled by his cold milk and fresh honey. They were fully as good as the feather bed and cozy room he gave us in which to sleep. Decidedly the best place we had. 

SAT. 19-Started at six for a knob called Pilot Knob, which on one side presents the appearance of a tower built by some pre-historic race with which to ascend to heaven, or like a monument that had been hewn from the mountain by the ancient Indians in honor of the Great Unknown God. 

According to the natives roundabout, many a weird legend is told of this mountain, in which stories of buried silver are invariably interwoven; and many are the weary and anxious fortune-seekers who have come here to search for the hidden treasure. But always unsuccessful they have always gone away disappointed. After winding through thickets, under overhanging rocks and up, straight up, through an almost effaced Indian defile, we arrived at "Dripping Springs," which falls out from the side of an overhanging rock some 250 feet above the valley below, which looks like a deep abyss. Here we slaked our thirst and pushed on through many difficult passages to the summit, finding on our way no less than 15 new flowers. 

Below the mountain was of shale, then came the conglomerate, all capped with limestone. On the summit the scene was magnificent. To me it was the grandest I ever looked upon. Though enchanting it was real. Though indescribable it was inspiring - my eyes closed - my thoughts turned from nature up to Nature's God and from my heart came a hymn of thanks to the Creator for the greatness of His work.
