I climbed a tree upon the top, and could see in every direction, except to the south, just as far as the eye could reach. To the west the whole of Clark county and part of Fayette beyond. To the northwest Bourbon hills were to be seen. To the north the whole of Montgomery lay at my feet. To the northeast Bath county was plain, together with the Nicholas and Fleming Hills in the vicinity of Blue Lick. On the east the distant Bath mountains obscured my view somewhat, but the sight in that direction was nevertheless exceedingly interesting. To the south and southeast, appeared a continual succession of knobs, constantly rising as they receded, until they culminated in dim, distant and lofty hills. After analyzing all our flowers on the top of the summit we commenced our descent, down, down now a very good path now almost perpendicular and every now and then throwing our things over a rock and climbing down an ancient tree to them. The road once reached that leads to Red River, we pushed on without difficulty, taking 

---

Kentucky Mountain Trip – June, 1869 

[ 47 ] our dinner at a spring by the wayside, and reaching the "Forge” at 4 P.M.

Crossed in a canoe. Drank some sulphur water. Found that there is no furnace nor anything else here, but that it is necessary to move on 10 or 

12 miles southeast. At a store went swimming in Red River. Ben killed a quail with the pistol. Near the "Forge" we met Fitch, treasurer of the furnace company, who gave us some specimens and information. Moved on to Mr. McKinney's, three miles beyond the Forge, where we obtained lodging. (75cts)

SUN. 20 - Started rather late this morning, and proceeded rather slowly up Hardock Creek, to the spring half way up State House Mountain. Here we took our snack dinner, the best we have had yet; and also had the best water to drink with it I ever drank out of a spring. From there we moved on up a steep and winding path to the top of State House Rock. This is said to be the highest elevation in the county of Estell, a county full of knobs and young mountains. The path leads up a narrow backbone, and when you get to the top of the rock is a perpendicular precipice down for several hundred feet on every side except where the path lies. Many names were inscribed on the top of this mountain and ripe huckleberries were also to be found - (we got a fill of mulberries before we started up.) While up there a dozen hard looking customers resembling bushwhackers came filing up, and had the "dead wood" on us; but they proved to be furnace mechanics. The scene from this mountain is not as interesting as the one from Pilot Knob, though it is higher. To the southeast, the outlines of the 

Cumberland Mountains, 100 miles distant, are visible; but in the other directions the surrounding knobs obstruct the view. I went to sleep on the summit. Ben analyzed a flower, and Vol wrote a letter to his gal. From here we descended to the Furnace, about a half a mile distant, where we witnessed the modus operandi of smelting iron; and obtained several specimens of the ore. Here we got a mule ride down to the new furnace - three miles - by riding three and driving four mules for some lazy but accommodating teamsters. This new furnace is destined to become an important affair. It will, when finished, be over a year in building, and will cost an enormous sum, as $2000 are now spent daily in construction, and between 500 and 600 hands are employed on it. Their houses constitute quite a little town, that looks exceedingly picturesque down between the mountains as it is mountains that seem to press it on every side. A wooden railroad, six miles long, is being constructed down Millers Creek to the River. 

We followed the road down to near its intended terminus, and stayed all night with a Mr. Elliott. ($1) 

---

[48] 

water MON. 21 - The Ky River at this point (10 miles above Irvine) is almost deep enough to float a steamboat, except upon the shoals, even in this low in fact it has not been more than a week since a boat was up this far. After some trouble we found a negro who owned a boat, and bought it from him for $5. It is a good thing for green hands to go down the river in, for, being neither a canoe nor a skiff, it cannot upset very easy. It is shaped just like a flatboat, and is propelled like a canoe, with a paddle; hence we will necessarily go slow. Soon got started, and moved slowly down the famous Kentucky through the most picturesque hills. First through water almost still, and then over rapid shoals, around crooked bends. Now meeting a flatboat of corn working its way up the river, and every mile or two passing a canoe or flatboat tied to the bank; which has been left muddy and nasty by the recent fall of the water. We reached Irvine about 12 and landed. It was county court day, and the little old town (which has a better court house than Lexington) was full of people. They drink nothing but pure whiskey up here in the mountains, and the bars contain no other kind of fluid. We went about a half a mile north to the "White Sulphur" spring, which looks more like a watering place than any other I have yet seen. It is now played out. But the large frame hotel, almost vacant, with its piazzas extending around to the spring, and fronting each other, tell of its former glory. Though dilapidated now, it has been a most pleasant place, and no less than five different kinds of water are to be had in its immediate vicinity. The building contains some 500 rooms, and any number of shade trees make the spot exceedingly delightful. We drank of the 

White Sulphur, which is very different from the Blue Lick. Returned to our boat. Dropped down half a mile and stopped at Calybiate Spring.

This is the most health-giving water of all. It tastes like iron. We then floated on ate our dinner — stole a rock perch from a trot-line — stopped about 8 miles down and washed out our boat. Moved on down, and as dark was approaching, and no house visible fit to stop at, we stopped at a hut and prepared to run all night. But as it looked like rain, and as the woman could not get us any supper “case she had no wood," we gave our fish to her lying little boy, and moved on to Major Lane's, a mile below, where we obtained lodging — arriving just in time to miss a storm which continued all night. 

TUES. 22 - Here we had the first, last and only exhibition of Kentucky hospitality on our trip. The Maj. kept us for nothing. We moved on all day, passing a pretty water- . . . (the last pages of the MS are missing)

Kentucky Mountain Trip - June, 1869 

[49] It is true that Ben is mentioned by name only three times during the course of the all-too-brief narrative, but the mere fact of his having taken such a journey is of importance. It seems to have been a sort of informal geological field-trip, against which are projected the three above-mentioned references. These constitute three neat keys to the boy he was, and to the man he was to become. "Ben wonders how they manage to gather their crops on these steep hillsides where no wagon can venture." This is an expression of the practical horse-sense of a man who, all his life, had a great feeling for the land. It prepares us to be less astonished when we learn later that he sized-up, bought, sold, and traded millions of acres of land in his adopted state. Here we have beginning that sense of land values which enabled him later to profit from the sale of whole sections in the northwest portion of the State of Texas for $100 a section. This same sort of transaction he repeated over and over. Where the margin of profit is small, and where the deal is worthwhile only when hundreds of thousands of acres are involved, it takes not only an ear closely tuned to the trends of the Northern land markets, but also a good eye for the character and value of the land itself. "Ben killed a quail with his pistol.” 

Of the two promises implicit in this quotation, one was realized and one was not. He did turn out to be a great lover of shooting, though, according to his hunting companions, he was only a fair shot. But he loved it. On big quail- or deer-shoots he would range the country like a coursing-hound; walk down all the other men in the expedition, and return to camp still the freshest one of them. "Ben analyzed a flower."

This is Maria Linley's son; here is the definite appearance of one of the other sides of this man, an indisputable bit of evidence of that well-roundedness that was to grow and develop as he grew older. If the reader will review, in immediate succession, the three

[ ] 

excerpts taken from this vacation account, he will see the importance of the whole selection. The first, to recapitulate, is the practical man, who developed into the biggest land-trader in a section. of the country where land-traders deal in stupendous figures; a man who retained for himself and his family, as the sinew of his private fortune, some of the choicest acreage of what passed through his busy hands, and which was scattered over a wide section of Texas. 

Next is the appearance of the sportsman; the man who hunted and fished over much of Texas, Oklahoma, Wyoming, Idaho and Colorado; who belonged to sportsmen's clubs in the Rockies, and whose great pleasure was to escape to the mountains every summer, not only because he detested the summer heat of North Texas, but because in the mountains he could indulge his sincere love of the out-of-doors, and the pleasures afforded there by field and stream.

The last reference to Ben in the narrative comes almost as a shock. Other than the few professionals, there are not many men in the country today who have any real interest in botany; and as few as these are now, they must have been proportionately much fewer in 1869. He never lost this interest. An examination of his letters shows this. And he loved to give flowers as presents.

CHAPTER A Place in the Competitive World 

[OW THAT we have seen, in this young man of twenty-three, NOW these three astonishingly true clues to the more mature and older man, we must return to that summer of '69, the actual season when he began to forge for himself a place in the competitive world.

He had, on leaving college, expected at least to begin his career as a civil engineer, and nothing is more natural than that he should seek employment in that field, and in his native state. When he reached home he found that the Rockford, Rock Island and St. 

Louis Railway was laying trackage through his own county of Fulton. Ben's parents had moved to Astoria by this time, and his father now either had no suitable conveyance for Ben to use, or he could not spare the horse or team necessary for the trip. Ben walked twenty miles to the adjoining town, and there applied for a position at the office of the chief engineer. The engineer's great need was for men, just any kind of men who could push the rails on down toward the Mississippi River metropolis, St. Louis. "Have you had any experience with this kind of work?" he wanted to know. "No; but I'm well equipped for this work, and I'm good at mathematics." "That all?” "I have a college education." "Oh hell, you college engineers are no good at building roads.

I couldn't afford to trust you with any important engineering

[52] 

work, but if you want to work, I'll pay you the same wages I do to a farmhand as a rod-man or a chain-man.' 99 "When and where can I report for that kind of work?" was the question and answer.

He got his instructions, the where and the when, and he was "Johnny-on-the-spot." He found that his immediate superior was a practical construction man of much experience, but one who lacked the technical training that he had had. It did not take Ben long to learn that there was something to be said for both sides of the matter, and he bided his time until he could master the actual constructional details, and until his theoretical and technical training would stand him in good stead. The immediate job he was set at was that of cross-sectioning the right-of-way in order to fix the location of the stakes which were to show the amount of cut and fill at each point of the roadway. In this work Ben took the rod. As soon as the older man had the data down on paper, Ben would begin to figure in his head the amount of cut and fill at each stake. Ben's mental arithmetic was faster than the other's paper "ciphering," and it soon turned out that the practical engineer had such confidence in Ben's accuracy that he was accepting Ben's figures without taking the trouble to check them out on paper. Sometimes, when the old fellow grew too tired, they rested, and on these occasions Ben would handle the level. It so happened that, through his eagerness to learn, his quickness and his industry, he was soon doing the major portion of the actual engineering work. In the matter of making the monthly estimates, Ben was asked by the engineer-in-charge to figure the cubic yardage to which the contractor was entitled. This was now strictly in his line, and he performed his duties so expeditiously that he was soon given complete charge of all this work, receiving but little or no help from the older man, who was willing to get the credit and the better pay while another did the greater share of work.

A Place in the Competitive World 

[53] For this job of increasing importance and responsibility Ben received two dollars a day, and out of this he had to pay his board. 

He had little left at the end of the month. But, since he knew that he was acquiring valuable experience, and that merit and virtue were bound to be recognized in the long run, he did not complain of the manifest injustice, nor offer any suggestion that he be advanced in the titular job, or have an increase in pay. However, somebody in the local corps of workers knew who was really getting the job done, and before much time had gone by the chief engineer sent Ben word to take over the position of transit-man in the party engaged in locating the right-of-way in advance of actual construction.

He now received a regular salary of $100 a month, and was given his board. All this amounted to an increase in real wages of at least one hundred per cent. He filled this job in a manner consistent with his now-established reputation for efficiency, and held it until the road pushed on down-state and made junction with the trackage already laid into St. Louis. He had made a splendid impression on the contractor, and the latter suggested seriously to Ben that he was far too good a man to stay in such work; that there was not much future in engineering, and that he should take a contract of his own or find some other work that would move him along faster in the world.

The contractor said he had the influence to secure for Ben a contract for any kind of work along the line for which Ben felt himself fitted. Acting on this suggestion, Ben applied to the contracting engineer for the subcontract of getting the piling and bridge timber necessary to the construction of that section of the railroad that ran through his home county of Fulton. This immediate section was forested with the finest oak timber, eminently suited to the work in hand. 

He frankly admitted to the engineer in charge of contract work

[54] 

that such work was unfamiliar to him, but that he did know the country and its timber, and felt that, because of this knowledge, he could handle a contract for the heavy piling and bridge timbers.

The price, or bid, was a problem, for he was without experience in this. He hit on the idea of taking the contract at what he considered the "usual" price (although his willingness to proceed on this basis would indicate that he had at least a pretty good idea of how the deal would figure out in the end), and made this suggestion to the contracting engineer. His proposition was accepted. 

Ben, of course, had no sawmill with which to cut his timber, and so he went into some sort of partnership agreement with a friend who did own a sawmill that lay within workable distance of the proposed right-of-way. This agreement appears to have been nothing more than that of a subcontract; for the sawmill owner named a close figure at which he agreed to furnish the timber from his own land if this became necessary, but agreed that the timber could be furnished by other parties if Ben could persuade these other parties to sell at a figure below that mentioned by the sawmill operator. It was the latter procedure that prevailed. 

The two men, working together, completed their part of the contract within ninety days, and for his part of the business arrangement young Munson made a net profit of $4500, a princely sum for a young man just starting out with a college education which he had worked for and paid for himself. The money gave him a feeling of confidence. At the prices for which he himself, later in his life, was to sell off a big portion of northwest Texas, he could have, with this sum, bought and paid cash for about 25,000 acres of this land. But there was a piper who had to be paid. 

He strained his back, and was forever to suffer from the effects of this great youthful enthusiasm for his first independent job. 

But this was not the most serious ill effect of these ninety days of strenuous effort.

A Place in the Competitive World 

[55] In his life in the woods where he was cutting the timber, and along the railroad right-of-way, it often happened that suitable drinking water was not available, and Ben - like all young men -thinking he had a cast-iron constitution, drank whatever cleanlooking water presented itself to his thirst. The result of this not-unnatural carelessness was a very severe spell of typhoid fever, which kept him in bed for eight weeks. His life was despaired of. 

Indeed, following a complete collapse, due doubtless to the sudden subsidence of the fever, the report was all over the town of 

Astoria that he had died.

But the circulators of this rumor had not taken into account the heroic efforts of a young man cousin who was visiting at that time in the family. This was Jim Linley, who helped nurse Ben through a great part of his illness. When Ben was apparently lifeless, young Linley refused to give up hope. He called for help, and himself gave the example of strenuous friction of the limbs to restore the circulation of the blood. Young Jim Linley won his fight, and Ben's life was saved.

It had been a close call; and during the long period of convalescence that must have been galling to a young man of his energetic nature, Ben knew that he would have to take things easy for a while. A safe investment for the whole $4500 would have to wait; matching the temper of his own private steel against the armor of the world would have to wait too. He would have to find close at home some mild form of employment that would be not too taxing. With this in mind he formed a partnership with his brother, J. T. Munson, and bought a drugstore in the town of Astoria. For a young man of Ben's mentality, temperament, and equipment, the drug business, as it was then conducted in little towns, was simply no business at all. He knew that if he stuck to this it would mean the end of his dreams and ambitions to amount to something in the world. He saw himself caught in

[56] 

a deadly groove that called for an alpaca jacket and false black sateen sleeves to protect his cuffs from the tops of the counters. 

The money remaining from the drugstore venture burned his pocket, and moreover he had got back his health and strength. 

After six months he had had enough of this kind of life. "J.T.," he said to his brother, "I can't stand any longer this business of selling pills; I'm pulling out." "Where you going?" "I'm heading for the Southwest; that's the coming land of opportunity. I'll hire a clerk in my place to help you, and I'll keep my interest in the business until you sell the whole thing, or until you want to buy out my half."

J.T. and the others let him go. His heart was set on it, and he felt in his bones that he was going to succeed. He had such confidence in this coming success that he relinquished his child's share in his father's estate. Before leaving Illinois, he executed a quitclaim deed in favor of his two younger sisters, Tryphena Maria and Mary Genevra, young ladies of twenty-three and eighteen years respectively.

But he was not to be so soon free and independent of his family, for six months after Ben left Illinois, J.T. sold the drugstore in 

Astoria, and followed his younger brother. However, this is getting ahead of the narrative.

Ben headed into the Southwest and stopped for some time at Parsons, Kansas, a new town that had recently been laid out on the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railway, which was building through to Texas. He did not like Parsons, and so passed on through the southern part of Kansas, across the Indian Territory, with the avowed goal of settling in Sherman, Texas, a town which, at that time, everyone thought would be the terminus of the M.K. & T. in the State. He reached Sherman in the fall of 1871, and from this time on his life belongs to the State of his adoption.

PART TWO

CHAPTER 

Denison - Gateway to the Texas Plains

HERMAN WAS already a well-established and conservative town. 

SHERM 

Its leading citizens were scions of aristocratic families that had come out from the old states, mostly before the Civil War. There were pretty and charming women and girls, and there was one in particular with a quick wit, a fabulous independence, a complexion and a head of blond hair that were already famous. She was small, nineteen, and an unreconstructed Rebel from Alabama. 

But Ben knew none of this. He was twenty-five, and busy with the future that was opening up before him like an Eldorado. He was helping to establish the new town of Denison eleven miles north of Sherman, and had no time now for thoughts of romance - although somewhere back in his immediate past there was a girl named Liza Sims who either already had, or was soon to wear, an engagement ring from Ben.

Ben's knowledge of surveying immediately stood him in good stead. He surveyed and located a great deal of land, some of it near at hand in Grayson County.

But before we go more into detail of Ben's life, let us take a look at his adopted community through the eyes of Edw. King as evidenced in excerpts from Chapter 17 of his book, The Great 

South, published in 1875, four years after Ben arrived in Texas.

Standing in the main street of Denison, Texas, the new town near the southern border of the Indian Territory, six hundred and twenty-one miles southwest of St. Louis, it was hard to realize that only four months before

[60] 

my visit its site was almost a wilderness, not a building of any kind having yet then been erected there. For all around us was Babel a wild rush of business, a glory in affairs, an unbounded delight in mere labor, by which 

I was at once oppressed and appalled.

The slightest indication of progress was pointed out as a gigantic foreshadowing of the future preeminence of Denison. "There are from 2500 to 

3000 people here now," said one gentleman to us; "how's that for four months? That'll make some of the incredulous folks take their frame houses off from the rollers!" - an expression intended to open up a startling prospect for the future of Denison. But, indeed, all these enthusiastic pioneers of a new civilization were justified in their seemingly wild prophecies of greatness. Northern Texas, under the beneficent influences of railroad pioneering, is assuming a prominence which had never been imagined for it until within the last five years. Denison into existence sprang as the chief town of the newly developed region. It was organized four months before my visit, and since that time the Denison Town Company had sold $90,000.00 worth of building lots.* Two railroads,† coming to it from opposite points, and not costing it a cent, laid the foundation for its remarkable advance, an advance more like magic than like the normal growth of a pioneer settlement. . . . The lumber for the houses and business establishments was brought hundreds of miles. It was extremely remarkable that in a community one-half of which was undoubtedly made up of professional ruffians, terminus gamblers, and the offscourings of society, and where there was not yet a regularly organized government, there was not more of terrorism.

Every third building in the place was a drinking saloon with gambling appurtenances, filled after nightfall with a depraved, adventurous crowd, whose profanity was appalling, whose aspect was hideous. Men drunk and sober danced to rude music in the poorly-lighted saloons, and did not lack female partners. In the vulgar bestiality of language, in the pure delight of parading profanity and indecency, the ruffian there had no equal. The gambling houses were nightly frequented by hundreds. Robberies were, of course, of frequent occurrence in the gambling hells, and perhaps are so still. . . . Rough men abounded, and would, without doubt, have knocked anyone upon the head who should find himself alone, unarmed, and late at night, in their clutches. But the carrying of concealed weapons is so • Denison Town Company was organized and on September 23, 1872, the first lots were sold. Denison was named by the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Ry's. engineering crew for the road's executive vice president, George Denison. † Missouri, Kansas & Texas, and Houston & Texas Central.

JOT GUNTER

[62] 

expressly forbidden by Texas laws, that cases of shooting rarely occurred. 

Staging to Sherman we passed immense wagon-trains of merchandise, creaking forward through the wax-like soil... we reached Sherman, the forty-year-old shire town of Grayson County. . . . The town is built around a square, in the center of which stands a low, unpainted wooden building, known as the Court-House. . . . Large numbers of people seemed diligently engaged in doing nothing whatsoever. Although it was midwinter, the day was perfect as one in June at the North. From the tone of Mr. King's comment it is easy to see that he himself was nothing of an adventurer. As a product of the effete 

East he could not have been sympathetic with all this lusty life he describes, and it is doubtful if he did more than poke his head in at the window of a "gambling hell." Certainly, from what he tells us, he did not venture out after nightfall. Mr. King may have felt a bit safer, physically, in Sherman; but it is obvious that he had a poor opinion of the town. Chastened and intimidated by what he had seen in Denison, he could look with nothing clearer than a jaundiced eye upon a little city that was already a center of culture and learning. A man so prejudiced could hardly have been received socially by the old and aristocratic families already established in Sherman, and such a reception was not accorded him.

He was just another late-come carpetbagger who experienced occasional difficulties in writing correct English, and doubtless thought his first coconut was made of solid wood. 

Sherman that is the people of the city did not make the acquaintance of Mr. King, but, had they only known it, they would have found themselves in close harmony with him on one salient point: a sovereign scorn for Denison and all its works. 

It was this leaning-over-backwards on the part of Sherman toward the proposition of the railroad to build into their city that was, really, responsible for the founding of Denison; but of such conservatism in one place is progress born in another. 

Ben was never a carouser, and did not let himself be swerved

Denison - Gateway to the Texas Plains [63] from his purpose by the attractions of the region. He realized almost immediately that a knowledge of the law would be essential in the career he had chosen for himself, and during such time as he was not actually surveying and locating in the field, he began serious reading of the law. In the meantime, to make assurance doubly sure, he formed a temporary partnership with a former schoolmate from the college in Kentucky. It is obvious that he was diligent in his study, for, at the end of six months, he passed his examinations and was admitted to the bar. 

In 1873 he formed a partnership with Jot Gunter. Mr. Gunter was himself a lawyer, married, and already established in Sherman. The talents and personalities of these two men were perfectly fashioned to dovetail into a good partnership, for each respected and felt the need of the other's capabilities. Jot Gunter was a widely popular man; not only was he good company and "hail fellow well met," but he had repeatedly proved himself a true friend to nearly all who knew him. He was directly responsible for bringing, and continuing to bring, a great deal of business to the firm; however, the many cares and responsibilities attendant on conducting the company's affairs irked him, and it was here that Ben made his contribution, for all of his life W.B. loved problems and gleefully exerted all his physical and mental powers to whip them into an orderly and logical solution. 

His quick, analytical mind would rapidly expose all the facets of any business proposition, detecting the flaws and estimating them against the possible advantages. His decisions were sure and true, and Jot Gunter admittedly accepted them without question. Yes, it was a good partnership of good friends, and it was the Gunters who encouraged and furthered Ben's and Mary Ella's romance. 

Today, seventy-two years later, Mary Ella still speaks of them fondly, saying: "I was always devoted to Jot and 'Bocca' (a pet name for his wife, Roxana) as well as their only daugher, Eula.”

[64] 

According to the evidence of persons still living, there were only three men of importance in Sherman who favored the raising of a bonus to be given to the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railway as an inducement for the railway to build its line on to Sherman. 

These were Ben Munson, Jot Gunter, and Captain Tom Brown, the latter to become later a Chief Justice of the Supreme Court of Texas. The people of Sherman saw no necessity for having a railroad from the north, and they did not subscribe to the bonus in sufficient volume to induce the railroad to come to their city. This was not a unique situation in the history of reactionary communities surrounded by frontier territory, but it was particularly unfortunate - certainly in a business way — that Sherman did turn down the railroad, for, in so doing, they not only turned down the general shops of the road, but also the proposition for the building of two branch lines, one southeast and the other southwest* 

Ben was particularly active in his efforts to arouse the inhabitants of Sherman to their opportunity, and in this was ably abetted not only by the two men mentioned above but also by Mr. R. S. 

Stevens, the principal promoter of the railroad.

When all efforts had failed in Sherman, Ben, accompanied by two agents of the company supplied by Stevens, went to what is now the site of Denison and began his ambitious plans for the laying out of the new town. He set about this with the same enthusiasm that characterised everything he did. He worked hard and long at platting the new town, giving it wide streets and helping to locate the terminals of the main and the branch lines of the railroad. This fascinated him, and railroads remained a major preoccupation with him for a great part of his active business life. At this stage of his career he liked to do the actual work, and later, when he was to promote and build several short lines in The Denison & Southeastern R.R. and the Denison & Pacific R.R., both later purchased by and incorporated into the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Ry.

THE FIRST HOME BEN BUILT IN DENISON

[66] 

Texas, he kept his acute and accurate interest in the actual business of securing right-of-ways, securing equipment, and maintaining the services of the roads. 

His eye for business, right at the moment of the founding of Denison, was wide open. He saw that there would be an immediate demand for building material, and, as there was as yet no railroad for such to be shipped in on, he made a deal for a sawmill on Choctaw Creek, about six miles east of the present town of Denison. The difficulties were great. There were no roads as we know roads, and the country was hilly. This investment was not, in itself, much of a gamble, since he doubtless had a monopoly on the sawed lumber, and since a fairly figurable quantity of lumber would be needed to erect the buildings for the railroad and the houses for the workmen who were to be brought in. The presence of a guaranteed number of inhabitants would necessitate the erection of stores to serve them, and sidewalks to keep them out of the red mud. Furthermore, he was fortified in this venture by the close friendship which he formed with Stevens, a friendship and a loyalty on the part of the Road which was passed on by Stevens to his successors in the company. When one reads the letters from the "Katy" men to Ben, one finds not only an unfailing courtesy and respect, but a tone almost of affection — positive proof of the admiration and regard in which he was held throughout his life by the officers of the company.

But Ben did gamble on the realization of his dream, and that dream was to make Denison a town of importance. If that dream failed somewhat of its greatest realization there are factors of opposition and blocking tactics which should be taken into consideration. The gamble referred to is that he invested heavily in lands surrounding the new city of Denison. He, wisely, bought in several directions from the town, and much of this land is still in the hands of the estate.

Denison [67] 

Gateway to the Texas Plains

Ben wanted desperately for this new town to boom, and felt that the erection of a brick residence might help in that direction. 

Though a bachelor at the time, he would be able to use a home because the ecstatic letters he wrote to his family in Illinois, extolling the golden opportunities of this new land, gradually brought all the Munsons to Denison. "Yes," he thought, "I'll build a good sturdy house. Jennie and

Trite can run it between them, and J.T. can live there with us." 

Bravely the new house started to materialize, Denison's first two-story brick residence, a fine and attractive dwelling with simple dignified lines and no gewgaws. But this idyllic dream came close to being a nightmare. The Great Depression struck and Ben's meager resources were gobbled up. The half-completed house threatened to fall about his head like the walls of Jericho. 

All of one night our hero walked the floor with a pistol in his hand, thinking that the gun offered the easiest solution to his troubles. But easy solutions were not in his line; he liked 'em tough. So the gun was a bit of melodrama that lost its appeal with the sane light of day. Instead he went to see Ed Perry, vice president of the bank that was owned by Ed's cousin, John Scullin of 

St. Louis, whose name still graces one of Denison's streets. "I'm sorry, Ed," he said; "but I've tried to pull out every way 

I know. I'm broke flatter than that toy balloon you saw one of your boys stick a pin in. I guess the bank will just have to take the house and do what they can with it." 

Ed Perry listened quietly; then leaning forward and resting his elbows on the desk in front of him he clasped his hands and started speaking: "I'm an older man than you, Ben, and I've seen depressions before. They're rotten things, but the world of business and finance has always managed to struggle to its knees before the count of ten, and it will do it again. I have faith in your integrity,

Denison - Gateway to the Texas Plains [ 69 ] and the greatest confidence in your future. Go ahead with your house; finish it in exact accordance with your original plans. This bank will foot your bills right down to the last nail."

And so was built the brick house that now shelters the Denison Public Library.* J.T. moved into the middle upstairs bedroom and gave Jennie and Trite twenty dollars a month for pin money. 

Ben contributed another twenty, and agreed to pay all the grocery bills. Trite was a good cook; the new home was safely launched on a high sea of domesticity.

In the meantime Ben and his partner, Jot Gunter, in addition to their Sherman office, opened a law and real estate office in Denison. There was an interesting variation in the outside signs of the respective offices. In Sherman the outer sign read GUNTER 

& MUNSON while in Denison it read MUNSON & GUNTER, the first name in each case indicating the managing partner of that particular office. However, the manifold activities of the partnership immediately made it necessary for Ben to engage the services of a young lawyer to take care of the legal business in his branch while he was away, as he was a great deal of the time, and on a kind of business that was destined to constitute the solid core of his personal fortune.

This was the business of locating land certificates. For the reader to whom this is not clear, a few words will suffice. During the great period of railroad building in Texas, the State, which owned immense portions of the land within its boundaries, offered special inducements to the railroads. These inducements consisted principally of the outright gift to the railroad of eight alternate sections of land for every mile of road that the companies built. 

But since, at this time, so much of the land area of the State was * The building is still part of the W. B. Munson Estate and has been loaned to the 

City of Denison, rent free, to assist in the establishment of a creditable public library.

[70] 

unsurveyed, the gift was made in the form of certificates, each certificate representing a section of land. The locating of the section represented by the certificate could be made anywhere on State-owned lands. This meant that, even as late as the seventies and eighties, many railroads, and other companies that had performed services for the State, held certificates representing millions of acres of land but, as yet, not surveyed land or any particular body of land.

The owners of these certificates, whether they were public companies or private individuals, were free to dispose of them as they chose; and the owner of a certificate could, if he wanted to, have his land surveyed from any lands belonging to the State. He then was the owner of this land, and had a title to it directly from the State.

Ben made money at this, going and coming. He not only was paid for his surveying of the land for others, but he, individually, and with his partner, Gunter, and his brother, J.T., bought certificates outright and surveyed the land for themselves. In this way a man could, at that time, secure a clear title to a section of land for the price of the certificate entitling him to a section, plus the cost of surveying. These certificates sold for as little then as sixty dollars. This land, secured for, say, sixty dollars a section, could be sold for a very handsome profit at the price of seventyfive cents and a dollar per acre, prices at which the land was fairly gobbled up by men with money, especially in the North. It was the recognition that most of this investment money was held in the North that led Ben, some years later, to form the Corwith Pool, of which more will be said later. 

It was certificates belonging to the Houston & Texas Central 

Ry. and the International & Great Northern Ry. that the new partnership located in great quantity, especially in the Panhandle counties of Texas; and this was done after they had located a great

Denison [71] 

Gateway to the Texas Plains deal of land in the near-by counties of Cook, Denton, Wise, Clay, and Wichita.

The outlying sections of the State were, at this time, pretty generally infested with Indians, and it was unsafe for a surveying and locating party to go out into the hinterland without the protection of armed guards. Such parties also maintained a scouting service designed to warn them of the approach of marauding Indians. As late as March 1880, we find Ben's brother, J.T., writing to Governor Roberts in Austin:

Sir: I expect to leave here with my surveying parties for the Pan Handle country about Apr. 1st and to resume work under my contract to survey the capital lands not later than 1st May next. I respectfully request that you furnish me escort of Mounted State Troops to accompany my parties until their work shall be finished. Safety for those who shall do the work in my opinion makes an urgent necessity for the escort asked. The escort would also greatly facilitate the labors of the Inspector in obtaining the topography of the country. Please inform me if the escort can be granted. 

Probably May 25th will be as soon as there will be real necessity for the escort, when we shall be some distance South of the Canadian River. 

An incident took place in 1874 (which Ben passed down by word of mouth to his children) that is eloquent of conditions that prevailed on the then frontier. There were thirty armed men, and they had two four-mule teams to haul supplies from Camp Supply. 

Before they left this place they were warned by the prairie grapevine that the Indians were due to go on a rampage within a matter of weeks, and that unless the surveying party could complete its work within sixty days it would be in great danger of attack. 

Accepting this advice and warning in good faith, the party rushed their work with all possible speed. However, on the day before the surveying was actually completed, a runner from the Buffalo Camp on the Canadian River joined the party and reported with great excitement that his three camp companions had been murdered while he was away from his camp, hauling a

[72] 

load of buffalo hides to Adobe Walls. The latter settlement was the center for buffalo-hunters for that whole section of Texas and the Indian Territory.

Ben, together with a number of armed men from his party, accompanied the fellow back to his camp, and there indeed they did find the three horribly mutilated and mangled bodies. The men's hearts had been cut out and staked to the ground. While the little group of men was engaged in burying the three bodies, they saw, to their extreme anxiety, a small band of mounted Indians watching them intently from the distant hills. With the incentive of such close and visible danger it is no wonder that the surveying party completed its work the next day by nightfall; and, in order to put as much territory as possible between themselves and the peril, they marched and drove until way into the night, and made their camp in utter darkness in an effort to throw their enemies off the track. This first night's camp was pitched on the 

North Fork of the Canadian River. But they were destined not to rest easy.

Most of the men, worn from the hard day's work and the trek of the early night, fell asleep while the cook was preparing supper. 

They were rudely awakened by a volley of shots fired into the camp, and by whooping and yelling Redskins who raced madly around the spot in typical Indian tactics. This show of aboriginal temperament seems mainly to have been in an effort to stampede the horses and mules of the party, but the animals had been securely lariated and none got away. An Indian is a good deal like a coyote: one can sound like a dozen. The white men seized their courage and their guns and fired into the wildly circling raiders. 

At last a pony and his rider went down, and the remainder of the raiders, who could now be seen to be few in number, took to the heels of their mounts and disappeared into the darkness. The 

Indian who had taken the count was only winged, and he skulked

Denison — Gateway to the Texas Plains [73] off through the brush on the floor of the valley and made good his escape. The pony, blanket, and bridle, however, remained in the hands of the white men as a trophy of this close brush with death. 

The only persons who came off really badly in this clash with the red outlaws were Government Scouts, who had fallen in with the party only that day. They must have been new on the job and tender to the region, for, when camp was made, they neglected to stake their fine cavalry horses; and these the Indians were successful in stampeding and in taking away with them. The plainsmen were so disgusted at this lack of foresight on the part of men who should have known better, that they allowed them to walk all the way back to the Post - although it can be wondered if there was any place where they could have ridden. 

The following year, 1875, a party went out again, even farther into the western portion of the Panhandle, and this time too had an interesting adventure which Ben passed on to his children. 

At Fort Elliott the white men had again been warned of the presence of Indians and lawless Mexicans farther west, and they were on the qui vive during the whole of their expedition. As is well known of such expeditions in those times, the fresh meat for the men consisted mainly of buffalo and antelope. Water was a terrible problem. They often had to drive snakes from the little sinks on the high llano, before they could secure water for drinking and cooking.

One midnight the horses and mules gave unmistakable signs of the excitement these animals feel when a stranger approaches a camp. The horses were drawn in closer and staked in a narrow circle around the camp. The men waited in silence. After more than an hour of this tense anxiety a human voice hailed them.

Although the white men feared that this might be a trick of a party of Indians to ascertain their effective strength and the exact locaion of their camp, they answered several times in English but got

[74] 

no response. The guide for the surveyors spoke Mexican, which was the language widely used by the untamed Indians and, of course, by the lawless Mexicans still roaming this section. When the guide finally answered the hail in Spanish, he was quickly answered, and three men came into camp. The number of the intruders was a great relief to the work party.

The three strangers claimed to be buffalo-hunters strayed from their friends, but the white men suspected that they had been sent reconnoitering by a band of Indians or other outlaws. They forced the men to bed down in the camp, and a man with a cocked rifle sat guard over them all night. The following morning they were permitted to leave, but before they left, the surveyors made a great show of their armed strength, getting out all their rifles, pistols, and ammunition. The party was not molested further on this trip.

It was in the years '74 and '75 that Ben, as the more active member of the partnership, made his first contacts with ranchmen from farther north and west; and as a result of these contacts, Gunter & Munson located vast bodies of grazing land for these ranchers, the locations being made through certificates and direct patents. 

The partnership reaped splendid profits in this way, Adair and Goodnight alone having secured from Gunter & Munson at this time more than 240,000 acres.

But since the year 1874 was to bring to Ben Munson the beginning of the greatest experience of his whole life, we must abandon, for the time being, the vast plains of the Panhandle with their Indians, buffaloes and lucrative opportunities, to plunge into that other world that is their very antithesis, the fair land of romance.

CHAPTER  

HE HAD been born in Alabama, when Ben was six years old, and Sher name was Mary Ella Newton. She was seventeen when she came to Sherman in 1869 with her parents, her grandmother

Allen, her sister Jessie and her aunt, Sally Allen. The war had ruined her father financially, and had deprived Aunt Sally of a girlhood sweetheart.

On their slow way west her family had remained for a while in Mississippi. Here they joined four other families and moved out to Texas in a train of five wagons and two carryalls. The trip took a month and was not without the usual ups and downs; a delay was occasioned when one of Mr. Newton's prize mules overate and died of the colic. To find another to help pull the load was not an easy task, but with the energetic assistance of one of the other men in the party a mule was brought in, and the wagon tracks led on to Texas.

Mr. Newton was soon to have the opportunity of demonstrating his appreciation to this fellow voyager, for the latter's party was made up of his wife, his two daughters, and his negro concubine. 

The negress was pregnant, and while it is supposed that her master had counted on reaching Texas in time for her to be delivered there, the long jolting journey made this a foolish hope. The two women were perfectly friendly, and the wife assisted at the birth. 

The rest of the train, however, were thoroughly disgusted with the situation and refused to tarry while the new mother rested for two days; all, that is, except Jesse Newton, who stoutly con-

[76] 

tended that he would not desert a man who had so recently befriended him. They stuck together, and despite the two days lost they caught up with the others before the Texas State Line was reached.

The necessity of getting water forced the travelers to make an early camp just west of Clarksville on a Saturday afternoon. Some

Indians were picking cotton in a nearby field, and after work they relaxed by dancing and whooping around a huge bonfire. Today,

Mary Ella laughingly recounts that Grandmother Allen sat frozen with terror, convinced that they would all be massacred. Eventually, however, one old Indian came over to pay a friendly call, and Grandma Allen's scalp stopped tingling.

In due time the little wagon train pulled into Sherman, and Mary Ella and her family made their home at Randolph's Farm, five miles south of the city. Her aunt and uncle, the Goodes, who had traveled in the caravan, rented a house in town, and it was through them that Mary Ella met Jot Gunter, who had come to 

Sherman with his bride.

Since she is today, in her ninety-fifth year, a fabulously charming woman, it staggers the imagination a bit to think of the effect she must have produced on the young men of Sherman and of 

North Texas who knew her when she was a girl. And even this method of expression fails to do her justice, because the cruelly natural sorrows of a long life have been powerless to change her essential quality. Today, in spite of severe illnesses since her ninetieth year, she will be the dominating and outstanding woman in any group of women who have the good breeding and manners to recognize superlative qualities when they meet them. She will be pre-eminent in such a group, not one whit because of her years, but because she will be the wittiest and most gracious woman there; and her wit and the verbal manifestations of her graciousness will be produced in a voice to be long remembered. It is a

[77] voice that should be recorded and preserved for posterity. She has that rarest of all virtues in good talkers: total attention and adaptation to the person with whom she is conversing. She possesses a memory to put the historian into ecstasies of delight, and through her long life her mind has never been closed but to two things, despair and defeat. She simply, for herself, does not know what these are. And her sweetness in a woman of such indomitable will would disarm and capture the most recalcitrant mortal. Persons of wide information and intelligence make no concessions to her age, but treat her with competitive equality, a method of social approach inherent in which lies the great danger of having very soon to admit her superiority.

There is a tricky fly in this heady and intoxicating ointment: she can, and does, turn on and off her charm at will. She has been observed sitting with a few persons in an atmosphere of ambient warmth, of ease, of good-fellowship, when, suddenly, something would displease her. Then such a bleakness as the northernmost 

Eskimo knows not of instantly fills the room. It is as though the sun had gone into eclipse, or a light turned off, or music stilled; or as though a warm source of benignity had suddenly revealed a frozen countenance of rebuke; or as though contentment had drained away.

She was born, and remains, a guiltless and superb coquette. In 

France she would have been a celebrated salonière, the idol of men of talent and intellect, equally adored by women of more appreciation than envy. In America, in Texas on the frontier, she became a wife and mother, and the mistress of a famously hospitable home. It is no good dwelling on the possibility; we have the facts and actualities of what has been, and is, her chosen career; and this latter is what must content us here. 

Ben and Mary Ella met at a party.

Mary Ella had a friend, Mabel Doss, a music teacher, who lived

[78] 

in Denison in the home of a Mr. and Mrs. Tom Crooks, but who came to Sherman on certain days to give lessons in the latter town. 

One week end Mabel Doss invited Mary Ella to return to Denison with her, and the invitation was accepted. Mabel gave a party for her visitor on Saturday night, and to this affair were invited Ben 

Munson and his sisters, Tryphena Maria and Mary Genevra. They played games, especially card games, and most particularly whist, a game at which Ben was already expert.

Ben and Miss Newton took to each other at once, and during the course of the evening they evidently got around to speaking of their likes and dislikes in the matter of food, for the young lady admitted to being fond of turnips. When the party broke up, Mary Ella went home to stay with the Misses Munson, who were now living in the two-story brick house whose early history we know. Mary Ella had come over expecting to go back the next day. She remained a week. She was encouraged to think she had made a good impression on young Munson - Ben was twentybecause bright and early on Monday morning a bushel of carefully de-earthed, white and purple turnips was delivered to the Munson residence. The sack bore a card: "To Miss Mary 

Ella Newton, with the compliments of Mr. Ben Munson." seven 

This is a perfect "gaslight" anecdote. It has the innocent wholesomeness of the era which, in a small Texas town, corresponds almost exactly to that period in the history of the sidewalks of New York when Al Smith was born. But it has for us a uniqueness that makes it noteworthy. Does one know of any other such highly successful courtship that began under the auspices of the gift of a bushel of home-grown turnips? 

He came to see her often, after she had returned to Sherman.

It was not first-sight love, but it was at least a strong attraction; for they soon got around to explaining to each other that there were obstacles along the path of true love. Ben was already en-

[79] gaged to a girl in Missouri named Liza Sims, and one Joe Moody had been Mary Ella's steady flame.

While Ben was in the awkward position of being engaged to one girl and in love with another, he felt that it was only fair to Mary Ella to insist that she not give up all her beaux for him. They discussed all these matters during the long buggy-rides that they took in Sherman, and in the country roundabout, always driving behind the same gentle old white horse that Ben rented from the local livery stable. They found the horse's gait eminently suited to their endless talks, and also favorable to Ben's reading aloud to Mary Ella the letters that he received from the other woman, Liza Sims. Any consideration of the ethics of this latter procedure is drowned in the roar set up by the application of the adage which has pertinent things to say on fairness in love and war. At any rate a campaign was planned, and since it was a subtle and effective method for the silencing of Liza Sims, it is to be doubted if this campaign was wholly conceived in the head of the young lover. The plan was simplicity itself: Ben was to allow a greater and greater length of time to elapse before he answered the letters from Missouri, until it would be forced in upon the consciousness of Miss Sims that the solitaire diamond she wore on her engagement finger might have meant something at the moment of presentation, but that it had now become the glittering symbol of a waning passion. Miss Sims was an honorable young lady, and when she finally reached the conclusion that Ben no longer cared for her, she did the right thing and sent back the ring. 

The news of this important event came on May 1st, 1876. 

Mary Ella was out in the country near Sherman, picnicking with some friends. During the course of the afternoon the familiar horse and turnout appeared, and on the buggy-seat was a very pleased-looking young man. Mary Ella knew at once that he was the bearer of good news. As soon as he could monopolize her ear

[80] 

and eye he told her, and showed her, that he was not only the bringer of good news, but also the bearer of positive proof: he had the diamond ring. They became formally engaged that day. 

Ben had the good taste and sense to know that the diamond had served its purpose, and that its usefulness to his love affairs was ended. The engagement ring that he gave to Mary Ella was a solitaire pearl. She loved it; but she had the tact not to inquire whether the proceeds from the sale of the diamond had gone into the purchase of the pearl. Although she asked no questions in this connection, she must indeed have wondered, because all during their married life, and especially during the early years when he was laying the solid foundation of his fortune, she kept a keen weather eye on his personal financial affairs, and knew that these first, most productive years were vastly important to their future. 

The course of their love ran smoothly enough, although the two were long in disagreement over a most important proposition: he, naturally enough it seems to us, contended that, since they were engaged, she should allow him to kiss her. She had been brought up to think differently and succeeded in keeping him. for weeks at arms' length. It was a burning question and a point of argument every time he told her good night. But men, as well as women, can play at this game, especially when they are sure that they are loved. Ben finally showed his trump card when he up and told her one night on her father's porch that he was not coming back unless she kissed him good night. She yielded. "Why did you do it?" she was asked. "Why!" she exclaimed in mild impatience for the questioner's being so obtuse as not to recognize that she had been acting on that world-old feminine logic that has had such a great share in assisting the earth's revolving, "I wanted to hold him; I was afraid I would lose him if I didn't."

She held him, all right, in that strongest of all webs that is

[81] woven of the right things in the heart and mind of the one possessed. He never again, as we say, "looked" at another woman. 

And, indeed, why should he?

But they did not know when they could be married. Ben was frequently absent on short trips that summer, one of these being long enough to warrant his writing: 

Weatherford, Texas, August 11th, 1876 

MY DEAREST ELLA: As I have changed the programme of my trip somewhat and am presumptuous enough to believe that you will be interested I will drop you word that my trip will probably be much longer than at first supposed. Should I be delayed by any cause so as not to return in time we can be married by telegraph from Jacksboro or Henrietta & you can go on to the Centennial with Captain G & lady, and I will jump into a river and die happy. Before returning I shall go to Palo Pinto, Stevens, Young, Jack & 

Clay Counties.

I will be black as an Indian by the time I get home so that I expect you will back out as soon as you see me, for I know you will not fancy being called a "Squaw." We have been having plenty of rain on the way so that in crossing a creek this morning our horse almost had to swim with the buggy and we were almost converted to the Baptist doctrine, but fortunately our infidel proclivities prevailed & we were saved for some future "revival."

I feel so lonesome out here without the privilege of your bewitching company I would give anything to exchange companions for you for the balance of this lonely trip. Do not feel uneasy about me as there is little or no danger where I am going & should I come up missing the world is full of better men, who would gladly take my place in your affections.

Wishing you all the pleasure and peace of mind that earth can afford I am as bad as ever 

Your crazy admirer

W. B. MUNSON 

P.S. I wish I had your picture to waste my spare kisses upon in the absence of which I waft one to you on the gentle breeze and bid it cling to your lips until relieved by another. Sweetness!! 

Bye Bye

[82] 

She was glad now that she had yielded on the question of the good-night kiss, and she had no slightest idea of having any but a proper wedding. She made this clear to him when he returned some time later, and sent him away again on another business trip, this time to a more civilized part of the State, though one not without its own special kind of potential danger. 

HUTCHINS HOUSE J. P. HORBACH, PROPRIETOR; JAMES CHRISTIE, CLERK HOUSTON, TEXAS 

over 

August 27th, 1876 

MY DEAR ELLA: If you will excuse this business paper and envelope and consider them fitting messengers of love I will be happy and tell you what I have been doing since I left you. After a very dusty and tiresome ride I reached Austin at 6 o'clock Friday evening and found it as gay and attractive as ever. The first thing in order, after getting supper, was of course to call on the Gay Widow. Not finding her at home I made an appointment with her mother for the next evening or afternoon, when after completing a hard day's work I enjoyed the pleasure of seeing again her charming face. 

You may not believe me but I tell you the sight was almost sufficient to make me wish I lived in Mormondom. She sends her love and a kiss by me to her friend Miss Mollie D. neither of which I am much inclined to deliver (Well I might deliver the kiss.) Well enough nonsense. I met here this morning the gentleman whom I expected to have to visit at Palestine thus shortening my trip a day or two. I will go to Galveston this evening and leave there for Sherman tomorrow evening and be at home either 

Tuesday or Wednesday so that if you still prefer we can start North either

Thursday or Friday (Friday preferred) and thus put a stop to my widow wandering fancies. My trip has been very pleasant so far and I hope will yet prove profitable in a business point of view. I wish you were with me today and we could take a boat ride this afternoon on the Gulf, and dine this evening on her celebrated fish. I fear that I shall become so sentimental while brooding around without you as to jump into the Gulf just in order to make sure of a happy death and a hereafter filled with the happy dreams

[83] of matrimony. I hope you will not take exception to my calling on the widow for it was just as natural for me as was your preference for Mr. 

Moody's company when you knew I was almost dying to see you. And besides I am bound to keep even with you and enjoy the benefit of the last flirtation. There are but one or two ladies in Galveston with whom I am acquainted but I shall do my best to find them while down. Well believing that by this time you are mad enough to look bewitching I will stop ere you become too “Moody” to suit my taste. Hoping that if you get mad at my joking I shall not find you "Moody” on my return I am as ever 

Your own true and devoted lover,

W. B. MUNSON 

P.S. Please excuse this terrible scribbling and accept a floating kiss.

The great day came at last, but the wedding was performed with a minimum of festivities. It took place on Sunday morning, September 3rd, 1876, at eight-thirty, in Ella Newton's home. Dr. J. C. 

Carpenter, a Baptist minister and friend of Ben's, read the services;

Ella's aunt, Sally Allen, was there, and the only outsiders were 

Mr. and Mrs. Jot Gunter and their daughter, Eula, who was six years old at the time. (Eula later married Mr. Hardie, but died before her time, after having become the mother of three children, Gunter, Esther, and Jack, who were reared by their grandmother, Mrs. Jot Gunter in San Antonio.)

Since it had been the newlyweds' wish that their friends, the 

Gunters, accompany them on their wedding trip, there were two surreys (yes, with the fringe on the top) at the door awaiting the exit of the bride and groom. In this case the old white horse was not good enough, and spanking teams of horses drew them over the low hills rolling toward Red River and the terminus of the railroad. 

Mary Ella laughingly recounts: "So awed were Ben and I at the idea of embarking on a future together that neither of us had

BEN AND MARY ELLA ABOUT THE TIME OF THEIR MARRIAGE

[85] the breath to speak, so we rode the entire distance between Denison and Sherman clasping hands and not uttering a word. Jennie and Trite had a real wedding luncheon ready for us at the brick house, and when we boarded the train for Chicago part of our luggage included an enormous lunch basket that the girls had fixed up, including every delicacy imaginable and several bottles of wine. "In Chicago we stayed at the Palmer House, which was new then and beautifully appointed. I suppose we looked as happy as we felt, for as he was handing Ben the pen to register, the clerk was offering us the bridal suite, ‘all done' he said, 'in green satin!' 

Bridal suite indeed! Neither of us wanted to have anything to do with a bridal suite, so we turned him down. We did the Centennial at Philadelphia and visited Baltimore, Niagara Falls and New 

York City. It was a lovely trip!"

The Gunters went on to Ann Arbor to place their nephew in school there, and Ben and Mary Ella went to Astoria, Illinois, to visit Ben's parents. On their return to Texas they rode in one of the first "sleepers" - a converted day-coach- and even this part of the trip was not without excitement. Jennie, after waving Ben and Mary Ella off to Chicago, decided to pay her parents a visit in Illinois, and not only did she elect to return to the Lone Star State with her brother and his wife, but after boarding the train she began to have qualms about the curtains of the sleeper providing adequate protection for her maidenly virtue; hence she shared the bride's berth, and the groom slept alone. "Jennie," said Mary Ella, "you know if this train should happen to hit something or be in a wreck of any kind, I believe the best thing for us to do would be to grab hold of this stout pole they have running along here to hold the curtains up." 

Before Jennie had a chance to reply, the train, apparently wearied with the chatter of nervous females, gave a mighty groan

[86] 

and ever so gently rolled over on its side. The passengers were relatively calm until our groom, jolted out of a sound slumber, ran to the rescue of his bride. He landed in the midst of two strange females and the confusion was great. Women were squawking, Ben was cursing, and everyone else was shouting: "Who's hurt? What's the matter?"

They spent a month on their honeymoon, and when they returned from it in October of that same year, '76, they settled down in the big house that Ben had built for himself and his sisters. 

Eight rooms were available to Ben's family. Downstairs there were first and second parlors, and Ben's and Ella's bedroom was also on the first floor. It was in this downstairs room that Ella would sit in a low chair next to the window, night after night, waiting for her husband to return from Sherman. He made the trip both ways on freight trains, and the schedules of these were so irregular that she scarcely ever knew when to expect him. She had a real fear and need for his protection. The Indian Territory, a few miles to the north, across the Red River, was dry; and the 

Indians, male and female, came down to Denison for their liquor. 

The Munson home was only a few hundred yards from the center of town, and on quiet nights she could hear the shouting and carousing in the town's twenty-five saloons. The Indians, sober on their way in and gloriously drunk on their way out, streamed past the house all night long, as Mary Ella, conscious that Time facetiously was clothing minutes in the guise of hours, sat by the window frozen with apprehension, watching for Ben's appearance. 

Ben had thought, apparently, even when he was away, to divert her attention, for, from the very first months of their marriage, he recommended a course of reading to his wife. Whatever his thoughts or reasons, the following letter is amusing.

OFFICE OF OUNTER A MUNSON, 

Attorneys at Lane & Dealers in Real Estate,

YOURS IN HASTE

[88] 

 Office of GUNTER AND MUNSON ATTORNEYS AT LAW & DEALERS IN REAL ESTATE 

Sherman, Texas, Nov. 28th 1876 

MY DEAR ELLA: I am compeled to start to Denton County this morning to be gone three days. Will try and be at home Thursday evening on 8 or 9 oclock train. I hope you may be able to amuse yourself reading Geology, Mineralogy, Paleontology, and Archaology and such other apology as you can find for nothing to do. Do not be uneasy as I am not going to Jack* & where there is any danger (Smack) 

Your loving husband

W. B. MUNSON 

This letter refers to the first book he had made her a present of after their marriage. It was Forms of Water, by John Tyndall, LL.D., F.R.S., Volume I of a fifty-volume series called The International Scientific Series, published by D. Appleton & Co., 1,3,5,

Bond Street, New York. This series had been a great favorite of the Munsons back in Illinois, and after Ben came to Texas he purchased the remaining volumes to complete the set. 

What a book to give a bride! The young husband cannot have been wholly serious in handing her such a book. We know that he was a great jokester, and it is likely that there was more than a touch of his humor not only in the mere fact of his giving her the book, but also in that he actually "assigned" her so many pages to read while he would be away in Sherman, or on longer business trips. At any event, she cried her eyes out over the whole business because she was not familiar with the scientific terms. She did not even finish the first volume. It floored her. Moreover, by early spring of the following year, '77, she had the best possible excuse for not taxing her mind and strength with reading that would prove a burden to both: she was expecting her first child.

It was then that Mary Ella complained in no uncertain terms ⚫ Jack County.

[89] that the drunken Indians who went shouting by late every night made her nervous. "I don't care, Ben, what you say, nor how much anyone else storms; you know perfectly well that you have to be in Sherman as much as in Denison; and when you're away for weeks and months at a time on your surveying trips, I'd be safer there. It's not necessary for us both to be fighting savages, and by George I refuse to do it!”

They removed to Sherman, where they bought a house that had belonged to Dr. Martin Kennedy, on Rusk Street, next door to the 

Jot Gunters. After a year in the house as it was, they added four rooms, two up and two down, and settled down happily in it to remain there until 1883. To them, in this house, were born the first three children, Linley in '77, Maud in '79, and Vida in '81. 

For one of the years between '76 and '83, Jennie, Ben's youngest sister, lived with Ben and Ella in Sherman; and Tryphena spent a good part of the time with her brother, T.V., in the country. 

In the meantime, during the year 1878-79, Ben's mother, who had left Illinois before her husband, spent a year with her son and his wife in Sherman.

And, as his correspondence will indicate, Ben's travels continued.

OFFICE OF DISTRICT AND COUNTY CLERK CLAY COUNTY, TEXAS G. W. ALEXANDER, CLERK THE FOLLOWING COUNTIES ARE ATTACHED TO CLAY FOR JUDICIAL PURPOSES: 

WICHITA, WILBARGER, HARDEMAN, ARCHER, BAYLOR, KNOX, COLLINGSWORTH, DONLEY, GRAY, WHEELER, ROBERTS, HEMPHILL, LIPSCOMB, HALL, OCHILTREE, AND CHILDRESS 

Henrietta, Texas, April 21, 1877 

MY DEAR ELLA: You will doubtless be looking for me ere you receive this, as I have been detained longer now than I expected to be when I left home. 

The surveyor has been absent from his office now for 20 days, and is ex-

[90] 

pected back tomorrow; so I will wait and see him, thus avoiding a second trip here. I have just returned from a ride out to the edge of Wichita 

County, about 20 miles west of here, having stayed all night last night with a farmer who lives on the banks of the Big Wichita River. Examined a fine tract of land which I think we can capture. But you ought to have seen the deer that were grazing on it. They were in herds like cattle, and almost as tame. I could have killed them with my pistol if I had tried to do so. may be detained here two or three days yet, but will come home as soon as I can get my business in proper shape. I hope you are enjoying yourself, and taking good care of the "Prospective One." You must be very careful about overwork and overconfinement without exercise and fresh air. Excuse brevity. Accept love and imagine my kisses. Good bye darling. 

Lovingly, BEN 

In August of '83 Mary Ella's fourth child was due. Ben had arranged his affairs so that he would be home during the time of her confinement. However, the days wore into weeks, and the month was fast slipping by. Ben continued to receive word that someone was "mavericking" half the calves of a herd he had pastured in the Panhandle. Mary Ella, sensing the tension he was under, finally exploded: "For heaven's sake, Ben, this isn't my first baby, it's the fourth.

I'll get word to you when the baby comes, and with an increasing family you should be tending to your business instead of sitting here like a bewildered midwife." 

Ben left.

August passed into September, and on the 18th of that month, Price, the white woman nurse, allowed baby Vida, then in her third year, to take off her shoes and stockings and swing, after she had become overheated from play. The child became very ill immediately afterwards with what was diagnosed as cholera infantum. Four days later, on the 22nd, she slipped into a coma and kept repeating: "I love my mama, I love my mama, I love my mama." She died at midnight, and before morning the fourth baby was born.

[91] A messenger rode a hundred miles on horseback to get the telegram through to Ben. This wire had been composed by Jot Gunter, who had remained at Mary Ella's side throughout the nightmarish experience. (Mrs. Gunter and Eula were away on a summer trip.) In the same message he urged Ben to remain and finish up the business he was on, as both Mary Ella and the new baby were all right, and if he left now he would only have to return again in a few days' time. 

Ben wired back his heartbreak and what comfort he could muster. He also wrote immediately.

Fort Reno (Indian Territory) Sept. 27, 1883 

MY DEAR ELLA: It is hard for me to remain away so long while you are having so much trouble at home. I shall be very uneasy until I hear from you, but I hope you will not make yourself sick worrying over your trouble.

Sweet little Vida is gone, but we still have plenty to live for, and no amount of weeping will help poor Vida now. I would like to hear how she came to die so suddenly, and what the character of her sickness was. Did she suffer much during her sickness? How is Jessie getting? Has she entirely recovered from the flux? I hope to hear from you when I get to Elliott or Mobeetie.

If I do not get a letter there, I may telegraph Summerfield to let me know how you are getting along. Should either of the other children get sick, have Summerfield telegraph me at Mobeetie, so I may come home. 

The weather is dry and cool now, and it will be pleasant traveling from here to the ranch. Everything pertaining to business is going well. Take good care of yourself. Tell Linley and Maud that Papa feels so sorry for them that he cannot write to them now. Kiss them often for me, and don't let them be sick without letting me know. 

Accept love and kisses for yourself. Write me often, as I will be uneasy. 

Yours affectionately, BEN.

It was no mere façon de parler on his part when he asked his wife to tell the two other little girls that he felt so sorry for them that he could not write them then. He was not only an extremely sympathetic and tender man but, since he had not been able to

[92] 

accept the Christian version of the afterlife, he felt a more than organic connection with his children, seeing in them a proof of his own immortality in which he could believe, and which no one could deny. In later years he was to refer to his children as "acorns" who had had the origins of their beings in the "oak." 

He could scarcely bear to see one of his children ill, so greatly did he suffer through them, and even a mild case of sick stomach in one of them produced in him immediately almost identical symptoms. This was certainly not squeamishness on his part, for Bill 

Green, his frequent fishing companion, reports that once, in Colorado, Ben pulled back through the flesh of his hand the barb of a three-barbed fishhook that had been accidentally run through the solid tissues. He did this, moreover, without turning a hair, if we except a justifiable string of mild oaths on the occasion. This sense of physical identity with his children was something entirely different; it was almost mystical.

Mary Ella had Vida's body placed in a vault in Sherman, postponing the burial until Ben could reach home. When he did arrive, Ben clung to Mary Ella and sobbed while she, choking back her own tears, gave a halting account of Vida's passing. It was decided that she would be the first Munson interred in the family plot at Denison, and Ben himself wrote the inscription that was carved on her tombstone. 

Oh! she was beautiful, loving and bright 

So like a rosebud just starting to bloom 

Suddenly nipped on that terrible night 

Sweet little Vida now sleeps in the tomb.

The naming of the new little sister also awaited Ben's return, for he more or less exacted the privilege of selecting a name for each of his children. His thoughts on Vida, he was toying with one of the baby's tiny hands when he remarked quite casually: "Perhaps we should call her Marble."

[93] "Ben!" gasped Mary Ella, "that's horrible; I could never call a child of mine Marble." “Then I want a name as nearly like 'Vida' as possible. Vida, Vida, oh Vida!"

And as though the lost baby herself had suddenly come running into the room, his face lit up and with a joyful ring to his voice he turned to Mary Ella. "I have it! We'll call this baby Theda, you see? Vida, Theda; we'll spell it T-H-E-D-A, and we can tell J.T. that we named her for him, Theda for Theodore." "Yes, that's a fine name," Mary Ella smiled; "so soft and musical."

The next year, July 3rd, 1884, they moved back to Denison, Ben and Ella making the trip overland; again behind a span of horses that "cut up" on the road. This time when they returned to the old house it was all theirs; and with the growing family they needed all the room it afforded, and more besides. 

Now, truly, all of Ben's family ties were in the "Gate City"; for when old William Munson moved to Denison in '78, he bought land near what is now the entrance to Munson Park, and built a white frame two-story house there.
