The ground was rocky, and though Maria Linley was now approaching seventy, she herself removed nearly every rock on the hill. She made formal flower gardens edged with brick, and under her green fingers they flourished into a riotous beauty. She had a hothouse to shelter her delicate plants, and the large bay window in her bedroom was always filled with prize begonias. Jennie and Trite lived with their parents here, and the little group was happy for some years in their new home, until an insidious illness began to sap Maria's strength. She grew steadily worse, and when her suffering became unbearable Ben and Mary Ella took William Munson into their home, trying to spare him all that they could. Two years her

[94] 

senior, he himself had high blood pressure, and quite suddenly, on the afternoon of November 27, 1890, he started to cry softly. "Ellie, Ellie," he called; "what's wrong with Maria?" (Ellie was his pet name for Mary Ella.) "Why what do you mean, Grandpa? You know she's been ill for a long time now." "Yes, but it's something else," he whimpered; "all's not right on the hill. Take me home; take me to Maria, I want Maria!" 

Maria was all right. 

Her suffering had come to an end. Eight days later, on December 5th, 1890, the shadow-shrouded Stranger tapped her husband on the shoulder. "All right, William," he said softly; "I suppose you have reached the stage in life where we can afford to indulge the whims of an old man. Come along; I'll take you to Maria."

In the meantime life and business had to go on. The year of his marriage, but several months before that important date, Ben had had the idea that was to develop into what was to be known as the Corwith Pool. This Pool, briefly, was an agreement between 

Gunter & Munson on the one part, and several capitalists of Illinois on the other. The original agreement was made on January 

1st, 1876, and the Illinois men were Nathan Corwith, John E. 

Corwith, John Ross, and Robert McClellan.

The Illinois capitalists put up $75,000 with which to purchase 

Texas land certificates, while Gunter & Munson undertook to do the actual purchasing of the certificates, locating and surveying the land, and having it patented. The expenses incurred by the two Texas men were to come out of the money of the original outlay. It was agreed that, if at the end of two years there were not enough profits to reimburse the Illinois men, the certificates and land held by the Pool were to be divided on the basis that Gunter & Munson were to have one-fourth, and the other men the re-

[95] mainder. Clear profits were to be shared in the same way. It was understood that the men who put up the money could, if they felt that two years had not afforded them sufficient operational time, extend the agreement indefinitely. This they did, for the 

Pool was fabulously successful. It is no exaggeration to say that millions of acres were handled by the Pool. Much of it was bought in at sixty dollars a section, and sold at from fifty cents to a dollar and a half an acre. The Pool lasted for thirty years, as the final transactions within the framework of the Pool did not take place until 1906 — although, in the meantime, Mr. Gunter had transferred his share in the Pool to Ben, in payment of a private obligation. Somewhat more than 3000 acres were involved in this latter deal. 

The actual contact between Ben and the Illinois bankers came about in a natural but interesting way. In Texas Ben met a school teacher from Galena, Illinois, who told Ben that there were men in his home state who had so much idle money that they did not know where or how to invest it. Ben knew both. Armed with a letter of introduction from this acquaintance, Ben made the trip back to Chicago, contacted the men mentioned above, and the Pool was the direct outgrowth of this meeting, of Ben's idea, and of Ben's trip.

CHAPTER  

ADDITION to the children already mentioned, two more were born to the union: Ben's son and namesake in 1886, and the youngest child, Eloise, in 1889.

Ben had now definitely and permanently settled in Denison, and throughout his life continued to identify himself primarily with the life and prosperity of the town. Business, enterprise, investment, organization, promotion, were the very breath of his extra-family life; but although he frequently entertained his business friends and acquaintances in his home, he knew that the two worlds should be kept apart, and this he succeeded in doing. He worked hard, relaxed with great singleness of mind, and had the power - especially at home - of practically total concentration. 

He manifested this latter quality particularly in games and reading. 

For a number of years prior to 1912 there was much talk in the family of a new house. "Ella," Ben told his wife, "I want you to have the finest home in Grayson County; you deserve it, and I want you to have it."

But the architect who drew up and submitted the final and accepted plans had to work against the active opposition of the Munson girls. Across the street from the old brick home was a boardinghouse that was made lively and attractive by the patronage of many of the town's most eligible young bachelors. The majority of these were employed by the Waples-Platter Grocery Co., a wholesale house, and it was their pleasant habit to run in

[97] on the Munson family in the late afternoons, and during the evenings. 

The girls were quite naturally apprehensive that if they were to move so far "out" they would be deprived of a good deal of these young men's society. "Papa," they told Ben, “we like living close to town; we don't want to move so far away from things."

Ben had a clear idea of what "things" were, and, furthermore, the business in hand pressed so hard upon him that he had to renounce, for the present, any plans that he had for going ahead with the building of the new home. 

Finally, however, in 1912, construction got under way on a large plot of ground on West Morton Street, and the home was completed in 1913. "Things," however, apparently did not find the distance an insuperable difficulty, for the Munson home immediately occupied a prominent place in the social life of the town.

It is a house that is not only a great tribute to the builders, but is also a splendid exemplification of the kind of people they were and are. It is built of solid brick, and no expense was spared to give it the solidity of permanence, and quality of finish and fittings. It is, of course, a house that an architect would shrink from designing at the present time; he would also do his best to keep his client from building such a residence. And it is precisely these two related facts that make this an important house: it is a monument to an era, an era that believed in comfort, elegance, plenty of room; that gave no thought to what we now think of as "efficiency" as it is popularly and architecturally understood today, and was certain that servants would never become a problem. 

It is curious that a man who insisted on personally naming all his children save the last, should have left the designing of his home almost entirely to his wife. He saw to the character of the

[98] 

construction and the materials that went into it; he was especially careful about the foundation. But Ella was to have it as she wanted it. When it was done he made a devastating comment. "Anyone at all can see, Ella," he told her half teasingly, “that you have designed this house for just one thing — entertaining.” "Well, don't you want me to entertain?" 

"Of course I do; I want you to entertain and have parties in it to your heart's content."

This urging and encouragement were unnecessary, as she is the very soul of hospitality. 

After Gunter & Munson had cleaned up a fortune in the land business in general, and especially in the Corwith Pool, they turned their talents and attention to the cattle business. They bought cattle in the seventies for six and eight dollars a head, kept the breeders, and sold off some of the increase while the business was booming. By 1883 cattle had advanced in price to twenty or twenty-five dollars, and by that year they had accumulated more than 30,000 head.* These cattle had been bought in small lots all over West Texas and, depending on the season, had been driven to one of the two large ranches. The partners controlled over 400,000 acres in what was known as the summer ranch, and 480,000 acres in a different section that must have been spoken of as the winter ranch.

Everybody was trying to get into the cattle business, and the tactics of the already established cattlemen served merely to add to the excitement and mad scramble. The cattlemen believed that cattle would remain high, and as a consequence of this belief sold only a limited quantity of beef, keeping the cows as long as they would prove profitable as breeders. 

*The Munson & Gunter cattle brands were as follows: 

Ğ G GMS on both sides on right side. on left shoulder, side and hip.

[99] But Ben saw that these methods must ultimately lead to the overstocking of the available ranges, and that great numbers of cattle were likely to be dumped on the market at nearly the same time, thus ruining the price and causing a stampede to unload. He went very carefully into this - which he considered an inevitable development - with his partner, Mr. Gunter, and it was Ben's advice that they quietly begin to pull in their horns on their stock. 

Mr. Gunter, who seems to have loved the cattle business just for itself, figured that if a cow was worth twenty-five dollars she would eventually be worth fifty dollars because of the value of her potential calves.

On this rock of disagreement the two men split, and it was decided to dissolve the partnership. This they did, nominally, in 

1883. But the dissolution of the partnership had no effect on the high personal regard the two men had for each other, and they remained close friends until Jot Gunter's death in San Antonio in 1910. Moreover, the dissolving of the partnership was purely nominal in many respects, for although they did have what might be called a settlement in gross, it was many years before it could be said that their business interests were really and wholly separate.

The method they chose for the severance of the partnership was simplicity itself: they merely put up for sale the two largest properties that they jointly owned. These properties were of unequal size and value. The first was a Grayson County ranch of 12,000 acres, partially stocked, and the other was the Panhandle ranch containing 480,000 acres under fence. The two separating partners bid against each other on these properties (Mary Ella says they tossed a coin), and the result was that the Grayson County ranch went to Mr. Gunter for $150,000, and Ben bought in the 

Panhandle ranch at $650,000. Ben adjusted the discrepancy in the value of these two properties by assuming the whole outstanding

THE NEW HOUSE IN DENISON

[101] indebtedness of the firm, and by giving a note to Mr. Gunter for the balance. Of the Panhandle ranch Ben acquired 400,000 acres with a clear title to himself, and the other 80,000 represented a long-term and renewable lease. 

There was a valuable piece of virgin land in the Panhandle that Ben had long wanted to incorporate with his other holdings there because it had water on it. He had arranged a code system with the legal firm of Maddox Bros. & Anderson, in Austin. (The Maddox brothers were originally Sherman men; in fact one of them, in his courtship days, had been counted among Mary Ella's beaux.) The code permitted the firm to wire Ben when the choice bit of land was put up for public sale. The wire came, but to be the first one to reach the land and file on it was quite another matter. Denison was hours farther distant from this prize than was Austin, but this was one time when Ben's beloved railroads repaid him in full. He secured an engine and one boxcar, cornered an engineer, promised him a hundred-dollar bill to make the trip and, measuring him carefully, inquired: "How much speed can you get out of that steam engine?" "Oh, 'bout fifty miles an hour when I'm sober; sixty to seventy if I'm drunk enough." "That's what I figured. Here's the whiskey; let's get going."

It was now Ben who was being measured, with an eye that hinted at disbelief. "It'll be awful risky." "I know; come on!" 

The only thing that traveled faster than Ben's train that day was the rumor that an engine pulling a single boxcar was burning the ties with a one-way ticket to Hell. up "Whoever's pushing that thing down the track is either drunk or crazy."

Ben filed his claim, and was sitting with his feet propped up

[102] 

perusing a newspaper three hours ahead of the general rush. It took no more than a side glance from any man to ascertain that here was the cat that had swallowed the canary.

After this Ben immediately set to work in an effort to dispose of the ranch, together with all its stock and equipment. If he could not dispose of it in one block, he would break it up and sell it that way. 

David T. Beals, Esq., P.O. Box 445, Chicago, Ill. 

Denison, Texas, Sept. 12, 1884 

DEAR SIR: Your favor of the 10th inst. at hand. As some other parties have asked me for prices on my ranch, I would not care to cut the prices to you at present; but if you can make me an offer I will consider it. My property consists of one hundred thousand acres of land owned in fee. One hundred and seventy-nine thousand, two hundred acres leased for six years, from 

January 1st next, at 112 cents per acre per annum. Two hundred and twenty-five thousand acres practically fenced, though not entirely surrounded by fence, constructed so as to come under the prohibitory legislation regarding school land; and about twenty-one thousand cattle, including this year's calves, and excluding beef drive this year. . . . 

Yours very truly, W. B. MUNSON

There is an interesting letter to Mr. W. M. D. Lee, of Leavenworth, Kansas, eloquent of one phase of the cattleman's troubles; and speaking likewise in testimony of this one's character. 

DEAR SIR: Your favor of August 31st is at hand.

It is strange that the outfit should persist in upholding the thieving elements. Harry would have left the country if they had not stood by him, and the thieves would have had that much less backing. Mr. Isaacs [Ben's foreman on the big ranch] is now on the way with beef, and I cannot attend to the matter you suggest until late in the fall. I don't know that we can convict Harry, but think we can, unless he gets some of the outfit he was with to swear him out of it. What was done with Harry? Will you be able to convict him? I very much fear that the range will be this winter. 

Keep me posted.

[ ] In the meantime, on July 9th of the same year, he had written to George Isaacs, the foreman, a man for whom he had great respect, and in whose integrity he trusted. 

DEAR GEORGE: I wish you to send a team and buggy to Clarendon by the 

22nd of this month, and leave it at the livery-stable if I am not there. I will bring a man with me to look at beef, and will want the buggy to take him out. Put in the blanket I left with you. I hope you will get ranch work through by the 10th to the 15th of August at the latest, so as to be ready to handle beef. If I sell beef, the party will probably want me to drive them to the railroad.

Get the steers out of the home pasture as clean as possible; also the stock cattle, so that we can get clean work branding up the calves (about seven thousand calves) this fall as we put the cattle back, which will not be done until quite late.

Should anything turn up to keep me from coming out, I will telegraph you at Clarendon, so you can take the buggy back, or have it done. Hoping all is going well, I am,

Ben was now, since the breaking up of the Gunter & Munson partnership, in partnership with his brother, J. T. Munson; and much of the correspondence that is of interest was carried on in the name of J.T. for his brother, as Ben was so frequently and lengthily away from home. There is a letter of September 3rd, '84, to Leon & H. Blum, of Galveston, Texas, signed by J.T. for Munson & Bro.

DEAR SIRS: We have about two hundred thousand acres of land for sale south of, and adjoining, your lands in Randall County; ours being mostly in Swisher and Castro Counties. We think a cash offer of 75cts per acre would get this land. . . .

To judge from the last sentence of the above letter, this was land that the brothers were trying to dispose of as agents. On the 

23rd of June, the same year, Ben had written to Mr. Gunter, in 

Sherman, that he needed money at once, and he underlined the sentence that voiced his necessity. There was a national stringency that had him worried, and a few days later, after he had written

[104] 

to Gunter, he wrote to Mr. Scullin in St. Louis, to know if the latter saw any signs in the larger city of a letup in the financial squeeze. 

But Ben's confidence in his town and in the future of the country was not shaken, for, on the same day that he wrote to Mr. 

Scullin, he had taken a contract from the City of Denison - he spoke of it as a "charter" - to build a street railway system for the town. He had been in St. Louis not long before this, and at that time he had evidently had some talk with Scullin about rails to supply the system. 

At what price could you deliver street-car rails? Are they badly worn, or would they make a good road? How many tons are required to the mile of track? About what would be the cost of construction, including ties, etc on such streets as we have in Denison?

Ben was not to build the streetcar lines. He sold the franchise which he had secured to Rice and Ford, and it was the latter firm that was to give to Denison that public utility without which no town could lay any claims to being a city. 

A week after the street railway deal, he wrote to the general manager of the Atchison, Topeka, and Santa Fe.

As a citizen of Denison, I am interested in ascertaining the intentions of your Company in relation to building the R.R. through the Indian Territory to this city. The citizens of Denison feel a deep interest in this matter, and will be inclined to exert themselves to facilitate the early construction of the road to this point. If you should determine to build at once, and should find it to your advantage to build any part of the road from this end of the line, I might bid on the construction of the portion on this side of the river.

This is not only the letter of a public-spirited man, constantly on the lookout to better his home community, but also the reappearance of the young fellow who had had his first taste of business and solid money more than ten years before this, back in Illinois, when he had undertaken a similar job for the railroad,

[105] building down through his home county in the direction of 

St. Louis. 

D. G. Jones, Esq. c/o Missouri Republican, St. Louis, Mo. 

DEAR SIR: Yours of September 1st at hand. I never contracted for the one thousand copies of Missouri Republican. The article contained in the papers left here appears to be chiefly composed of personal puffs of individuals and firms in Denison, instead of being a first class article showing the advantages of Denison as a place of business enterprises and . . . manufactories, as promised by the young man who claimed to be the agent of the "Morning Call." I consider the article of no value to Denison, and will not circulate it. In short, I think the whole thing is a fizzle, and don't feel like paying for something for which I did not contract. The papers are here, as I have no use for them.

This letter tells almost a complete story. Ben was sincerely interested in attracting new businesses and enterprises to Denison and he was, as he states, willing to pay for a good comprehensive write-up of the town in the St. Louis paper. But, since he was totally devoid of personal and professional vanity, he refused to be sucked in on any such scheme as the proposition had evidently turned out to be. We are all familiar with the very thing for which he had no respect: the blown-up account of a town dry-goods store to make it sound like a big city emporium, with large pictures of the owners and managers, and smaller pictures of all the staff. It is a safe bet that Ben stuck by his guns in this deal, for, when he was convinced that he had right on his side, he was immovable. Witness the following letter, of December 9th, the same year, '84. 

Messrs. Bullock and Baker, Abilene, Kansas 

DEAR SIRS: Your favor of Nov. 30th before me. I am sorry and surprised to learn that you have lost nine head of the cattle. If these cattle really had fever, it must have been contracted in the cars or yards at Kansas City. In

[106] 

regard to my bearing the loss, I think you take rather a strange view of it. 

If I had insured the cattle to live, you could probably have claimed the loss, but cattle sold in the open market are sold without guarantee, unless otherwise specified. I told you where the cattle were from, and how they had been handled, and I would have been willing to have guaranteed them not to die of fever for a small consideration, had you asked me to do so. If, under the laws or business rules of selling cattle in Kansas City, I am liable, of course I will pay it. If I am not, I don't think you, as business men, would ask it. 

If you had paid cash for the cattle, buying them of a comm. man direct, would you or could you exact damages? It will be easy to arrive at the legal or business liability, if any. I, of course, knew nothing of any disease having affected the cattle, and it might be that the disease of which they died was not fever. Might they not have eaten too much corn, or some other accident have caused their death? I had every reason to believe that the cattle would not be affected by fever, and can hardly understand how they could have had fever, now as heavy frosts had preceded their contact with the places through which cattle had been. Whatever is right and lawful I will do in the matter.

This is an eminently reasonable letter and, as much as or more than any other of his business communications, shows not only his personal character, but also his business ethics. 

In March of the following year he is dickering on a land deal difficult of definite identification - wherein he voices his willingness to take a commission of $20,000, rather than the $25,000 he had been holding out for. In the same month, same year, he is offering to sell to Jno. Montgomery, of Denver, a ranch in Clay 

County of 116,000 acres, and 25,000 head of cattle “at a great bargain." This man saw, thought, and traded big; he went straight to the point. The two letters connected with these two trades take up less than ten lines.

But he could be canny, and even sarcastic. It is easy to imagine how taken aback must have been the recipient of the following letter, written in April of '85: 

Yours of the 12th inst. at hand. I do not now care particularly to buy your land, but if it is any accommodation to you I will make you an offer

[107] of $1.00 per acre for it, provided the title is all right. I do not care to lease at any price. I have moved my cattle out of the range where your land is located, and have abandoned it, as there were too many land-owners who, like yourself, thought they could rob somebody because the State passed a foolish law.

I would be glad if you would come to Texas and go to Austin, so as to satisfy yourself that the efforts you are making to bulldoze, or blackmail, are supremely ridiculous. I hold no offer open for any length of time, but will now take your land at price stated.

There is no discoverable record of a reply to this letter. But Ben must have been able to get hold of some of the money he had been needing, for he was pretty indifferent to business propositions during that whole spring and summer. To J. P. Douglas, of Tyler, in August of that same year, he wrote:

I am just back from the West, and will start north on a resting trip tomorrow. Am worn out and roasted, until I must have a rest. Will probably be at Spirit Lake, Iowa, for the next thirty days. Would have been glad to talk with you, but doubt whether any scheme involving much business worry would strike my fancy. You might confer with my brother, and if he thinks well of your scheme, he can write me details. I will be back about 

September 30th, and will then be prepared for business.

This letter is important for its revelation of his state of mind and his, at least temporary, satisfaction with his finances; also for the confidence which he always had in his brother, J.T. This letter is interesting, further, because there is an anecdote connected with the Spirit Lake, Iowa, trip.

Ben was a great fisherman, and he went to Spirit Lake primarily to fish for pickerel and to have, as he explained, a much-needed rest from work, and relief from the North Texas heat which he always hated with savage intensity. He took Mary Ella and the three little girls with him and the family settled themselves for a good stay. But business, and business cares, pursued him even there; they had been on the Lake only a short while when Ben received an imperious message to hurry to New York in connec-

[108] 

tion with a deal he was trying to swing to have a new railroad built into Denison.

They packed hurriedly and piled everything into a small sailboat which was to take them across the Lake to the railroad station on the farther shore. In the very middle of the Lake a violent storm came up and Mrs. Munson thought they would all be lost. Her concern was justified, for so intense was the wind that, in her own words, "the boat was standing on its sides. It literally had no bottom, and one could not possibly stand up or attempt to move any direction." in "Can't you manage the boat any better than this?" she shouted to the sailor, who was exerting every energy to keep them from capsizing. 

"I'm doin' my best, Ma'am," he shouted back; "this is the worst storm I've ever seen." "How long does that mean you've been sailing a boat?" "Two years, Ma'am; I used to be a foreman on the Munson & 

Gunter Ranch." "Oh my Father!" snapped Mary Ella; "now I know we'll all land in the bottom of the lake." 

Perhaps cowpunchers make better ports in a storm than she realized. Their profession demands toughness and certainly patience, and one can imagine that, between his patient, polite replies to this irate mother with three terrified little girls clinging to her skirts, he was carrying on a very fervent dialogue with his God; for now she was shouting to him that his passengers were the Munson end of the Munson & Gunter Ranch! What she did not tell him, and what we assume was not yet evident, even to a practised eye, was that another Munson was on the way. That particular lack of information was all that kept the cowboy-sailor from giving up completely, and allowing the waves to stampede the little boat into oblivion.

[109] They made the opposite shore, but the boat was being tossed so violently that it was impossible to get the children off except by picking them up and throwing them through the air into waiting arms on the dock, a hazardous expedient in itself, and one that made such a vivid impression on the mind of eight-year-old Linley that, in later years, she was able to give she was able to give an accurate account of it to her own children. She carried to her death an immense dread of water.

In Chicago Ben was to catch a train for New York, and had wired for reservations from Chicago to Denison for Mary Ella and the children. However, in Chicago his connection was so close that he had to run, and run hard, to catch his own train as it was pulling out. He had time only to shout back to his brood: "Go to the ticket window and pick up your reservations; they're there waiting for you; you'll be all right." All right they would be under the vigilant and sensible eye of Mary Ella, but the ticket agent himself delivered yet another blow to the buffeted group when he said, "Yes; we have Mr. Munson's wire for reservations, but there has been a convention here, and our facilities are taxed to get all the delegates out again. All I have for you, Mrs. Munson, is an upper, and you're lucky to get that. Of course, over here a few days. . ." if you wait "No indeed!" replied Mary Ella with a toss of her head. "Not with all these children, and no guaranteed hotel reservation. I'll take my chances with the train conductor, and see if he can't work out something for me."

As it turned out, the conductor bravely encouraged the harried young mother as to the possibility of his being able to obtain at least a lower berth for her. He later reported: “I'm sorry, Mrs. 

Munson, I've tried all up and down the cars to get you a lower; but the men say they're worn out, and not one of them is willing to give up his berth."

[110] 

Mary Ella stood up, and from the heights of womanly dignity, replied: "Thank you, conductor; thank you very much. I'm as grateful to you as I would have been if you had secured me six lower berths." Then, letting her cool gaze travel up and down the length of the car, she continued in a voice calculated to reach all the occupants: "If I had taken stock of this car I should never have dreamed of asking you to strain the gallantry of these gentlemen." 

Immediately a Jewish boy jumped up. "Madam," he said, "I insist that you take my berth."

His offer was graciously accepted, and Mary Ella solicitously inquired if he had a long journey ahead of him. "Denison, Texas, is my destination; I'm visiting relatives there." "Denison is our home," she smiled; "and I know everyone. 

Who are your relatives?" "The Eppsteins," he replied. "They live across the street from me, and I count them dear friends."

The young man, completely charmed and filled with good will, even went so far as to suggest that he would gladly take one of the little girls in his upper berth, if that would help matters any. 

Mary Ella masked a mild case of shock, and refused with the assurance that he had already put them deeply in his debt. 

Ben had many business connections in New York City, and was always making new ones. At one time, somewhat later in his career, he thought seriously of buying a New York apartment house, for about $500,000. From Denison he used his New York business connections to get an on-the-ground opinion of the trade. 

It did not go through.

TH 

CHAPTER 10 Buying, Trading and Selling 

HE EXHAUSTED and roasted state that Ben complained of in the recently quoted letter to Mr. Douglas was due, no doubt principally, to his having just returned from the Panhandle, where he had been actively superintending the delivery of a final batch of cattle. This delivery was made in pursuance of a contract signed between W. B. Munson and W. N. Ewing of Kansas City, on December 31st of the preceding year, '84. The contract called for the cash down-payment on that date of $200,000, all cattle to be finally delivered by August 1st, '85. The cattle were sold at $18.50 a head, and were to be delivered in bunches of 3500; the horses and mules went for $50 a head, and the bulls at $60. The land involved in this sale was that known as the Palo Duro 

Canyon Ranch, lying mostly in Randall and Deaf Smith Counties, but which seemed also to include either leased or owned land known as the Dixon Creek Pasture, in Carson and Hutchinson Counties.

It was the impression of J. R. Handy, W.B.'s old-time friend and for many years his secretary, that the above lands came eventually into the possession of Col. Charles Goodnight. However this may be, the contract for the above sale of cattle and lands provides that Goodnight be one of the parties designated to see that the deal went through. If there was any hitch, an auction sale was to be held, and Ben was to have the privilege of buying it in through that method.

On this deal Ben took in a building in Kansas City, and when

[112] 

his agent in that place, S. M. Ford, informed him that Ewing and 

Coburn had collected all the back rents before turning over the building - evidently in violation of the sales contract - 

Ben was not surprised, and thought that such conduct of the two men was characteristic. He had already had trouble with them about the cattle count.

In spite of this, on the 16th of March, '86, in answer to a private inquiry, he paid a somewhat reluctant tribute to the business methods of Mr. Ewing: . . . while I do not wish to become a partisan in any matter arising between Mr. Ewing and the Cedar Valley Company, I have no objection to stating to you for your own private information the facts relative to the purchase of my ranch by Mr. Ewing. In answer to your first question, would say that Ewing used every means within his power to make a close trade with me for the ranch, and succeeded in getting me to sell at a much lower figure than I had ever offered the property before. I considered the market value of the cattle at the time I sold about $20.00 per head, while 

I sold at $18.50 per head. I was induced to cut the price by Mr. Ewing's threat to buy another property, and by my determination to go out of the business, even if I had to do so at a sacrifice. I considered at the time I sold that I was selling the property at about $40,000 less than the recognized market price of the property at the time.

While Ben had committed himself to the principal policy of buying, trading, and selling land on commission, he was not yet able to turn his back completely on the cattle business, although it was this point of difference that had caused the dissolution of his partnership with Gunter. J. T. Munson, on September 1st, '85, wrote to George Isaacs, Ben's old foreman:

My brother, W. B., writes and requests me to say to you that he may be asked to become General Manager of the Cedar Valley Land and Cattle Company, and in that event he will want you to run it for the Company, the same as you had done for him.

And Ben himself, a month later, wrote to his old partner that, while in New York, he had called on Mr. Franklyn of the Frank-

Buying, Trading and Selling 

[113] lyn Land and Cattle Company. Cattle were in his blood. The following year he wrote to J.T. that he thought, before long, there would be a splendid opportunity for people in his section of the country to pick up a great many cheap cattle; because cattle owned by non-citizens of the Indian Territory would soon be forced out of the Territory in great numbers. 

In 1885 Munson & Bro. formed a partnership with Ben's brother-in-law, Charlie Newton. The latter was to manage a ranch, which they purchased in Hunt and Rockwall Counties, Texas, consisting of approximately 7300 acres that they had stocked with cattle. Ben owned one-half interest, J. T. Munson one-fourth interest, and Charlie Newton one-fourth interest.

Their books show that they had extensive dealings with a Mr. E. 

Poe Harris of Savannah, Indian Territory. The partnership was dissolved in 1913 and proved a very profitable venture for all three men.

CHAPTER 11 Railroads Enter the Southwest 

JANUARY of '86, after not having had a satisfactory reply from 

I wrote the general manager of the company, Ben wrote direct to the president of the Santa Fe a follow-up letter.

I beg your pardon for troubling you with another letter. The people of Denison are very anxious to secure a R.R. up the Washita River Valley and, knowing that your Company have a charter to build such a road, we naturally look to your movements with much interest. We have formed a local company here and have procured a charter from Texas to build to the mouth of the Washita River, a distance of twelve miles from Denison, and would now like to get the right from your Company to build on your charter up the Washita, say 100 miles. We would undertake to build the road under such a contract as would give your Company the entire road at as cheap a price as you could otherwise build it, in the event that you should build to a connection with it at any time within two or three years. 

This would enable you to connect with the Texas system of R.R.'s much quicker when you do start south, than you could otherwise do, and could work no injury to your interests in any event. Will you kindly refer to my letter of October 28th, and advise me whether your Company will entertain any proposition from our people, or from the Denison & Washita 

Railway Co. 

He was excited over all this railroad talk, and lost no time in writing another letter, on the following day, to R. S. Stevens,

Attica, New York. The communication speaks for itself: 

As you are the oldest and best friend Denison ever had, it is natural to look to you in the times of emergency. As you are aware, there is now setting in a new impetus in R.R. building in the Southwest. The Indian 

Territory is looked upon as on the eve of being opened up. Two or three new lines of road will be pushed through it in the next few years. Denison

Railroads Enter the Southwest 

[115] wants these new enterprises either to start or terminate with her. Believing that two roads could be profitably built out of this city, one N.W. up the 

Washita Valley, and the other N.E. towards Ft. Smith, we have organized a Texas Company with charter for two short lines to strike the Territory N.E. and N.W., and now wish to secure charter rights from Congress to build through the Territory. If this could be done, and the roads pushed, say, fifty or a hundred miles in each direction, the large rival companies seeking to get a hold on the Indian Territory before it opens up, would doubtless bid up to get these roads. I write this letter to ask you if you will not take an interest in the enterprise with us. I think Messrs Scullin, Newman, and MaDill of St. Louis, will all take stock in it. The City of 

Denison would give liberally to it, and I would personally take $50,000 stock in it, if it can be put properly on its feet. We need your R.R. ability to direct and engineer the scheme to insure it's making big money. The 

Washita Valley is the very finest agricultural country in the southwest, and the road that first gets possession of it will have a good thing. The H. & 

T.C. Ry. Co., the A.T. & S.F. Co., and the Gould system would all want this road if built. What say you to the proposition? We must work quick to get a charter from Congress.

If you think favorably of the scheme, wire me, and I will meet you in 

Washington, D. C. at any date you may name.

There follow, one on the same date and one on the next day, the 21st of January, two letters of manifold importance; they are both addressed to Major L. L. Maughs, in Washington, D. C. 

Your several letters, up to the one dated the 16th, received. I have been waiting to see Colbert in regard to the bridge matter, but as he has not come in, I will name Frank Colbert, J. B. McDougall, Jno. R. Carr, Sam

Starr and Edward Perry as the parties applying for the bridge charter. 

You can style it the Red River Bridge Co. and, if Mr. Stevens will consent, add his name as one of the incorporators. It is not much matter who the incorporators are, as it will be assignable. Have the charter drawn so as to cover a right from Colbert's Ferry on Red River down the river for three miles, if you can give it that latitude. Let the charter be so drawn that the bridge can be made a combination wagon and R.R. bridge. You can consult

Stevens and get a model there to go by in preparing a bill. 

If it is at all possible to get a R.R. charter up Pauls Valley up east, to connect with the Frisco Road, press it. If we had the charter we could force the A.T. & S.F. here, as well as the Frisco and the G.C. & S.F. roads.

[116] 

over Throck- We have seen Harris and feel confident his racket is morton. I will try and go to St. Louis in a few days, and come on to 

Washington with Scullin. I wrote Stevens at Attica, N. Y. before receiving your letter, thinking he might be in Washington. We must get him interested in these matters some way. Would a memorial to Congress signed by a lot of the leading Chickasaws up Pauls Valley asking for the grant of the right-of-way to the Denison & Washita Valley be of any use in getting the bill through?

Don't fail to have the R.R. charter introduced at once, and keep it up with the Frisco bill, so that they will be afraid to fight it, for fear of killing their own bill. All well. 

Since writing you yesterday, Mr. Colbert has come in, and wishes that the Bridge Charter should be in his and J. T. Munson's name, as he wishes to hold a half interest in it. You will please have the charter drawn in accordance with his views. We want it so worded that either a straight wagon bridge, or a combination R.R. and wagon bridge, can be built under it. We may have to use it in crossing the river with our N.E. R.R., should we fail to get the railroad charter from Congress. 

I am a little afraid of Colbert, in the event we need the charter to cross the R.R. on, and I think it would be best to have McDougall's name retained. I think Colbert will oppose the matter through Harkness, unless he has a half interest in it. C. promises to allow the R.R. to use it if necessary. You can talk to Harkness and also to Stevens, and if there is any danger of making the charter defective by having it granted to a Chickasaw and citizen of U.S. jointly, don't make the change. The U.S. does not hold as valid any contract made with Indians, so they might hold that a charter granted to an illegal firm would be void. If you decide not to ask for the charter in Colbert's and J. T. Munson's name alone, tell Harkness that my instructions were to that effect, but that, after consulting lawyers, you concluded it not safe, as it would be necessary to raise money on the bridge, and any legal obstacle would defeat the development of the coal interests entirely. I will guarantee that Colbert shall own a one-half interest in the wagon bridge part of it, if he desires. If you think best, you can not receive this letter until after you have filed the bill, and then object to changing it on the grounds that it would be endangered thereby. 

These two letters are so characteristic that they are quoted in full. They show with what clearness of mind Ben had sized up

Railroads Enter the Southwest 

[117] the railroad situation developing in the Southwest; they show the bigness of his vision; and they indicate the care with which he tried to obviate all foreseeable difficulties. They also show a business drive, or commercial ruthlessness, which was indispensable to a man who was to compete with the large railroad systems in the general free-for-all.

The matter for the charter of the railroad to be built north from Denison to the Red River had not been settled by the following spring; for in March of '86 Ben writes to former Governor 

Throckmorton, expressing his anxiety for the fate of the bill which would have made the charter possible, and voicing his willingness to return to Washington if the Governor thinks he, Ben, could be of any help. Throckmorton was in Washington at this time, evidently considering the possibility of coming to Denison to make the latter place his home. 

On March 17th there is a letter to A. A. Robinson, General

Manager of the A.T. & S.F. 

Having heard that your Company has been negotiating with the Gulf, Colorado and Santa Fe of Texas, with a view to making that line a part of your system, I write to call your attention to the advantages your Company would gain by building your main line to our city. I hope you will excuse my presumption in writing you, and suggesting what I do, and I assure you that my object is simply to place before you such facts as may aid you in deciding to favor our city as the terminus of your main line. 

Not knowing what your object may be with reference to future extensions in the State, I herewith enclose you a map showing what connections could be made at Denison. I have supposed that your company are interested in the G.C. & S.F., and have designated a line connecting with that road at 

Ft. Worth, by way of Sherman. This line would run through as fine a country as there is in Texas, already thickly settled. It is a line that could be cheaply built, as it is over a smooth country. The cities of Denison and 

Sherman would give your company about $75,000 each, as a bonus to the road, and Ft. Worth would do the handsome thing to prevent your going to Dallas to connect with the G.C. & S.F. The line would be a better paying line, and could be cheaper built than by way of Gainsville.

[118] 

If your company desires to penetrate the great pine timber region of Texas, you could select no better route to the very heart of the timber than the one indicated by the red line on the map running S.E. from Denison. 

From Longview to Sabine Pass you would run through heavy pine and cypress timber all the way. This timber would furnish an immense business for your long lines of road through the prairies of Kansas and Indian Territory. You could connect with the H. & T.C., the Frisco, and the entire R.R. system of Texas at Denison. Our people are anxiously awaiting your engineers, and will do everything in their power to induce your company to come to this point.

P.S. I am familiar with the prices of pine timbered lands in the section through which the proposed line runs, and could secure for your road, or its friends, 100,000 or 200,000 acres of pine timber along the proposed line at very low prices, say $2.00 to $4.00 per acre. Of course this would have to be done before agitating the building of the road. If I can serve you or your company in any way, by purchase of lands, right-of-way, depot grounds or otherwise, let me know. I have large experience in handling real estate in Texas, and understand the necessity of keeping matters quiet. 

It is interesting to see that, even at this early date of almost sixty years ago, Dallas and Fort Worth were already rivals. Then, too, Ben must have felt that the temper of the people of Sherman had changed on the subject of railroads, since he committed them to a very handsome bonus.

The bridge over the Red River, referred to recently in several letters, was finally to be known, naturally, as the Red River Bridge. The franchise was granted originally to B. F. Colbert, and the Chickasaw Indians figured in this grant. The rights of the company extended six miles up and down the river. Ben formed a company, capitalized at $60,000, and personally took one third of the stock. They had bought Colbert's franchise, and the bridge was built during 1890-91. This particular bridge was washed out in 1908, and the rebuilding did not start until 1913. The bridge was completed two years later.*

Ben had relinquished all his interests in the Red River Bridge Co. years before the "Free Bridge Fight," and had no part in this political squabble.

Railroads Enter the Southwest 

[119] The railroad company whose organization Ben had been agitating, the purpose of which was to build a line north to the river, and then up the Washita Valley, was formally organized on January 7th, 1886; and the charter - which had given so much anxiety - was finally secured by specific act of Congress, July 1st, the same year. This charter granted the company permission to build its line into the Indian Territory. The company was known as the 

Denison & Washita Valley Railway Co. 

up In March of this same year, '86, Ben writes to Mr. Scullin to know if the latter has been able to learn anything of the intentions of the A.T. & S.F. He resignedly supposes in this letter that there have been negotiations between this company and the G.C. & S.F., and somewhat mournfully fears that the road will go to Fort 

Worth through Gainsville, and not through Denison, a fear which was to be realized. He is not discouraged in his other enterprises by his inability to convince the management of the Santa Fe of the advantages that would accrue to the road if they would but build their line through his home city of Denison. Two days after the letter to Mr. Scullin, he wrote to Charles Dillingham, in 

Houston, the receiver of the Houston & Texas Central Ry.:

The Denison and Washita Valley Ry Company are preparing to build a line of road from this city north-east to the coal fields in the Indian Territory, with a view to connect with the Frisco road; and the company desires to secure from the H. & T.C. R.R. Co. the right-of-way and old grade abandoned by them from here north for twenty miles; also an interest in your depot grounds in the city, if such arrangement could be made as would be satisfactory to your company. As the line proposed to be built by our company would furnish your road a very desirable connection, we believe it will be to your interest to trade with us on reasonable terms. 

On March 23rd, '86, Ben writes to William Walton of Butler,

Missouri, offering to sell him 200,000 acres of land, in alternate sections, situated in Randall, Deaf Smith, Castro, and Swisher Counties. He wants net to $1.10 per acre for this property. Early

[120] 

the next month he wrote to his old associate, John Corwith of Illinois, that he was getting up another land scheme. What unflagging activity of the mind! What restlessness of the commercial spirit, to be always embarking on a new scheme. But he has not given up all hope about the Santa Fe. He wrote again to Robinson, the General Manager, on April 9th.

We learn from outside sources [a not-too-gentle dig at the gentleman for not having had the courtesy to inform him of the plans that the road had] that your company, or the G.C. & S.F., have decided to build the line by way of Gainsville. If this is true, does it destroy our hopes for your road here? If your company do not desire to build the line here on your charter, would you not allow the Denison & Washita Valley road to build from here on your charter to a connection with you on or near the Washita River. 

We are most anxious for a connection with your road, and will do anything in our power to get it.

If you desire to build a line here our company would like to join you in the bridge across the river, and the road from Denison across the river on our way to a connection with the Frisco road. We wish to commence work as early as possible, so as to have the summer season to do the work in. 

If you think there is any chance for us to trade with your company in any way, one of us will come up to see you.

Ben signed this letter with his official title as vice president of his new railroad company. This company he was destined to put through, and to have its road in operation before long.

CHAPTER 12 The Railroads' Growing Pains 

LTHOUGH BEN was never interested in politics for himself — he never proposed his candidacy for any public office- he nevertheless had a keen concern for politics in the broader sense. Local politics did not worry him, but he felt it his duty to take a hand in Congressional, State, and even National elections. In June of '92 he accompanied the local politicians to the Chicago convention of the Democratic party, and there acted as alternate and proxy voter for his Denison friend, Dr. J. C. Field.

It was, perhaps, his continuing admiration and friendship for 

Joseph Weldon Bailey that kept activating his interest in the broader political field. This friendship dated almost from the very beginning of Bailey's appearance on the horizon, and it was his invariable habit, whenever Bailey made a public address in 

North Texas, to take Mary Ella with him and attend the "speaking." Senator Bailey rewarded this devotion with the gift of his confidence; and, later, when the Senator became involved in his celebrated controversy, he had no more faithful and active partisan than Ben Munson.

Back in 1880 there is every reason to believe that Ben joined with his brother, J.T., and a few other Denison men, in raising funds for the Democratic National Committee; in fact $130 was forwarded to the treasurer of the National Committee. 

To the letter that enclosed this draft there was appended a postscript signed by J.T. He wanted to know if Mr. Canda, the Democratic chairman, thought it would be safe for these Texas Demo-

[122] 

crats to "risk anything" on the general results, or on the voting in New York and Indiana. We do not have Mr. Canda's reply to this cautious query, but Garfield, the Republican, of course beat out the Democratic candidate, Hancock, in the total number of electoral votes; although he failed to secure a majority in either of the states mentioned because the Greenback candidate, Weaver, muddied the waters. The vote was very close in both Indiana and New 

York, and it is possible that, if they placed their money right, our 

North Texas Democratic stalwarts may have won something. At any event, the following letter written by Ben in the early summer of '86, to Throckmorton in Washington, is interesting:

I reached home yesterday evening and find our people very much discouraged and nervous. I have cheered them up with the assurance that the railroad bill at least is safe and will pass. I stopped in St. Louis and found everything there in shape to take hold of the enterprise immediately on the passage of the bill. It is the desire of all our people that you should become interested in the enterprise, as we will need your legal assistance and the benefit of your experience. I have matters arranged so that some money can be made out of it, and desire that you shall share in the profits of the enterprise. As soon as the bill passes, and we get fully organized, I will give you a full outline of the plan and hope you will find it to your interest to go into the enterprise with us. If you can use the influence of Mr. McMillin of Tennessee in any way connected with this bill, call on him and ask him as a favor to me to assist you. I have written him by this mail to help you if necessary in getting a night session for such bills as have passed the Senate and been reported favorably by the House, and of a character not to call out discussion, etc. He is an old schoolmate of mine, and I know will take pleasure in doing me a kindness. I find our people red hot against all of Gainsville, and it will be very hard to hold them from stampeding to Hare. Our people must insist on your staying one more term in Congress and we hope that, as a final measure, you will not refuse us. Should you succeed in getting our R.R. through, our people will feel better and more independent and, if nothing else will please you, may be controlled for Davis. I am working with them and hope to keep them from being committed to Hare, and finally bring them around to support Davis, if it has to be done, but it is like feeding a child on castor oil. It will take much coaxing.

The Railroads' Growing Pains 

[123] Although this campaign is pretty far back, and the personalities and merits of the men involved are not particularly vital for us, it is fairly obvious, from this letter, that Ben was a "hosstrader." He seems to suggest, clearly enough, that he and associates will scratch the Throckmorton back with support for him or his personally chosen candidate, if the former Governor will scratch the backs of the Denison people with the very smooth paper on which a railroad charter would be written. This was only fair, and recognized as such; and it is refreshing to see that the country was going to hell in those days with the same rapid insouciance that is characteristic of present-day political deals. 

On June 9th, '86, Ben writes to his old friend, Scullin, of St.

Louis, a letter that is important and interesting for us, because it is the first appearance in his correspondence of a scheme that he had evidently long had in mind, and which he evidently had already discussed with the St. Louis man. 

The more I think of the road Southeast from here, the better I think of it as a money-making enterprise. The line from here to Ladonia, by way of 

Bonham, would be through the finest farming country in Texas all the way. 

The town of Denison will give $50,000 bonus, and Bonham $25,000, and right-of-way and depot grounds; and Ladonia $5,000 to $10,000, making a bonus of about $80,000 on forty miles of road. It is an easy line to build. 

It will connect Denison with the G.C. & S.F. at Ladonia, and effectually head off Sherman's scheme to build to Mt. Pleasant. If we find it profitable, we can extend the road on to Mt. Pleasant, and then either sell out to the 

Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe, or Texas and St. Louis.

The reader will be glad to know, at this point, that the road* was chartered on the 27th of January in 1887. Twenty-four miles of trackage were laid from Denison to Bonham. 

On the same day that Ben wrote to Mr. Scullin, sketching in a plan for one new railroad, he wrote to Charles B. Mitchell, of 

Bridgeport, Connecticut: * Denison, Bonham & New Orleans R.R.

[124] 

Your name has been given me by A. S. Dickinson of New York as a party who would probably take an interest in a timber or lumber enterprise. 

We have, in S.E. Texas, some very heavy pineries from which the great quantities of lumber that will be required in developing the immense prairie region of the S.W. must be mainly drawn. These lands, while not near present railroad lines, can be bought at very low prices, say about $2.00 per acre for timber which will yield an average of 10,000 feet of fine lumber to the acre.

The lands are fair agricultural lands after the timber is removed, and would bring a profit in a few years independent of the timber. My scheme is to form a syndicate to buy 200,000 to 500,000 acres of this timber, located with a view to constructing a railway line through it, and then putting it into saleable shape. The history of timely investments in pineries elsewhere has been very flattering, as immense fortunes have been made wherever judgment and proper business methods have obtained. The great pineries elsewhere in the U.S. are being rapidly exhausted, and there can not be a question but that this timber will be very soon demanded and must be used, as it is the nearest and almost the only source of supply for the vast prairie counties lying to the N.W. of it, which are now settling with great rapidity. I know of no enterprise that promises more certain returns or greater profits, and there is not an element of risk in it, for the lands are bound to increase rapidly in value. If you think favorably of the enterprise 

I would be glad to have you visit Texas and look over the country and, if pleased with the prospects, I will join you in the enterprise. If you desire to investigate my standing, I would refer you to the National Bank of Commerce, of New York, the Laclede Bank of St. Louis, or the Armour 

Bros. Banking Company of Kansas City, for information. I have been operating in Texas lands quite extensively for the last fourteen years, and am familiar with the land laws, system of titles, and can see that the titles and character of the lands are desirable and good in all cases before money is paid. The country is comparatively level, and railroads can be built very cheaply through it; so that money should be made out of a railroad enterprise, as well as timber. The project referred to in the above letter in such glowing and true-to-life colors was destined to be realized some years later, but in a form different from that proposed here, and Ben had no part in the actual construction of the railroad. This road, originally the East Texas & Red River, was bought by the M.K. & T., and

The Railroads' Growing Pains 

[125] Ben was made president of the line as a testimonial to the friendship and admiration in which he was always held by the officers of the "Katy." At the time of Ben's election to the presidency, the road was known as the Sherman, Shreveport, & Southern.

The close relationship between Munson and the big men in the "Katy" dated, of course, from the very earliest days of the road's entrance into Texas, just north of Denison. When Ben was ill in the spring of '98, Mr. Henry Rouse, the president of the M.K. & T., wrote a solicitous letter to Mrs. Munson, hoping for a quick restoration of her husband's health. He continues: I entertain a sincere regard for your husband, who has been one of my staunchest friends in Texas, and one who has always been more than ready to assist me in any work that I had in hand; and no man's allegiance has been more faithful to me than his has been. 

Last week I perfected my arrangements for my annual meetings, which take place next week in Texas, and I have arranged to cause Mr. Munson to be re-elected Director and President both of the Sherman, Shreveport, and Southern Railway Co, and the Denison and Washita Valley Railway Co. as heretofore, and I hope that these associations will be maintained for many years to come.

Whenever Mr. Munson shall inquire about me, now or hereafter, I hope you will give him every assurance of my continued regard and my best wishes for his speedy recovery to health. ...

This is not only a sincere personal tribute to a friendship, but also an open recognition of Mr. Rouse's confidence in Ben's business ability as a railroad man.

CHAPTER 13  

'N A LETTER dated July 7th, '86, to the Honorable Richard Coke of Washington, D. C., Ben shows his intimate knowledge of conditions in the "Territory," and along the border. It is a forceful and clearheaded communication. 

I see from the Record that our court bill has been reported without recommendation. I hope you and General Maxey will be able to get it through. It would be a great injustice to the people of the Territory, as well as to those along the border, to place the only court having criminal jurisdiction at a point where the outlaws that infest that country could terrorize the witnesses and juries, and virtually prevent the execution of the laws. 

To make this court effective in holding the lawless element of that country in check it must be supported by a well organized community of intelligent people, from whom proper juries can be selected, and who would not be intimidated by the fear of violence, which would certainly be the case in the Territory. If Vest's bill should pass, could it not be so amended as not to conflict with the criminal jurisdiction provided for in our bill? 

During the past year, and in fact every year, many lives of good citizens are sacrificed on account of the inefficiency of the execution of the criminal laws in the Chickasaw Nation. The removal of this jurisdiction from Ft. 

Smith to Muskogee would make the matter even worse than it is now, as it would place the juries and witnesses under the control of the bands of desperadoes that infest that country,. . . The blood of many murdered citizens rests upon the hands of those who defeated this bill in the last Congress. Your friends will highly appreciate any efforts you may make to relieve us in this matter. 

This letter is of interest not only for the reasons already pointed out above, but also because it shows Ben's continued concern with the law as law; there is the further flavor of the West and the fron-

[127] tier in this letter, a recklessness seasoning the social setup that is apt to be forgotten or lost sight of. The country north of the river was wild and woolly, and this must be kept in mind even as we read of this man's manifold business efforts in a relatively civilized community.

The remainder of the year finds Ben as busy as ever. He makes an interesting report on the Corwith Pool, to the effect that not only has all the invested money been returned, but there is, in addition to a money surplus, something more than 240,000 acres left on hand, which he was offering to sell at $1.25 per acre. He is on the lookout for steel rails for the new road up into the Territory, and suggests that the road's bonds be accepted for the rails. 

He thinks, at this time, that he will eventually dispose of his road to the Frisco people (but it was the Katy men who finally secured it). He is still boosting Denison as a potential rail center to the 

Santa Fe people, this time in an effort to induce them to build the Bonham road; or, if they would prefer, he will build it for them on contract, and turn it over to them. 

He has heard, in February of '87, that Mr. C. W. Reynolds, of

Chillicothe, Ohio, might like to buy a good newspaper. He tells 

Reynolds that the controlling stock in the Denison paper can be bought at much less than the plant is worth; that the setup is all there, waiting for a competent editor; that he expects the population of Denison to double in two years from the 15,000 they then have. In March he wires Scullin to price a one-third interest in the gas works, and to send a man down at once. "The thing has gone off prematurely, but there is big money to be made here if immediate and proper action is taken." In May he has heard that the Abilene people are anxious to secure a railroad from the northeast, and he writes to a Dr. Tollar (sic) of that place, that the Denison Southwestern R.R. Co. is now being organized to build from Denison to the doctor's city; he speaks of the excellent northeast connec-

[128] 

tions, and thinks the company will soon be ready to solicit subscriptions along the proposed line.

As though he did not already have enough irons in the fire, and in enough different sections, he became interested in Duluth, Minnesota, and wrote to a number of people in that city, making the inquiries about the town that would supply the kind of information that a solid businessman would want to have before investing in property. The eventual contact in Duluth was made through a former schoolmate; this man was in the real estate business and acted as Ben's agent in the investment that the latter made in that city. In November of '86 he was anxious to secure a northeast connection for the road he expected to build up into the Territory, and wrote to the president of the Little Rock & Ft. Smith in an effort to get this gentleman interested in building an extension of that road to the southwest. He pointed out that such a building program would, through the Memphis connections, give the Louisville & Nashville a through line to the developing coal and lumber regions of the Territory. Early in the following year he admitted to one of his rare periods of reasonable self-satisfaction. He reported that he had sold his ranch for something more than $700,000; that it was a cash transaction; that he was in comfortable circumstances, and felt that he could afford to "take a little rest."

He said that he had worked hard since coming to Texas (this in a letter to an old friend in Illinois), that he had suffered many hardships, but was "satisfied with the result."

In January of this year, '87, there are two amusing letters to 

Mr. X of St. Louis. The first one is as follows: 

I see from quotations of mining stocks published by Donaldson & Co., that M. Granite is quoted .50 bid and .60 asked per share of $10.00. You charged me $1.60. Has the stock declined this rapidly, or did you make a mistake of $1.00 per share in my case? I know that you would not have

[129] intentionally charged me more than the market price, and as you stated that the stock was pooled, and would be held steady, I am satisfied that there is a mistake somewhere. Please let me hear from you in regard to this matter. What are the prospects of this property now?

There doubtless was already born in Ben's mind some little hint of suspicion. His expressed belief in the integrity of Mr. X was merely a thoroughly commendable effort to arouse that gentleman's better nature. There is bitterness and sarcasm in the second letter, written four days later, but not what could really be called surprise: 

Your kind proposition of the 13th received. Since you admit that you charged me three times what the property was worth by reason of a little pool that was arranged for the benefit of your friends, I conclude that a good way to make money will be to let your soft snaps alone. I console myself by the reflection that I am probably as well off as though I had bought 10,000 shares for the money that I paid you. 

He was too careful in his investments to be stung often, but such deceptions have to come to even the most cautious, and, since the investment was not large, his philosophical resignation to the loss is complete.

This same month he wrote to the president of the St. Louis & 

Texas R.R. Co., inquiring about the prospects of that road's building down toward Denison. Pending the securing of the rightof-way through Sherman, he was willing and anxious to organize a company to build north to a connection; or was willing to handle the matter in any sound way that Mr. Fordyce would suggest. 

He was also anxious to know the truth about the rumor that Gould, or the Missouri Pacific, had gotten control of Mr. Fordyce's road, if such information was not inconsistent with the latter's best interests. Later in this same month of January he was in correspondence with Colis P. Huntington. He had heard that 

Huntington had secured control of the H. & T.C., and thought that his own recently chartered D. & W.V. would make a valuable

[130] 

extension northeast for the H. & T.C. The road, he wrote, could be built for $12,000 a mile, including the cost of the bridge over the Red River; and the coal business alone originating in the 

Territory would, he felt, pay the interest on the money required to build the whole connection. 

The firm promise of a deal for some other company to take over the new road northeast was not forthcoming, however, and he proceeded under his own power with the project. On January 5th, '88, he wrote George A. Boynton in New York:

I was very much annoyed yesterday when your telegram announcing the protest of the paper given for the rails and spikes came. I had arranged for the funds in due time, and instructed the bank to be certain to protect the paper, but by some blunder or oversight Mr. Perry got it into his head that the paper was to be forwarded here for collection, and while I supposed everything was fully understood, the matter went by default. 

I regret this very much, as it will have a very bad effect as it will look like bungling business at best, and cannot be explained away. You remember I was very particular to send exact copies of the paper to Mr. Perry at the time of its execution, so as to guard against possible error. It will doubtless make the Huntington and A.T. & S.F. crowd think they have an easy victim to deal with; but I will show them before I am through that I have backbone enough to stand a half dozen such blunders, and then carry the enterprise through on my own resources, without asking help from any railroad organization. The track will be completed to the river today.. 

A few days after this, Ben sent his application for a charter for the Denison Coal and Mining Co. to the Secretary of State in

Austin. Early in February of the same year he was beginning to be in need of some ready cash that he could put his hands on, and so he wrote his agent in Kansas City that he would pay him a five per cent commission on the sale of his Kansas City building. 

On February 14th he wrote a characteristic letter to Jot Gunter in Sherman:

In looking over the Gunter and Munson books I see that Summerfield is still charging $50.00 per month for keeping books, while the entries do

[131] not amount to more than a dozen a month. He has charged $250.00 for such service while in Europe, while all the entries made for the five months filled little more than one page of the journal, and were made in probably fifteen minutes after his return.

As far as I am concerned I don't want his services any longer, and I don't propose to allow $50.00 per month for work that was not done when he was in Europe. It is like his charge for the trip to Kansas City for Corwith, when he was getting a commission for making the sale that should have covered all such expense. If you will send me the notes still belonging to the firm 

I will have what little book-keeping is required done here. 

During that same month he was worried about the fate of the compromise Court Bill which had been introduced by Culberson, and wrote to both Culberson and Throckmorton about the 

To Dr. J. M. Ford, of Kansas City, he wrote on May 1st: 

Your dispatch just received. I hope there will be no great delay in your coming down, as it is important to act promptly in the matter. The representative of the Choctaw Coal and R.R. Co. is here, and is figuring to get control of the Bonham road; and the people here, being anxious to have the matter pushed along, will insist on something's being done at once. 

They can make $5,000 to $8,000 per mile on this road. 

He was pushing the organization and building of two railroads, the organization of a coal company to operate in the Territory, and pressing land sales all at the same time. He offered 135,000 acres, in alternate sections, in Crockett County, to Beals of Kansas City; and then went out for a much-needed summer rest and vacation to Wagon Wheel Gap in Colorado. When he returned, the second week in September, he wrote to Gunter that he was “hard up," and that, if there was "anything of consequence" in the stillactive Gunter and Munson joint account, he would like to have a check. During the first week of the following month, October, he wrote to Walter Gresham of Galveston in an effort to interest the latter in the Denison, Bonham & New Orleans Railroad. "There is twenty-eight miles of this road graded and bridged,

[132] 

with some of the ties already on the ground. 

But a few days later he was off for New York, and for Boston, where he was to stay at the Quincy House. When he returned, about the first of 

November, he wrote to Scullin in St. Louis:

Enclosed I hand you a prospectus of the Denison and Washita Valley Ry Company, which you can forward to anyone desiring a safe and profitable investment. I think it would be well to speak to your St. Louis friends about the construction company. Should a sale of the bonds be made in 

Europe, it would not require over $200,000 to handle the construction company.

At this time, and for a number of years afterwards, money was very cheap in Europe, particularly in England and Scotland, and this idle capital was constantly on the lookout for investments in America. Many firms and individuals of known standing borrowed money in England and Scotland for as low as two per cent, and reloaned it in the Southwest for rates that were as high as the traffic would bear, often ten per cent. A number of solid fortunes were made in the Southwest through this method. 

The Bonham road was still a painful puzzler. On October 10th, '88, he wrote to A. J. Tullock, of Leavenworth, Kansas:

Your letter of the 4th of September has just reached me, having come in my absence, and been forwarded to St. Louis, where it was missed, and just now returned. I hardly know what to say to you concerning the bridges. 

I have tried every expedient to get the enterprises pushed ahead, but so far nothing has proved successful. How would it suit you to take stock in a construction company, organized to complete this line to Bonham and Ladonia? The road can be completed at a cost of about $6500 per mile, and the company are willing to turn the road over to the company that will complete it. I think if the road was completed even to Bonham it could be leased to the M.K. & T. receivers, and as soon as it was completed to 

Ladonia, I think the A.T. & S.F. would lease it. If you can raise one half the capital I can raise the balance to complete to Bonham, and then we certainly could use our bonds to finish it to Ladonia. 

That same month, October, he was, of course, not unmindful

[133] that something good might come of playing the Missouri Pacific against the Katy. To the vice president of the former railroad he wrote:

The M.K. & T., now having passed into the hands of a receiver, and the 

Missouri Pacific having gotten control of the St. Louis, Arkansas & Texas road, I write again to call your attention to the Denison & Washita Ry enterprise as a means of connecting the Missouri Pacific with the coal mines of the Indian Territory. The D. & W.V. charter is such that the road can be built from Denison to Ft. Smith, via the coal mines at Lehigh, Savannah and McAlester, and pass through the center of the coal field lengthwise. 

The coal traffic alone would make this line pay well, and it would be one of the best means of making the M.K. & T. appreciate the strength of the 

Mo. Pac. situation. I will come up and confer with you. On the 11th of October the Bonham road is still on his mind. 

He writes to Mr. S. B. Allen of that city: 

The D.B. & N.O. Ry Co. owe the First National Bank of Denison* $9000 which the bank desires to be put in some shape that will make it secure. It is suggested that if the company will allow a friendly suit, and confess judg ment, that expense could be saved and the bank secured by a judgment lien, and would then wait for developments that would enable the company to take care of the debt. There are certain reasons which I will explain fully to you when you come over why I think it best for the company to have a directors meeting and authorize the acceptance of service and confession of the judgment. . . There are some other matters to consider, and we may hit on some scheme to get the road completed. ... ... 

On November 16th, '88, there is a character-revealing letter to Mr. Boynton, of 47 Broadway, New York:

Your favor of the 13th, as well as several former letters, received. I am much obliged for the information contained in them. In regard to telegraphing an extension of time, I will simply say I will wait until I see Mr. 

Enos and Colby. I will extend such time as I think will be necessary, provided I see no indications of a disposition to dally with the enterprise and kill it by consuming time necessary to pull it through. It does not occur * Ben was a director of the First National Bank at this time, having been on the board of directors since the bank was organized. He was elected president of this organization in March, 1893.

[134] 

to me that thirty days more will be needed, as all examinations can be completed within the next ten days. If the parties are acting in good faith, they need not fear for want of time; but they must not expect me to allow them to dally with this enterprise at this stage of the affair. Other parties are ready to go ahead, and Mr. Bond should not expect me to jeopardize the enterprise by waiting on uncertainties. 

A few days later a matter was called to Ben's attention, and the way he handled it was one of exemplary fairness. To A. E. Reynolds, of Denver, Colorado, he wrote: 

Yours of the 19th inst. to hand.

In reply would say that our surveys were made by Mr. John Summerfield, while he was a deputy surveyor of that land district, and were always supposed by us to be correct, and that they covered the land as platted and sold to you. ... We do not propose to shrink from any legal responsibility arising out of this transaction, but would suggest that it is not necessary that you be insulting in order to get what assistance it may be in our power to give you.
