In spite of the "insulting" tone that Mr. Reynolds had assumed in his letter of November 19th, Ben does not allow his natural anger at such an assumption to blind him to the issue of right and justice. On the 11th of December he wrote to Gunter and 

Summerfield: 

I think we should do all we can to assist Mr. Reynolds to hold the land as it was platted and sold to him. 

Ben had been holding two notes signed by Mr. Gunter for a total of something more than $9000 and, since he "will probably need this money," sent Mr. Gunter two new notes to sign, if the latter found it inconvenient to take them up, and requested, since the notes would have to be discounted, that "Summerfield or someone else" sign them, as "two names make better looking bank paper."

The same scrupulousness that Munson had evidenced in his attitude toward Mr. Reynolds' "insulting" outcries — which prob- 

---

[135] ably concerned a matter of thousands of dollars - is manifest in his handling of a trifling affair. To Mr. William Battle, of Cleveland, Ohio:

Owing to your hasty retreat today, I forgot to refund you the money paid out for team hire and hotel bill. Enclosed I hand you draft on New York for $12.00, which is the amount as near as I remember.

CHAPTER 14 Railroads and the Indian Territory THE 

THE FATE of the Denison, Bonham & New Orleans was still in doubt, but on January 12th, '89, Ben was ready to go ahead with the actual construction of the Denison & Washita Valley, and wrote to O. T. Lyons, of Sherman: 

If you can recommend to me two or three contractors who are honest and capable, and have outfits of their own sufficient to do from three to five miles at a time, I would be glad to have their addresses. I wish to steer clear of scoundrels. . . I am now ready to let contracts for ten miles of the road referred to in the Indian Territory. . . . 

Also in January of '89 he wrote to Mr. Grigsby of Jefferson, Texas: 

I write to inquire what the people of Jefferson would do in the way of donations to secure the building of the Denison, Bonham & New Orleans Railway to your city. We have secured an extension of time from the legislature, and I now wish to put the enterprise in shape to present to New York parties who are building the Denison and Washita Valley Railway. 

I should like a statement signed by some of your leading citizens stating what amount of money the company could depend upon raising in your city; and also statements setting forth the importance of your iron interests, and probable consumption of coal and coke in your vicinity. 

I send you in separate wrapper a map showing proposed location of road, provided the towns on the line give proper encouragement to the enterprise. Can you advise me who the best parties are to address in the small towns this side of you? Times are so blue now that it will require strong inducements to get capitalists to take hold of any R.R. enterprise. What

Railroads and the Indian Territory 

[137] amount of fine timber would there be accessible to the line as indicated? 

Jefferson was, at this time, one of the largest and most thriving towns in East Texas. This will account for Ben's phrase: "the small towns this side of you." The response of Mr. Grigsby must not have been too discouraging for, on February 2nd, he explains the whole situation fully in a letter to Mr. Enos of New York. 

Your telegram regarding charter to build to the Oklahoma country received. I control the charter of the Denison, Bonham & New Orleans 

Railway Co., which has 28 miles of road graded and bridged between here and Bonham, with about 15,000 ties delivered on the line. This company owns the right-of-way on the 28 miles, and depot grounds at Bonham, and right-of-way to connect with the D. & W.V. at Denison. I can deliver this entire property for $25,000 clear of all debt and encumbrances to anybody who will agree to complete the line. The line can be completed to Bonham for about $6000 per mile. I send you a map by this mail that shows the proposed extension of this line to Jefferson, Texas, and Shreveport, Louisiana. It runs through the iron fields near Jefferson, and reaches the pine timber country of East Texas and Louisiana. It would be a valuable adjunct to our coal enterprises, as well as a feeder for the M.K. & T. We could haul timber to Oklahoma direct from the pineries if we built this line. If this doesn't suit, I control the charter of the Denison and Sherman

Ry. Co., properly incorporated under the laws of Texas, which I can turn over to our syndicate at cost, say $500. We could ask for the right-of-way on either of these companies. As for the terms of our mutual interest in the enterprise if undertaken there will be no trouble. I would expect only a fair share in it, such as I am satisfied you would think reasonable. We might introduce a bill organizing a new company under national authority, if that course was thought best. I have all contracts let for the ten miles and could leave the engineer in charge of the work, so if you think best I can come to New York, and on to Washington to push whatever bill is decided on. Wire your wishes on receipt of this. 

During the last of March, '89, he wrote again to Mr. Enos:

The President's message opening Oklahoma for settlement is out, and the necessity of immediate action in the matter of R.R. operations there is apparent. I think we should be prepared to commence construction on the 

22nd of April. I can have a preliminary line run for about 25 miles west

[138] 

from the town of Oklahoma in the meantime, so that we can act so quickly that others contemplating a similar thing will be headed off. Should we not be ready to act, we will in all probability make a similar mistake to what we did in not applying for the right-of-way at the proper time. The West is a rapid country, and those who hesitate too long are usually left. I don't think there is a doubt of there being big money in this scheme if pressed. 

There will be ten thousand people in the town of Oklahoma within 60 days of the opening of the Territory. The proposed line will pay from the start, independent of our coal trade, and will be a big thing when connected with our present line. If you people in New York will work the money arrangements, I will shove this end of the line, and I am certain we can make big money. I will be ready to report approximately 50 miles of the line by the time the proclamation takes effect. This we can build so as to connect the two land offices. Please confer with Mr. Hoyt and other interested parties, and advise me what you think of immediate construction.

Two days later he himself wrote to Mr. Hoyt, a letter as full of suppressed excitement as the preceding:

I am starting the Oklahoma survey, and will press it with all possible speed. I wish Mr. Wetmore to investigate the legal status of townsites, as to whether we could have subsidies in land or money voted us by new towns organized, and also whether under the general laws authorizing the construction of railroads we could secure right-of-way in the event settlers get on our line in advance of our occupancy. My idea is that we should build 

40 miles of this line immediately, beginning at Oklahoma town, running thence north-west to the western one of the two land offices established in the Territory, where we will connect with the Rock Island road. This latter point is, I think, likely to be the capital of the Indian Territory, and a large place. The funds necessary for this enterprise should be available immediately upon the completion of the survey, as the big towns will be on the first line built east and west. We will have our road completed from Lehigh to the north end within about twenty days, if nothing prevents. I do hope 

Mr. Cameron will stay with us. 

The middle of the following month, April, '89, he wrote an eminently sensible letter to Hon. W. H. Noble, Department of the Interior, Washington, D. C.: 

In view of the complicated condition of affairs likely to arise in the Territory known as Oklahoma, on and after the 22nd inst, I beg permission

Railroads and the Indian Territory 

[139] to make a suggestion. As the law now stands, the difficulties surrounding the laying out and building of towns in the country will be very great, and will no doubt lead to bloodshed and much litigation. Everyone conversant with the situation knows that at least three large towns will be built in that country very soon after opening. I would name Oklahoma, Guthrie, and 

King Fisher as points that will require, and should have, at least 1780 acres of land each reserved, and sold for townsites. Could not such reservation and sale be made under sections 2380 & 2381 of the Revised Statutes of the 

United States? If so, I hope that you will call the attention of the President to the necessity of immediate action in this matter, and thereby avoid the general scramble that must take place over these important townsites, and subserve the best interests of the people who are about to settle in that country? Could you see the thousands of desperate and determined men who are standing waiting to engage in a personal scramble and conflict for the profits involved in the possession of these leading townsites, you could more fully appreciate the motives that prompt me to intrude my opinions upon you. Hoping that I may not be considered impertinent for making these suggestions, and that the law will permit some relief for the coming trouble. . . . 

In June another letter to Mr. Enos: 

Your favor of the 24th inst. at hand. I will leave for the North and East in a day or two, but will not reach New York until about the 15th of August, as I will stop a few days at some cool place to rest. Will see Mr. Wakefield on my way, and get the Llano iron property in shape to handle. The Denison land scheme is now all in shape to take hold of, so you can have your 

Eastern friends ready to take the matter up when I reach your city. I send you a small sample of coal and iron ore from a point in Missouri that can easily be reached from the M.K. & T., which I think there is money in. 

Please have these specimens examined by an expert, and I will explain the situation when I see you. Our coal company is progressing rapidly, and we will be prepared to furnish the M.K. & T. 500 tons per day by August 

1st, or sooner. I will stop at Sedalia on my way North, and close contract with the Receivers for their coal. Messrs Ford and Rice will be in New 

York at the same time that I am there, so we can get the Denison iron scheme formulated and ready for business. I received your paper about 

Birmingham, and think we have an even better field of operations here than they had. There will be an immense rush to Denison as soon as it is known that such a combination as we propose has actually taken hold of the situation. .

[140] 

On the same day, June 28th, 1889, we find this letter, written to Jot Gunter in Dallas: 

Your favor. . . enclosing Judge Robertson's letter to the News received.

From the standpoint of a R.R. president, the doctrines set forth in the letter are viciously unsound, but viewed from the standpoint of unbiased statesmanship I regard the letter as able, and correct in its positions. 

I think, however, that the enunciation and adoption of this doctrine as the settled policy of the State would effectually put a stop to all railroad construction in the State. To say to capitalists desiring to invest, that their money was to be turned over unconditionally to the control of the sovereign voter, without any restrictions or contract for remuneration, would not be an inviting prospectus to present to an average money-lender. If the State is the real owner of the railroad, why should she not issue her own bonds for the actual cost, and do the hauling for its average cost, and secure to the people in rate of interest and expense of operation that such a course would ensure?

It looks to me like there is no stopping place short of Governmental ownership of all the avenues of commerce. What are the undeveloped portions of the country to do for conveniences of this kind, if the State will neither build the roads, nor allow those who would build them to have anything to say about the rates of payment for the business to be done? 

It is too big a question for me to decide, so I shall leave it for the dear people to settle.

The contents of this letter will come as considerable of a shock to those of us - and that will include any but close historians who had not imagined that such questions as Government ownership of the "avenues of commerce" had made their appearance, and seriously, in the press of this section of the country as early as fifty-five years ago.

CHAPTER 15 

Mining for Coal and Gold

TH HE SPUR from Lehigh to the mines in the Territory had been completed, and in October of '89 Ben wrote the following interesting letter to Mr. Enos, of Wall Street, New York City:

Your favor of the 4th inst. at hand. I am much obliged to you for the interest you are taking in the establishment of a hospital here. Everything is moving along satisfactorily at the mines, and our output is increasing daily. Last week we got out 85 cars, and expect this week to set at least 90 cars. We have been delivering only 60 cars of this to the M.K. & T., and selling the balance to commercial consumers. The McJames mine is turning out first class, and is pronounced superior to the McAlester in quality by everybody that has tried it. We have been having some trouble to get miners as fast as we need them, but have sent word to various places where we have learned that men could be had, and hope soon to be fully supplied. 

We now have room in the mines for thirty additional miners which we expect to have within a very few days. If nothing unforeseen prevents, we will produce 500 tons per day within thirty days. Mr. * * * told me when he left that he would return in a week, but he has not come yet. Should he not come I think that we will be better off than if he does. He is unquestionably a very slow man, and I think defective in general business ability. We have now 38 houses completed, and we are building others at the rate of about one per day, so as to supply the men as fast as we can get them. 

Everything connected with the mines is in better shape than it has ever been before. I think you should feel no uneasiness should * * * not return, as the mines can be run just as well without him as with him. It is my opinion that the blunders made by him, together with the delay caused by his action at the start has, or will, cost the company at least $25,000. 

All men, however, are liable to make blunders, and it is possible that I may overestimate the extent of his. Hoping that everything will move rapidly from this on, and our expectations be realized.

[142] 

It will be noted from the above letter that things had not panned out so well with the coal mine as Ben had hoped. In an earlier letter to Mr. Enos, he had hoped to be delivering these 500 tons per day to the railroad by August 1st. Now, although they have actually got under way, he has had to postpone the date for such delivery of tonnage until about the end of the first week of November. Behind this delay lie all the worries and annoyances that Ben had with the mine, and he is not out of the deal, and the thing is not settled, until June 2nd, as the reader will see later.

Like every good businessman of the period, Ben took a flyer at western mining. The character of this venture is not to be confused at all with such a scheme as the coal mining deal. The latter was a much surer thing, and the risks involved were not similar in the least. The following letter of Ben's - one of several on the subject - will give a good idea of the man and the material. 

This letter was mailed first to "Camp Carson," Colorado; returned with the notation that there was no such post office; and then redirected to Lake City, Hinsdale County, Colorado. This detail, small as it is, is eloquent of the whole nature of such enterprises. The letter is to W. G. Franklin, and bears the date of 

November 12th, 1889. 

Your favor of the 4th inst. at hand. From your report of what you have struck in the Dunderberg, I think it quite certain that there is no vein on the property, and I think the existence of a paying deposit of mineral on the claim is quite doubtful. I do not feel inclined to spend any more money on the property at present unless, by the time you have received this, you have reached ore that will pay expense of further development. I am fully satisfied now that the Camp is chiefly of the deposit character, and I therefore have more confidence in the properties further down the slope on this side, but as you say no one can tell certainly until they have dug down to see. I think it best to await further developments by other parties before spending too much money. Work has now proceeded far enough on the

Mining for Coal and Gold [143] Dunderberg to entitle us to a patent on the property, so it is safe to let it rest until the character of the camp is more fully determined. Should you strike ore that will pay expense, I will come out in the spring and arrange to push the work. Please send me an exact statement of expenditure and work done on the Dunderberg up to the date of receiving this, and I will remit whatever you have expended over $1000. According to our contract you were not authorized to expend more than $1000 without my consent. 

But what you have done up to this time I now approve and will pay for. 

Please advise me how deep the shaft on the Raven now is, and how the material at bottom looks. Should you strike any mineral of consequence within 50 feet, I think it would be best to tunnel from point showing best mineral prospect so as to settle the question of deposit or no deposit. 

Please let me know on what part of the Anna claim the mineral of which you speak has been found, and what quantity there appears to be of it. How far is it from the line of the Raven? Has anything been struck on the 

George, and how is the mineral holding out on the St. Jacobs? Hoping to hear something has been struck when you write again. 

This letter, of course, shows clearly enough that this was not an ordinary mining venture in the usual sense, where a man took stock in a company, and either made money or lost it, depending on the luck, the intelligence, or the honesty of the management. 

Ben was evidently well acquainted with the locale of the mine, for his directions to Franklin are specific, and show either a total recall of the mining terrain or that he had an accurate plat before him, or both. Further, it appears from the above letter that this was a partnership venture between him and Franklin. The indications are that the financial responsibility was wholly Ben's. 

In the middle of December, '89, there is a follow-up letter to 

Mr. Franklin in Colorado:

Your favor of the 7st inst. at hand. I hope your expectations relative to the Dunderberg mine will be realized, but I have little confidence in the value of any vein in the Carson Camp. My opinion is that what paying mineral has been found in the Camp has been exclusively deposit, and not vein matter. My hope for the future of the Camp is based entirely on the deposit theory, and I believe that money spent in hunting for veins is money thrown away. Should you never find anything in the Dunderberg

[144] 

mine, I wish you not to feel that you are under the least obligation to me on account of what I have invested in the mine. I went into the enterprise well knowing the uncertainty of all mining prospects. I was just leaving for the East when your letter of former date was received, and instructed my book-keeper to answer it. I hope to get out to the Gap early next season, and we will then decide what is best to do about the mine. Mrs. Munson joins me in sending kindest regards to Mrs. Franklin, and best wishes for your prosperity.

This is not only a fine, generous letter from the writer, but it also gives us a glimpse of Mr. Franklin, who appears to have been genuinely concerned, and felt somewhat responsible, that Ben's investment had not turned out too well up until this time.

CHAPTER 16 Always New Fields to Conquer 

ON 

N NOVEMBER 16TH, '89, Ben wrote a long letter to Tom Randolph of Sherman, Texas. The letter explains itself, but its fairness and clarity should be specially pointed out. 

Believing that the mutual welfare of both Denison and Sherman would be greatly improved by the construction of a railway line between the two places, I am prompted to address you this letter.

As the two towns are now situated, each has important railway connections that would greatly benefit the other could some arrangement be made so as to permit cheap and convenient shipment by the citizens of either town over all the lines entering the other. I believe this can be accomplished by the construction of a line to be used as a transfer road for all the roads not entering both towns, as well as for a rapid transit line for passenger traffic between the towns. If such a road were built it would practically give to Sherman the M.K. & T. and the D. & W.V. railways; and to Denison the Texas & Pacific and Cotton Belt lines; and at the same time put the two towns into such close contact as to make them practically one aggregation of capital, operating as competitors of Dallas and Ft. Worth, instead of continuing the folly of each other's destruction. If this road were built, I think there would be no trouble to lease trackage over it to the four roads mentioned, or arrange to transfer the cars of all these lines from one city to the other at such a figure as would practically amount to the same thing. 

It is an enterprise that can only be made successful by the hearty cooperation of both places, and this can be accomplished only by putting the enterprise on such a basis that the interests of each town will be equally and impartially subserved. The road can be built at a cost of $100,000 without equipment.

We have a charter that could be used for the purposes of constructing

[146] 

the road known as the Denison & Sherman Ry Co., and a preliminary survey has been run between the two towns. Now I wish to propose to you that if the people of Sherman will subscribe for one half of the stock in this road, the people of Denison will take the other half, and the road can be constructed at once. I would suggest that a construction company be organized, the stock of which shall be owned equally by the citizens of the two towns, and that the present railway co. contract with the construction company for the construction of the road for the consideration of $ . . . in capital stock of the railway company, and $ . . . in first mortgage bonds on the road and property of the company. 

As soon as the road is completed and put in operation the bonds would be sold, the road would be fully equipped, and the remaining funds and the stock be equally divided among the subscribers to the stock of the construction company pro rata. I believe that in constructing a railroad some money can be made clear, and that the stock will pay a handsome dividend. 

Should your people entertain this proposition I am willing that such a contract shall be drawn between the subscribers to the stock as will fully protect the citizens of each town against any deviation from the agreed basis, and purposes for which the enterprise is undertaken. I believe that the sooner the two towns begin to engage in common enterprises of this kind, the better it will be for both of them. 

I do not suggest this enterprise in order to get in the way of the proposed electric motor line, but because said enterprise does not meet the most urgent needs of the two towns; that is, the transfer of freight between the railways entering the two places. Please advise me what you think of this enterprise, and whether you think the people of your city will take hold of it.

Ben was in New York early in the year of '91, and there talked with Mr. Gould about the prospect of the T. & P.'s* building the line between Denison and Sherman. He had personally obligated himself for the terminal property in the former town and was, of course, anxious to get out from under. He was even willing to let the Bonham road wait if the Sherman-Denison deal could be swung. * Ben was instrumental in bringing the T. & P. into Denison (securing the rightof-way for them in 1895 by getting up a subscription list and himself subscribing $4500).

Always New Fields to Conquer 

[147] One of the remarkable qualities of Ben Munson was that, during his active years, he was never discouraged with one undertaking to the point of allowing this discouragement to blind him to the possibilities in another direction.

About the first of August of the year '90, just before he is to leave for the "Gap" - the visit he spoke of in his letter to Franklin - he writes to his old partner, Jot Gunter, that he has a little scheme on for making some money, and wants to associate Gunter in it. At the end of the following month he writes to Mr. Enos, in New York, that he has a good prospect of selling the mine and its adjunct railway in the coal fields to an independent company. 

He prices the holdings at $1,000,000, and the agent will expect a commission of $25,000. He is thoroughly disgusted with coal business, and advises Mr. Enos that they should all try to get out of it the best way they can. One of the great worries had been to secure a competent superintendent for the mines, and past managers had made serious and costly mistakes. 

Under the same date there is a letter to Colgate Hoyt on the same subject, and in the same tone. He wants Mr. Hoyt to try to find a competent man to run the mine; but if this seems not feasible he is willing to go north himself to look for one. In a November letter to Mr. William Bond of Wall Street, Dr. J. M. Ford is revealed as the agent representing the company that is dickering for the purchase of the Oklahoma interests. Dr. Ford is also to act as a representative for Ben and the Denison people on one hand, and for Mr. M. V. B. Stimson of Boston on the other, as the latter is interested in building an iron foundry in Denison. Ben is willing to take stock personally in the enterprise, and, with his usual enthusiasm and thoroughness, points out the advantages of his region and his town.

In March of this same year he has something in the wind again. 

The scheme is outlined in a letter to C. E. Lyman, of Minneapolis:

[148] 

Your favor of the 13th inst. received. We are preparing, at Denison, to organize an Iron and Steel Company for the purpose of establishing smelters here. It is quite likely that we will desire to make arrangements with your company for ore to mix with the ores we have adjacent to Denison. We have demonstrated the fact that a first class coke, well suited to the reduction of iron, can be made from the Territory coal. The coke is pronounced superior to the Connellsville by Pennsylvania experts. The company we propose to organize may wish to become interested in Llano and Mason county ores, and it may be that we can make some kind of consolidation with your company to control the whole business. If you should pass through Denison in the near future, I would be pleased to have you call and discuss the matter with me. We expect to organize a company with $5,000,000 capital, that will become an important factor in the steel production of America. There is no other point in the United States where first class Bessemer ore, and first class coke, can be placed together as cheaply as here. Is Mr. Wakefield now in Texas, or who is in charge of your 

Llano property? I may run down to look over the situation in the near future, and if not acquainted with your manager would like a letter of introduction. I remember you very well, and am glad to renew our acquaintance.

As if this were not enough to occupy his complete attention, he writes, early that same summer, to see if he can get in on the ground floor of a new town development. The letter is to W. M. D. 

Lea, of Velasco: 

Your favor of the 10th inst. received; also a letter from Mr. Linley stating that he has invested some money with you, and is well pleased with the prospects of the new town. I would like to invest something in your place, but at present am very short of money. I have 18 sections of land in Dallam 

County, adjoining the Capital Syndicate land, which I would trade for property in your town. Enclosed I herewith hand you sketch of same. If you can effect a sale of this land to the Capital Syndicate, or are willing to exchange property in Velasco for it, I will invest the entire proceeds in your city. I hold the Dallam County lands at $1.50 per acre cash, which is cheap for the land. I think it would be of some advantage to you to have me interested, as I can help boom your place from this end of the State. If you think you can make this deal please advise me, and I will come down and select the property. If the Capital Syndicate only wish to purchase the

Always New Fields to Conquer 

[149] Dallam lands immediately north of theirs, I will trade them whatever amount they desire on the same basis. years

A good deal of the remainder of Ben's correspondence during the '90 and '91 is filled with the difficulties connected with the Southwestern Coal & Improvement Co. This was the company that had been organized concurrently with the Denison & Washita Valley Railway, and was set in motion to handle specifically the coal business that Ben and his associates expected to develop in the Territory on the line of the new railroad. Ben secured the leases on the coal lands from John M. Hodges, a Choctaw Indian, and was given a separate contract by the company to build ten miles of track connecting the coal mines with the M.K. & T. at Lehigh, Indian Territory. Ben was the first president of the coal company, but later Colgate Hoyt, a Rockefeller broker, held this position. 

The head office of the company was maintained in New York, but a local office existed on the ground, at Coalgate, I.T. The directorates of the coal and railway companies were interlocking, and the two largest stockholders in the railroad were W. B. Munson and the Southwestern Coal & Improvement Company. 

The officers of the coal company had a great deal of trouble, not only with the management at the mine, but also with labor employed there. The disputes, as related in the voluminous correspondence connected with the mine, sound very modern. Both the coal company and the railway were eventually sold to the 

M.K. & T., and Ben made a profit on both these deals — although the affair of the coal company was long drawn out. It merits some particular attention. 

In September of '91 Ben accepted from the Southwestern Coal & Improvement Co., as a settlement for salaries, traveling expenses, and all claims valid up to that time, a claim that the coal company held for $10,000 against the San Antonio & Aransas

[150] 

Pass Railway. His letter to Perry J. Lewis of San Antonio, of October 31st, 1891, states his position clearly enough. 

Please advise me whether or not there is any prospect of any early settlement of the Southwestern Coal & Improvement Co. claim against the 

S.A. & A.P. Ry. Co. Can you not get Mr. Yoakum to use his influence with 

Mr. Lott to settle this claim on a fair basis. I will accept ninety cents on the dollar, cash, for the claim now, but unless a settlement of some kind is made the matter is liable to become very embarrassing to both Mr. Lott and Mr. Yoakum, as I consider them personally responsible for the condition in which this claim now stands. Had they done their duty as R.R. officials, our Company would long since have the cash for this claim. If you do not settle on the terms indicated in this letter, please advise me when I will find Mr. Lott in San Antonio, and I will come down and see him personally. 

On December 3rd he wrote to Mr. Bond of New York City:

Your favor of the 29th ult. at hand. So far I have not been able to get any satisfaction out of the Aransas Pass folks, and I fear it will be some time before anything can be done about it. The Court has issued an order restraining the Receivers from paying anything on the class of claims to which ours belongs for the moment, so I presume we will have to wait until the Reorganization Committee get ready to take the property out of the Receivers' hands, or accept whatever compromise they are willing to offer. Possibly you can ascertain in New York as to the prospect for early reorganization. Should I succeed in making any kind of settlement of this matter will advise you at once. 

On May 27th of the next year, '92, he wrote to Mr. Uriah Lott:

Mr. Perry has informed me of your proposition to settle the claims of the 

Southwestern Coal & Improvement Co. against the S.A. & A.P. Ry., by delivering eighty cents on the dollar, twenty cents of which would be paid in cash, and the balance in reorganization certificates. As I know nothing about the value of the certificates, I cannot accept the proposition you make.

But later the matter seems to have been definitely closed, and rather satisfactorily so, as the following quoted document will show:

Always New Fields to Conquer 

[151] 

STATE OF TEXAS COUNTY OF GRAYSON 

Know all men by these presents, that, I, W. B. Munson, of Denison, Texas, in consideration of eight thousand ($8000) dollars to me in hand paid by Uriah Lott of San Antonio, Texas, the receipt of which is hereby acknowledged, have bargained and sold, and do hereby sell, transfer and convey unto said Uriah Lott all my right, title and interest in and to certain claims originally held by the Southwestern 

Coal & Improvement Company against the San Antonio & Aransas Pass Railway Company. . . . 

Witness my hand this 2nd day of June 1892.

W. B. MUNSON, by 

J. T. MUNSON, Agt. and Atty. in fact

There is an interesting implication which can be drawn from the above-quoted correspondence. It appears that, although the claim against the railroad may have been actually turned over to Munson by the coal company in September of '91, as stated above, Ben did not reveal the fact of his actual ownership of the claim until time came for settlement. The tone of the first letter indicates that he was writing to Mr. Lewis as the agent of the coal company, but the real ownership of the claim is revealed in the receipt for the $8000 quoted in full above. This is a perfectly legitimate business procedure. Ben evidently felt that the claim, if pressed in the name of the coal company, might carry more weight with the railway people, than if put forward in the name of an individual, even though that individual had organized, and been president of, the coal company.

A Finger on the

Pulse of the Times ¶OWARD THE LAST of the year '91 Ben called a meeting of the com- TOWA 

missioners of the World's Fair Exhibit Association, doubtless the one to be held in Chicago two years later; and he is also willing and anxious to do some lobbying against a bill which has been introduced in Washington. The bill, he says, would greatly increase the cost of coal shipped into Texas from Oklahoma, and thus would interfere with the prosperity of the former state. He thinks he can get the Texas delegation to oppose it solidly in Washington, and feels that a man should be sent on there to work actively against it. About a month after this, in the latter part of 

January '92, he wrote to his old business associate, John Corwith, that Mr. Gould's forfeiting his contract, after putting up $25,000 earnest money, had had a discouraging effect on the land business. 

But so much as usual as to be almost comical - "there are other things now in prospect," and he expected conditions to improve greatly before long.

This man could not be downed; he simply did not know what it meant to give up.

In the spring he was planning one of his many trips to Wagon Wheel Gap, but he took time to write to Jot Gunter to remind him that there was a boom on in Llano County, and that he thought they should try to sell the land they owned in that county while things were favorable. In this same month, May, he sent a

A Finger on the Pulse of the Times 

[153] draft to cover his dues and subscription to the Alumni Association of his alma mater in Lexington, Kentucky. He hoped to attend the June meeting with Mrs. Munson, and so wrote to 

W. S. Bell, of Lexington.

It is not known to what land Ben referred specifically, but on 

May 27th of '92, he wrote a characteristic note to Gunter and 

Summerfield in Dallas.

Should Governor Hogg be re-elected, I presume our hope of getting a patent on this land will be very slim, as I understand he proposes to make the holders of such claims disgorge. 

It may be that Ben was taking a too-poor view of the situation, and it is not known whether or not he did have to "disgorge," but it is a fact that Hogg was elected for another term. Ben may even have lost a little money on the election, for, a few days before the voting was to take place, he was inquiring of a Fort Worth friend if the latter thought it would be safe to risk even money on Clark, the defeated candidate.

That same month he was looking forward to the projected visit to Denison of Colis P. Huntington, and it is certain that whatever disappointment he experienced as a result of Hogg's re-election was submerged in the excitement incident to the arrival in his city of one of the great railroad builders of his time. In public affairs politics was a remote side line for him; business deals were the very essence of his public life. Moreover, this was not to be the last time that Ben and Huntington were to put their heads together over deals in general, and a railroad in particular. 

The projected visit of Mr. Huntington did not materialize for, early in the following month, December, Ben wrote to a friend that he had had a letter from Huntington in New York, and that the latter was much interested in the "proposition.” Acting on the hint contained in Mr. Huntington's letter, Ben left for New York before the middle of the month, and while there was a guest at

[154] 

the Astor House. The inference would seem to be that he was trying to interest Huntington in the deep water proposition for 

Aransas Pass. On January 25th, '93, he wrote to J. W. Maddox of Austin: 

I have been waiting on some parties connected with the M.K. & T. 

Ry. Co. who promised to look into the question of deep water at Aransas 

Pass, and who I had hoped before this would have sent a man to examine the situation, but for some reason the matter has been unreasonably delayed. I think at least $100,000 more money will be required to make a success of the deep water scheme at the Pass, and unless a pretty strong combination can be made, I fear that the expenditure of more money there will result in a loss. If the parties with whom I have been figuring do not take some steps in the matter very soon, I would favor taking up the matter in connection with Mr. Breckenridge, and see what amount of money can be secured to put into the enterprise. It occurs to me that the people of San 

Antonio ought to take a lively interest in this matter and put up the necessary money to carry it through. Of course if the M.K. & T. people could be interested in it, and if the construction of their road to the Pass could be secured, that would be the best solution of the question. • • About this time he is also pushing the sale of some Panhandle land that he owned with Gunter. He writes of 40,000 acres in 

Hutchinson County, and 35,000 acres in Potter County, this latter being subject to irrigation from the Canadian River - about onethird of it and for this reason he has set the price of $2.50 per acre on it. 

At this time also he engaged the legal services of William H. 

Seldon, of Washington, to represent him and the First National Bank of Denison* in a claim that the latter held against the Chickasaw Nation in Oklahoma. This was for money advanced by the bank, and secured on acceptances of the Chickasaws, and was used to erect school buildings in the Nation. After the money was advanced, a local wrangle took place among the Indians, and the acceptances were refused. * Ben was made president of the First National Bank of Denison in March, 1893. 

Prior to that time he served as a director.

A Finger on the Pulse of the Times 

[155] 

On March 10th, '93, he wrote to T. B. Wheeler of Aransas Pass, whom he addressed as "Governor."

I have just wired you that President Rouse and Mr. McDougall, of the "Katy," would be in your place Monday next. They are looking over Texas prospective deep-water ports. If you can make any trade with them at all, of course I expect a reasonable commission, or a share of the donated stock in compensation for my efforts in the matter.

This would seem to indicate that Ben, due to his railroad experience and connections with the bigger railroad builders of the country, was acting as a broker, representing a company that was attempting to utilize the possibilities of deep water for another port on the Gulf Coast besides Galveston. But the matter dragged on and on and was never put through. 

In the month of May, '93, he wrote to George Boynton of Chicago a letter which is amusingly eloquent of something which 

Ben himself admitted: 

Yours of the 21st inst. received. I do not wish to invest or become interested in any patent process. Have never invested in a patent in my life that 

I did not lose all I had put into it. Hoping that you may find someone else who has been more fortunate in this business. 

On June 7th, '93, there is a long personal letter to his brother,

J.T., who is in Chicago, and the letter is so characteristic that it is quoted in full: 

DEAR BROTHER: Yours of the 3rd inst. received. I am glad to hear you are well and enjoying the Fair. Everything is very quiet here. There is very little or no demand for property here, and prices continue to decline. It is now evident that unless something happens to stimulate values the *** estate will not pay out, as they will not be able to sell property much faster than will be required to pay interest on the indebtedness. The weather has been cool and pleasant here so far and we have had an abundance of rain in nearly all parts of the State, so that the crop prospects are first class. 

There is no prospect for anything being done for either the Cotton Mill or any railway enterprise for the present, on account of the extremely unfavorable monetary condition.

[156] 

My folks are mostly up in the country and are all well. Business at the bank is moderately fair, but money is getting scarce. If you should hear of an exceptionally good place to get quarters for the World's Fair during the latter part of July and August, please advise. What would a suite of two rooms with three beds cost at the hotel at which you are stopping, if taken for a month or more? I think the rates will be very reasonable during the months of July and August, as the hot weather will prevent the crowd from being very large during those months. If you are down in the City and feel like it you might call on David J.
