He is well posted in the City and will enjoy meeting you. 

Under that same date, June 7th, '93, he wrote to Colis P. Huntington, at that time president of the Southern Pacific, to inform him that the people who were interested in bringing deep water to Aransas Pass were making another effort to interest outside capital in the development of a harbor there. 

The 10th of the following month, July, he expected to be off soon for Kansas City, but before he left he wrote, on the 15th, to the Texas Railroad Commission: 

Referring to your circular letter of May 26th, a copy of which was sent to me, as President of the Sherman, Shreveport & Southern Railway, I desire to say that this property has, as you are probably aware, but recently come into the possession of the newly organized S.S. & S. Ry. Co., which was incorporated after the sale, under the State Receivership, for the purpose of acquiring and extending the road. We are of course unable to furnish definite information sought for in your circular, by reason of the circumstances stated. The line, originally a narrow gauge, was widened to that of a standard gauge under the State Receivership, and in that renewed condition was transferred to this Company, and having been so recently thus acquired we have but little of the information sought for in your letter; but whatever is in possession of the present management of the road on the subject of your circular will be furnished when desired, and every effort made to inform you as far as is in our power.

A Finger on the Pulse of the Times 

[157] 

A month later he is off to Chicago for a week, and on the 31st of August he reports that the money shortage is very severe, and that the First National Bank will need every dollar it can lay its hands on. Four days later this second letter probably written to a man who was not trying to borrow money or extend a notehe reports that they are still on their feet, but not as flush with funds as they would like to be; although conditions are improving steadily, and they expect "to make some large collections very shortly."

CHAPTER 18 Fall of 1893 and Spring of 1894 

TH THE FALL of '93, and the spring and early summer of '94, were periods of comparative stringency for Ben. In a man of his holdings this does not mean, of course, that he was actually pressed; it merely means that he was having to pull in the tentacles with which he usually felt out the possibilities of new investments. That autumn he was written up in Railway Age, published in Chicago; and a little later the New York Tribune requested a photograph and biographical data to be used in a business supplement that they were putting out. 

On October 8th he reports "the closest money market" he has ever experienced in Texas; and in that same month he writes to a friend in Denver, Colorado, that "unless the purchasing clause of the Silver Bill is speedily repealed," they will have a "long sweat of it." He believes that the "repeal of that vicious measure would bring immediate financial relief to the whole country." 

But the depression persists. On December 14th he writes that he is in "no condition to make investments in outside property”; and on March 6th of the following year, '94, it is the same story - "not in shape to make any investments now."

Ben's letters about this time show he was having trouble with the Kansas City Clay & Coal Co. There was a debt of $20,000 outstanding. Half of this was owing to the miners, and there were no funds with which to meet their demands. Moreover no credit could be secured. This latter doubtless means that the stock-

Fall of 1893 and Spring of 1894 [159] holders or incorporators did not care to endorse any paper individually. 

The Board of Directors found themselves in the highly embarrassing position of having two superintendents of the mine. They had discharged a Mr. X, who was on the ground, and had reinstated a Mr. Y, who desired to be on the ground. Mr. X, with the unpaid miners at his back, refused categorically to surrender the premises to the newly elected superintendent; and that was the way the matter was likely to continue to stand until $2000 could be raised "some way" with which to dispossess Mr. X and reopen the mines. 

There is a characteristic letter of May 5th, '94, to the receiver of the coal company: 

Your dispatch asking to draw for $900.00 just received. I have answered no, will advance no more money, which I hereby confirm. 

I am at a loss to know what you are doing with the coal, as I am being called upon to pay the entire payrolls. At the rate you are now proceeding it would take $10,000 to run you through until fall, and the longer you run the worse you get.

The following month he had a run-in with the Board of Equalization of Cottle County, Texas; he wrote them a letter whose sarcasm was not, it is to be hoped, so heavily veiled, but that they must have "got" it.

I am rendering my land at just what I am offering to sell it, and have held it for the past year without finding a buyer. Your Hon. Board, or either of you, are hereby authorized to dispose of this land at seventy-five cents per acre. And if you, or either of you, find a cash buyer, within a reasonable time, or before I have sold it to other parties, I hereby agree to pay you a commission of five per cent for effecting the sale. 

A cotton mill now makes a somewhat unobtrusive appearance in Ben's correspondence. On October 30th, '94, to Mr. M. F. 

Foster of Milford, N. H., he writes:

We were recently visited by a Mr. Jacob Koehler of Providence, R. I. 

He claimed to be sent here by parties who desire to invest in a cotton mill,

[160] 

to examine and report on this plant. He looked the mill over carefully, and got all the information touching its cost and present condition obtainable. 

He did not explain to us who he represented, but referred us to Charles Fletcher of 173 Valley Street, Providence, whom we understand is a wealthy cotton manufacturer. We offered him the mill at $60,000. . . . 

In the midst of his business activities he found the time to writę to Miss B. T. Capen, Wellesley College, Northampton, Massachusetts: 

DEAR MADAM: My daughter, Linley, who is attending your school, seems to have the impression that her music teacher, Mr. Fletcher, is not quite so fine a musician as Dr. Blodgett, who also teaches some of your pupils. 

She is quite ambitious to take lessons from the very best talent available, and I am anxious to gratify her. If it will be no inconvenience to you, and you feel disposed to do so, I will be pleased if you could arrange, after the Holidays, to allow her to take music of Dr. Blodgett. She may be mistaken in her estimate of the teachers, but she will doubtless be better pleased to take lessons of the one she thinks superior.

Linley seems to be very well pleased with her school, and I think she is doing nicely. I would be pleased to have your opinion of her progress, and any suggestions that you see fit to make regarding her.

Hoping you may be able to grant the change requested in this letter without the slightest inconvenience, I am, ...

Surely this was a reasonable and eminently sensible letter, and not at all in the tradition of those parents who act so irrationally in regards to the teachers of their children. But Miss Capen, as will be seen by the following letter quoted in full, was not to be touched either by the superior endowment of the student in question, or by the reasoned good sense of the girl's father. This second letter is dated January 15th, '95, six weeks after the first one cited. 

MY DEAR LINLEY: I have today received the bill from Miss Capen for your second half year's tuition amounting to $351.45. I herewith enclose you 

New York exchange for $375.00 payable to the order of Linley A. Munson, which you can endorse over to Miss Capen, and get her to sign enclosed bill and pay you the difference of $23.55.

Fall of 1893 and Spring of 1894 

[161] I wrote Miss Capen regarding your music some time ago, but she has not even condescended to answer. Has she mentioned the matter to you?

Your letter to Maud came this morning and we are all glad to hear from you. We have been having some very cold weather for Texas lately, but no snow or rain. Our water supply is running very low, and the town is in danger from fire, as there is not water enough to get any fire pressure. 

It looks like rain now however, and we are hopeful of early relief. 

I have been away from home a great deal of the time this winter, as I had to spend much time in Kansas City looking after the coal mines there. 

Denison is dragging along about as usual, still hoping for something to turn up to give her a boost, but still disappointed by delays. 

Your mama has been suffering from a bad cold and a stiff neck for the past few days, but she is some better now, and has succeeded in getting a white woman to help her. All the other folks are quite well.

The children all enjoyed their Christmas and Holidays very much, and were all glad to know that you had such a nice time at Worcester. 

When you need more money, let me know. Write often and take good care of yourself. 

Your loving father,

CHAPTER 19 Excursions in Rails and Sales 

ARTHER back in this biography reference was made to an investment that Ben made in Duluth, Minnesota, real estate.

This investment was made at the time that Duluth was booming, and a former friend of Munson had represented that the town was sure to grow and develop in the direction of Ben's property. There is no reason to doubt the sincerity of this recommendation, but the fact is that the investment, of itself, was a mistake. There are literally dozens of letters in the correspondence relative to Ben's efforts to dispose of the property; his objections to the amount of taxes levelled against it; and tentative schemes for improvement that would make the property more salable. The investment, though not large, caused him a great deal of worry, for he, like any good businessman, had pride in his judgment, and took only what he considered good risks, even when the amount involved was small. There was, however, a strikingly fortunate outcome of the initial trip to Duluth.

While in the Minnesota city Ben bought $10,000 worth of stock in one of the iron companies that had holdings in the now famous Mesaba Range. It was on this same trip, and through the medium of this considerable sum of money, that Ben formed the acquaintance of the iron people to whom he wrote relative to the foundry and iron situation both in Denison and Llano County. Letters relative to this scheme that was never developed have already been quoted.

The iron company in which Ben made his investment was, not

Excursions in Rails and Sales 

[163] so long after, bought in by John D. Rockefeller, and a new corporation was formed, known as the Consolidated Lake Superior Iron Mining Company. As a result of the manipulation, Ben received stock in the new company, worth considerably more than his original investment. Later on the Consolidated became part of the far greater United States Steel Company, and Ben, in exchange for his holdings in Consolidated, received equal amounts of preferred and common stock in the United States Steel Company. The $10,000 had soared to dizzy heights. Although the trip was made to invest in real estate which was a failure, and resulted ultimately in a loss, the mining venture was so fabulously successful that Ben certainly had no cause for complaint.

In October of '95 he went east with the twofold purpose of visiting some of his old business friends, and also of placing his daughter Linley again in school. Early in the following year he is considering changing some of his investments, and writes to Mr. 

Walkup of Chicago that he owns 50,000 acres of Texas lands that are unencumbered and valued at about $5.00 per acre, and that he might consider investing this $250,000 in Chicago real estate, provided that the property in the latter city passed muster to him. 

In February of '96 he is seriously considering trading for a large apartment building in New York City, known as the Metropolitan. He had friends on the ground in New York investigating the property, but the report did not please him, and he did not touch the property in either of the two big cities. This same year he is still agitated over the silver question, and wrote to Charles H. 

Jones, the editor of the St. Louis Post-Dispatch:

Enclosed I hand you copy of an open letter to President Cleveland, suggesting some lines of argument and methods of treatment of the silver question, which do not appear to me to have been heretofore fully covered. 

The paper is brief and intended more to call attention to lines of argument that might be pursued than as a treatment of the question. If you find upon perusal that the paper is considered worthy of publication, you

[164] 

will oblige me by giving it to the public. I have sent copies to several other papers in different parts of the country that will receive it at about the same time that you do. If you conclude to publish, please send marked copy and oblige....

The reader will recall that it was in this year of '96 that Ben succeeded in getting the Texas & Pacific R.R. into Denison. He followed this up by helping secure the Missouri, Oklahoma & Gulf (now the Kansas, Oklahoma & Gulf) into Denison in 1910.*

It was also in this year that his friend, Mr. J. P. Crerar of Canada, came to Denison and bought the street railway, the original charter of which had been secured by W.B. This traction company line was completely electrified by 1901, and in 1908, under the presidency of J. F. Strickland, was extended to Dallas and Waco.

Ben was elected a member of the Board of Directors of the Texas 

Traction Co. on January 28th, 1913, and when this company became the Texas Electric Railway in 1917 he was named a director, and remained on the board until his resignation in January of 1929. 

On March 9th, 1899, Ben wrote to W. A. Bradford, Jr., president of the H. & S. Ry. Co., Hutchinson, Kansas, a letter which first calls attention to an entirely new undertaking. The whole deal was neat and, taking into consideration the usual difficulties, quickly turned, so that this and additional pertinent letters are quoted in full.

Your favor of February 14th has awaited my return from the East. While in Washington I met your Mr. Whitlaw, and explained to him fully the condition of the Galveston, Houston & La Porte Ry., which we recently bought in at Receiver's sale. The road cost upwards of a million dollars * Mr. J. R. Handy stated (June 26, 1944) that in the twenty-six years he worked with W. B. Munson the latter spent $500,000 in his efforts to bring railroads to Denison.

Excursions in Rails and Sales 

[165] to construct, is shell ballasted for nearly its entire length of fifty-seven miles of main line, and eight miles of siding. The road has been appraised by the State engineers, and another very competent engineer, at upwards of a million and a quarter, and the State Commission would authorize a bond issue of that amount on the property if desired. There is three hundred and seventy-five thousand dollars still due the court on this property, upon the payment of which the court will make a clear title to the property, freed of encumbrance. The balance due must be paid before 

April 1st. If you are in a position to join us in this purchase and raise the $375,000 yet to be paid, we will make a very attractive deal with you. 

Immediate action is necessary. If you think you can do anything in the matter wire us on receipt of this, as we must make definite arrangements very soon.

Regarding the graded road from Denison by way of Bonham to Wolf City, amounting to about fifty miles of graded line, with right-of-way, terminals, and depot grounds in the three principal towns, valued at nearly two hundred thousand dollars. This entire property, with a franchise through the Indian Territory to the Kansas line and a branch west to Ft. Sill, can be had on your own terms, if you will guarantee the construction of the road and the location of the shops and general offices for Texas at Denison. There are parties, however, now figuring on building the 

Texas part of this road, but if you are prepared to join in payment for the Galveston and Houston Line, we could put the whole deal into your hands on most favorable terms, but immediate action will be absolutely necessary. 

I can meet you in Kansas City on twenty-four hours notice if you are ready to take up this matter.

On April 14th Ben wrote to his brother, J. T. Munson, in care of the Capitol Hotel at Houston: 

Your favor of 12th inst. received. I think you made a mistake in putting the price on the property to Mr. House before hearing from Huntington. 

You should have impressed House with the earnestness of our purpose to build north unless a satisfactory trade could be made, and keep a stiff upper lip until the time comes to cut the price to Huntington himself. 

From a trading standpoint, it is a weak move to reduce your price before you are asked to do so. It will doubtless not be necessary for Mr. House to go to San Francisco to see Mr. Huntington, as he can conduct negotiations by wire without expense, and much more rapidly than by travelling. I

[166] 

think you are doing the proper thing in getting a proposition from the I. & G.N. people. 

If Mr. Huntington does not make a proposition shortly, I think I had better prepare to go East with a view of financing the entire line through the Territory and on to Houston. With the present money market, and what we now have to offer, I think this can be done without any serious difficulty. Should Mr. S., or the other St. L. parties, close negotiations now pending with them, no further efforts on our part would be necessary. You should be careful to maintain an appearance of independence and complete confidence in the property, and in our ability to handle it to advantage.

I think you had better take steps to get all the information possible regarding the country north of Houston, with a view of securing a route to be covered by an amendment to our franchise. You might open correspondence with the leading men in the different towns on the proposed line with a view of ascertaining the amount of subsidies that could be had, and the amount of business in sight for a railroad. If you had an available man to send over the line, it would be a good idea. Should you select such a man, impress him with the firmness of our purpose to build the line, and the earnestness of our desire to get all necessary facts to enable us to file a charter covering the best territory. In other words, go at it as though you intended, and had arrangements, to build the road. 

I believe it will be easier to finance the entire road from Houston through the Territory with strong Eastern parties, than it would be to handle a less portion of the line, and I am willing to undertake to finance the entire scheme as soon as it is determined that we can not make a quick deal with Huntington. 

Everything locally going on as usual. Folks all well. You had better destroy this letter, as some one might get hold of it, and show it to the wrong parties. 

Your affectionate Bro.

The railroad referred to in the two above-quoted letters was the successor to the Galveston, Houston & Laporte Railway Co., which was chartered January 30th, 1895, roughly four years before these cited letters. The new railroad was called the Galveston, Houston & Northern Railway. The first road went into a receivership in 1896, and the receivers were T. W. House and

Excursions in Rails and Sales [167] M. T. Jones. The receivers operated the line until October, 1898, at which time it was sold on foreclosure to Charles S. Broadhead of St. Louis. The date given for this sale in S. G. Reed's History of Texas Railroads must be in error, for this date of sale is put down as March 31st. But on March 9th Ben says, in a letter to

Mr. Bradford, that they have already bought in the road at a receiver's sale. This discrepancy might be explained by some such deal as the contract of sale's being signed at an early date, and the papers finally passing on the later date. 

The statistics and names pertinent to this enterprise will be found in a later section of this biography, but, in the meantime, there is a story from the late Mr. Handy which should be passed on to the reader. Mr. Handy related that Ben returned from a vacation with Mrs. Munson to find that his brother, J.T., had used $350,000 worth of notes owned by the firm to buy in the railroad for the firm.

Mr. Handy also confirmed as facts certain things which, from the letters themselves, appear to have been true. He said that Mr. 

Huntington had long wanted this short line, but that, for some reason, Ben and his brother were able to beat Huntington to the home plate when the road was disposed of through a receiver's sale. Mr. Handy, further, identified the "Mr. S." of St. Louis as 

Stillwell of the Kansas City Southern. Colonel Foster, a lawyer, represented Ben in the succeeding operations, in which the line was resold to Mr. Huntington the same year that the Munsons had bought in the line. Mr. Handy reported a profit of $25,000 accruing to the brothers from the deal. 

In later years Ben often congratulated himself on having been able to get out with a profit from under this deal for, as the really old-timers may already have noticed, the road was sold just one year before the disastrous Galveston hurricane and flood which wiped out the lower end of the railroad line.

CHAPTER 20 

The Denison Cotton Mill

CONSIDERABLE period of time now elapses between significant business deals, and the first one of great importance after the railroad from Galveston to Houston, is the painful, long-drawnout affair of the cotton mill in Denison, which has already made its appearance in these pages. 

There seem to be no early records showing the exact origin of the first cotton mill in Denison, but on July 27th, 1897, we do find 

Ben writing to Mr. M. F. Foster of Milford, N.H. The letter was written for his brother, J.T., who was in Colorado at that time. 

Ben says: the English party has failed so far to close a sale of the mill. . . think with you that some decided action should be had this fall, and I would favor advertising and selling the property as a whole for what it will bring, as I see nothing to be gained by hanging on to it longer. Hoping however, we may avoid this by closing an early sale. ... 

On September 4th, 1897, J. T. Munson writes to J. M. Ford of 

Kansas City, that the mill is still unsold and that their "patience in attempting to find a buyer is well-nigh exhausted." 

The most revealing document, however, is the following letter, written on November 4th, 1897, to E. L. Foster, of Independence,

Kansas: 

Yours of September 4th ult. to "Post Master" here has come to my attention. The machinery in the Denison Cotton Mill is not offered for sale separate from the building, but the entire property is offered at the extremely low figure of $50,000. The property cost originally about $265,000.

The Denison Cotton Mill [169] The machinery is all new and of the best makes, and has been well cared for, and is in good condition.

Enclosed I hand you brief description of the property with condensed list of the machinery, supplies etc. The title of the property is vested in 

M. F. Foster, of New Hampshire, and myself as trustees.

We have a proposition pending for sale of the property, the result of which we will know in a short time. Should we not consummate a deal with parties with whom we are now negotiating, we will then be free to negotiate with others. There is a great bargain in this property, and no better location for a cotton mill can be found in the United States than Denison. 

It has fine facilities for distribution; is the cheapest fuel point in the State, is exceptionally healthful, and is in the midst of one of the finest cotton producing districts in the entire South. The mill would long since have been in operation by local enterprise but for the want of local capital and practical experience necessary to complete and operate the mill. Are your clients disposed to consider the purchase of this property as a whole with a view to putting it in operation?

But a buyer was not found at this time, and in August of 1899 the American Cotton Spinning Company was incorporated, and purchased the property of this first mill from J. T. Munson for $50,000 in bonds. There are records that show that Ben repeatedly loaned money to the American Cotton Spinning Companyalthough he owned only one share of stock at the time of its incorporation - and from time to time he accepted stock in lieu of other payment.

To save his investment in the mill, Ben was forced to sacrifice what he knew to be a very valuable piece of property in Dallas, between Jackson and Commerce Streets, still the very heart of the city. He made this sacrifice in order to organize a new company which purchased the ground, equipment, machinery, etc., of the 

American Cotton Spinning Company when it failed. He made a desperate effort to interest the citizens of Denison in taking stock in this manufacturing enterprise, but since the two previous mills had gone under it was impossible to find other investors with sufficient nerve to float what they considered an unseaworthy

[170] 

craft. In the end Ben himself purchased more than sixty per cent of the original stock, and Munson & Bro., together with J. T. 

Munson, purchased an additional thirty per cent. The new company was incorporated in August, 1905, with a capital stock of $150,000, and with the authorization to issue bonds for a like amount. 

On October 3rd, 1905, Ben wrote to Mr. L. N. Cox, of Greenville, S.C.: 

You may offer the stock of the Denison Cotton Mill Co. to the parties you mention, or furnish me their names, and advise them to write me for terms if they are looking for a cotton mill investment; and if I make a sale to them I will pay 5% commission, 3% of which will be to you, and the other 2% to Mr. Stanfield. I do not want the impression to get out that the mill is on the market, as I expect to develop the property myself, and wish to preserve its credit. If the parties are in earnest about desiring to purchase the property, the most satisfactory thing would be for them to come out and look it over at once.

The lowest price I would consider taking for the stock, would be one hundred and fifty thousand dollars, which is par for it; and par for what bonds may be out by the time the property is purchased, which will probably be fifty or sixty thousand dollars worth by the time a trade could be closed, as I am putting in quite a quantity of new machinery and overhauling the plant generally. 

This deal apparently fell as flat as all of the efforts made in behalf of the early mills for, approximately three weeks later, on 

October 27th, 1905, W.B. wrote to J. A. Smith of Bessemer City, N.C., the former president of the American Cotton Spinning Company: 

Do you know of a boom town that would like to have a cotton mill at any old price?

This comes pretty close to being comic, and Ben was as conscious of this as anyone else would be. His head was bloody but unbowed, for four days later, on October 31st, he wrote the Manufacturer's Record that the mill would be making yarns for the

The Denison Cotton Mill [171] present, 10s to 20s two- and three-ply, and that they would be ready to start work two weeks from this date.

The management and stockholders soon learned that the capital stock was not sufficient to give them the latitude for operations that they needed to have; they discovered further that although all the bonds were owned by the same men who voted the stock, these bonds nevertheless practically destroyed the company's credit. And so, having in mind to rectify both these objections, in 

June, 1909, the stock was increased to $350,000. The bonds were retired, and the company now proceeded on an indebtedness-free basis. It began to make money. 

The mill was now definitely Ben's "child," and anyone attempting to cheat the company of any of its hard-won profits was bound to run up against his ruthless determination not only to ferret out the culprit, but also to recover every strayed dollar, right down to the last decimal point.

A trusted employee did hatch the scheme of shipping uninvoiced goods to a mythical company in a near-by city, and under the name of that company he would sell the filched goods to customers throughout the country. This was accomplished by the bogus company's having a postoffice box for its only address, and orders sent to it were taken from the box by a hired accomplice, and remailed to the original guilty party at Denison, who would then order the goods cleared from the warehouse in the latter city for shipment to the customer.

This stolen property bore bale numbers which were duplicates of others on production records. The profitable little enterprise continued for five years, until it was discovered by W.B. and his son, Ben Munson, Jr. W.B. was relentless in running down this long list of stolen goods, and his chief attorney on the case was 

Louis B. Eppstein of New York City, a former neighbor and whist companion who, as a boy, had resided at 331 West Gandy, just a

[172] 

block from the old brick house. Final settlements were not made until as late as 1917, but J. R. Handy would always insist with a chuckle that: "Mr. W.B. got more back than he ever lost. Why, when you stop to think," he would continue, "that he stripped that thief of every tangible asset he had to make up a part of the property he had stolen, and then traced every cockeyed bale and successfully sued all the receivers of the stolen goods, he was bound to come out on the plus side!"

Ben always controlled the largest block of stock in the company, and was its president until his death, and its real manager for many years. When he retired from active business, the management passed into the hands of his son, W. B. Munson, Jr., who achieved an outstanding position in the cotton textile industry.

CHAPTER 21 Twentieth Century Curtain Calls 

YEARS Denison had been in need of a surety company and FBen, ever trying to boost the town that in truth was his most beloved enterprise, was influential in organizing the Southwestern Surety Co. in 1909, and was its first president. This was an 

Oklahoma corporation, and was built just across the Red River Bridge on the Oklahoma side. It later was taken over by the 

Southern Surety Co. of Duluth, Minnesota.

It was also in that year that Ben first stuck his fingers in the oil game. Ever since his arrival in Denison he and T.V. (Vol) had found a kind of recreation in making geological surveys of all the territory in and around Denison, and their belief that the land in this section might yield oil is evidenced by the fact that, on 

June 21st, 1909, they organized the Denison Petroleum Company, and had the Texas and Louisiana Drilling Co. drill a well on Julian Field's farm near Warner Junction. Between 320 and 

370 feet they got several gas showings, but at a depth of 812 feet they struck an artesian salt-water well. 

Ben's next venture in oil was on July 1st, 1914, when he secured leases and drilled a well at his own expense. This was abandoned in 1917, when a depth of 2200 feet had been reached. He drilled two additional wells in the years 1915, '16 and '17, but oil was not to come in on any of Ben's property until approximately two months after his death, when a small well was brought in in Jack County, and a short time later a gas well was brought in in Moore County.

[174] 

In 1910 Ben had reached his sixty-fourth year in an extremely busy, arduous and successful life. It was natural that he should think, not of retiring, but of taking things a bit easier in the matter of new investments and organizations that were such bonecrushers to put on their feet and keep going. Long before this year Ben had, out of what can be suspected as pure animal-business spirits, bought the controlling stock in the First National Bank of Denison. He did this, more than likely, simply because it afforded him an outlet for his phenomenal energy and business acumen. But he did not like the banking business, and after having carried his bank through a couple of major country-wide financial crises, he sold his controlling interest and retired as an active banker. 

However, Ben did keep his finger on the pulse of the banking world for he was a director and stockholder of The National 

Bank of Denison,* The Denison Bank & Trust Co., and The

Citizens' State Bank (afterward The Citizens' National Bank) of his city. When the Denison Bank & Trust Co. closed its doors, W.B., as director of the company, felt his responsibility so keenly that he was instrumental in working out the bank's affairs so that all the depositors were paid one hundred cents on the dollar. In 

June of 1928 The National Bank of Denison and The Citizens' 

National Bank effected a consolidation, principally because of W.B.'s willingness to venture his capital in bringing about the merger, which was largely engineered by his son, W. B. Munson, Jr. 

To retrace our steps a bit, the year 1910 was to call him back, however, into just the kind of thing he had been doing all his active life; although there is no doubt, had he had his choice, he * This bank was organized in October, 1890; and in April, 1894, it merged with the First National Bank of Denison and the name, the National Bank of Denison, was retained.

Twentieth Century Curtain Calls 

[175] would have chosen a circumstance less personally painful to him. 

By this is meant the death of his old partner, Jot Gunter, which took place in San Antonio in the above-mentioned year. 

Mr. Gunter left a large estate valued at well over $1,000,000, and an indebtedness against this estate of something like $600,000. 

Large as this debt was, it would not have daunted a man who had dealt, literally, in millions of acres of good Texas lands, but to Mrs. Gunter, the widow, it was a heavy responsibility. One of the largest items in Mr. Gunter's estate was a ranch situated in several counties of Southwest Texas, consisting roughly of 73,000 acres. 

Mrs. Gunter was successful of disposing of the cattle that had been run on this ranch, and nothing was more natural than that she should turn to Ben Munson to assist her in putting the real estate in better shape. Ben responded quickly and adequately. 

He organized the Nueces Land & Live Stock Company, and placed enough of the stock to buy the land. Ben acted as Mrs. Gunter's agent in this transaction. During the next few years he loaned considerable money with stock in this company as security, and the borrowers being unable to repay the money, Ben finally became the largest stockholder in the corporation. 

During many of the years that have been covered in the present section Ben had been operating in partnership with his brother, J. T. Munson, in the real estate business. The business of this partnership had been mostly what might be termed local, but it had been very successful. In 1915 this partnership business was incorporated under the name of the Munson Realty Company, the company taking over all of the former firm's assets, and each of the former partners contributed certain individual properties. 

The capitalization was fixed at $150,000. 

The last big business deal which might be called in the Munson tradition occurred a number of years after the formal incorporation of the Munson Realty Company. Ben made this deal

[176] 

over the protest of his son who, after the manner of sons in all places and climates, felt that his father was getting along just a bit to undertake such big ventures. But Ben still had his old hand in; once more he "showed 'em."

An oil field had been brought in in Eastland County and Ben, together with J. R. Handy and Bill Green, hurried down to invest in real estate. They got their real estate all right, but while they were at it Ben decided that the small light plant there might not make a bad investment. (He had organized the first light and power company* in Denison in 1886.) He bought the Eastland 

Light Plant, put in new machinery, added an ice plant, and incorporated for $150,000. With him in this deal he associated Pete 

Rogers, manager of the Texas Power & Light Co. in Denison, Bill 

Green, also with the T.P. & L., Pat Tobin, who owned the ice plant in Denison, Van Campen, W.B.'s bookkeeper, and his old friend J. B. McDougall. The idea was, of course, that Pete Rogers and Bill Green would handle the power end and Pat Tobin would manage the ice plant from Denison. Van Campen was to keep the books, and McDougall - well McDougall "got in" just because he and Ben were good friends, and it was customary for him to have a part in almost all of Ben's schemes. W.B. also tried to persuade Bill Green to open up a real estate office in Eastland. "Good Lord, W.B., I'd never be able to raise the capital to open up a real estate office in Eastland; nor any place else, for that matter." "Hell!" Ben exclaimed; "you wouldn't have to raise any capital, I'd finance it. All you'd have to do is run it." up "Run it?" shouted Bill; "how'd I run it? I don't know straight about the real estate business.' 99 "Why, Bill," Ben said as he shoved his hands in his pockets and flexed his shoulders, “all you'd have to do is to catch 'em as they 

* Denison Power & Light Company 

W. B. Munson, President.

Twentieth Century Curtain Calls 

[177] come in; just throw the good ones under the desk, and toss the others over the top." "No dice, W.B.; I'm not going in the real estate business." 

Bill Green did not end up in Eastland selling real estate, but the light and ice plant that he got in as a result of the Eastland trip was later sold to the Texas Electric Service Co. for considerably more than the original investment. 

This deal was the last brilliant glare flung off from that long career of success that had hung for a half-century over the skies of North Texas. From now on our man is a private man, and it is as such that he will be treated in the next, and final, section.

PART THREE

MOST CHAPTER 22 Ben's Fishing and Hunting Trips 

OST READERS, after they have, through the pages of a book, become acquainted with that side of a man that almost anyone can know, are eager for intimate glimpses. The cynical explanation, that fault-ridden humanity is pleased to discover faults in others, is not applicable in many cases. The explanation of this desire for details is probably merely the effort of the reader to identify himself with the main character. Did our great man have a dog whom he called "Butch"? Did he prefer black socks or white?

These are the kinds of details that are likely to interest the average reader.

Although, during most of his business career, Ben Munson had enough irons in the fire to keep him as busy as a bee in a tarbucket, his days were far indeed from being all work. It came to be an established rule in his office that whenever W.B.'s midday absence was too protracted, and demands for his person became bothersome to his staff, the least busy employee was dispatched to canvass the offices of Dr. Fields, Judge Muse, and Dr. Ellis. He was always to be found in one or the other of them, behind a chessboard or a whist hand. These sessions, especially of the former game, merely kept him in trim for his Sunday bout of chess with Vol, or served to clarify a move he would be turning over in his head before mailing it in, because he always played with the experts by mail. "Dammit, Judge, if you don't intend to move that pawn, don't touch it! That's the only way I lost my game in the East against

[182] 

that dummy, not moving the wrong piece, but by merely touching the confounded thing."

While Ben was in town, home was synonymous with relaxation, and relaxation meant a game of billiards. On the top floor of both the old brick house and the new home on Morton he had a fine pool table. Or else there was an evening party with Ella, card games with the children, or a game of dominoes with anyone he thought could stand up to him.

He was often not above giving his office the slip, and showing up at Bill Green's desk at the Texas Power & Light Co. With his hands shoved deep in his pockets, while he scratched his back against the nearest post or projection, he would announce: "Good day for fishin', Bill."

This was Ben's favorite approach when he wanted to entice Green away from his work. Ben's persistent and boyish enthusiasm, together with the natural proclivities of the other man, usually made the proposition an irresistible one. On one particular day he came into Green's office, peeking over the tops of his glasses and, with his customary display of winning blandishments, proposed a fishing trip. “Mr. W.B., my car's broken down, Bill replied, “But where were you aimin' to fish?" "Let's go to Randall Lake?" "Well, Mr. W.B., I don't know how we're going to get there, and besides we'd have to catch some minnows beforehand." "We can catch the minnows all right, and we can use the office Ford." "I don't know a thing about driving a Ford, Mr. W.B.; I've never even had my hand on a Ford steering-wheel." "Well," Ben replied with mock impatience, "you could learn, couldn't you?" "Yes, I suppose so."

Ben's Fishing and Hunting Trips 

[183] “Then I'll make Clyde come down and teach you.”

Shortly after this challenging conversation, Clyde Stewart did show up with the Ford, and drove Green once or twice around the block, explaining something of the Ford mechanism which, at that time, differed radically from that of other standard cars on the market. Soon after this rudimentary lesson Ben showed up, and the two men set out somewhat uncertainly for the Munson home, where they picked up two milk cans in which they expected to put the minnows. They finally reached Mineral Creek, a wellknown haunt of silversides and red horse. "I guess we'd better carry the cans down to the creek," Green suggested. "Hell, no;" Ben countered; "we'd just have to carry 'em back up the slope, and full of water, too. Just drive on down into the creek, Bill." "Mr. W.B. if we drive this car into the creek we'll never get her out; this bank's steep.' "Drive her down," Ben commanded; "a Ford can go anywhere, and get out of anything."

It was with obvious reluctance that Green gave in, and the fact that they made a fine haul of minnows did not do much toward relieving his anxiety. The bank was between twelve and twenty feet high and sharply washed. After they had loaded the two filled cans into the car, Green made a run for it, but the Ford stuck and rolled back into the water. After two or three such unsuccessful efforts, Ben offered his advice. "Back her up, Bill, as far as you can, and I'll push; but when you get started, go like hell beating tanbark."

Green gave the Ford the gun, plowed through the water and, with the stout help of Ben pushing from the rear, hit the incline with a bang. The result should not have been wholly unexpected. 

With their full quota of wriggling minnows, both milk cans

[184] 

turned over and spilled their mixed and precious contents on the assistant's head. During the brief period of confusion Ben kept shouting to Green to keep on up the bank, and they finally succeeded in making the top. They were able to retrieve enough minnows to warrant their going on to Randall Lake, and this they did, with Ben sitting beside the driver, wet as a rat, but mightily pleased that they were to be able to fish at last, and that he had been proven correct in asserting that a Ford could go anywhere. 

On another occasion Ben and Bill went out to the Blackford farm where they expected to catch some minnows from a tank. 

They were driving the same Ford, which Green had, by now, learned quite well how to operate, but Ben had never really bothered about getting the hang of it.

They came to a wire gap and Green pulled up the brake handle, which also set the gears in neutral. Green got out, made for the gate, and as he was preparing to open it turned back to see Ben in the driver's seat. "How do you start this damn thing?" W.B. called out; “I'll drive it through the gate." "Put your foot on the left pedal, release the hand brake, and push on the pedal slowly until the car starts." 

Ben followed directions with sufficient closeness for the car to get under way, and it and the proud driver sailed jauntily through the But his security and pride were ill-founded. gap.

When Ben attempted to stop the car, he let the pedal back all the way which, as old-timers will remember, put the car in high gear. The car started determinedly down the pasture road, gathering speed, with Bill Green in hot pursuit. "Come on, Bill," Ben shouted, waving his arm to motion his friend on to greater effort.

Although the race was somewhat unequal as to horsepower, Green finally succeeded in overtaking the car after a sweating

Ben's Fishing and Hunting Trips 

[185] sprint of about half a mile, and, hopping up on the running-board, reached in and removed the ignition key, which ultimately brought the Ford to an ardently wished-for halt. 

Ben's penchant for teasing was well illustrated once when he and Bill Green were fishing at Waterloo.* 

Bill had hooked a big water moccasin on a very limber pole. 

Every fisherman knows that this is a good deal like holding a lion by the tail: you can't let go, and you have to keep moving. The old cottonmouth, in between his frantic efforts to escape the hook, was making rushes at Green, and each time these rushes brought him perilously close to Bill's legs. Bill could not drop his pole to pick up a stick, because this would have given the moccasin the opportunity to strike. In his extremity, Bill called to his fishingpartner.

Ben came up with a maddening grin on his face, got a stick, and killed the moccasin. "You don't have as much sense as a jay-bird, Bill," he said; "trying to catch a snake on a pole as limber as that one. But I'll tell you what, I'm awfully glad we finally landed him because snakes are delicious eating."

Bill, still somewhat flustered from his herpetological contest, watched with misgiving as Ben built a fire and threw into the pan some slices of bacon, in the grease of which he evidently intended to cook the snake.

It was only after repeated protestations that Bill was able to convince Ben that he just did not care particularly for snake-meat.

But there is no doubt but that Ben would have gone through with his part of the hoax if he could have counted on the gullibility of his intended victim.

Most of Ben's serious fishing was done in Colorado and other 

Rocky Mountain streams, but in the North Texas of the time * A city lake south of Denison.

[186] 

when he was most active as a sportsman the creeks and small lakes were not fished out as they are today. He was an indefatigable fisherman. He used any kind of fly, and showed his resourcefulness at old-fashioned hook-and-line fishing by using any attractive bait that presented itself. Although he did not scorn the then-new flies, he much preferred a cork and a baited hook. He was extremely skillful at playing a hooked fish, and his favorite quarry was the big-mouthed black bass.

Ben's enthusiasm and ability to make instant decisions were manifest in his attitude towards fishing, for he once went off on a long fishing trip to Colorado with Bill Green and his wife Viala* on one day's notice. They caught the train to Denver, and in that city they rented a Ford (his favorite car was the Marmon, and he loved high speed) and went up to the Encampment River in Wyoming. On this particular trip Ben hung up his best fishing record: four trout at one cast. Ben, unlike many sportsmen, was fond of eating his own game; and, this being true, he did not like to throw back the little ones if the big ones were not biting. Although the type of fishing he did in Colorado and the mountains called for a good deal of stream-wading, he did not use hip-boots, but preferred to splash right in wearing tennis shoes and an old pair of pants; and he did not remove these wet pants when he came out to sit around the camp, or to move on to another and more likely spot.

Ben's outdoor sportsmanship was not confined to fishing: he loved to hunt. He knew a great deal about wild life, and was a very close observer of nature. Although this latter is true, he does seem to have been a bit skeptical about the poisonous properties of certain plants - witness the story of his chewing up some leaves * Viala Green was a daughter of T. V. Munson and was named for M. Pierre Viala, the French envoy who conferred the Legion of Honor on T.V. M. Viala was a guest in Vol's home at the time the little girl was born.

Ben's Fishing and Hunting Trips 

[187] of poison-ivy, out of sheer hardheadedness, and to prove his contention that he could eat anything. He had been forced into this humorously indefensible assertion after having insisted that the owl was a good table-bird. It is hardly necessary to add that the poison ivy gave him a bad attack of blisters. No one knows whether he ever really tried to eat an owl. 

Ben never owned but one bird dog, a registered Irish setter named Turk, that was a present from John Scullin. W.B. was extremely fond of Turk, who lived to a ripe old age only to meet a foul death at the hands - the family always thought - of an old woman in whose back yard one of the Munson turkeys had been discovered and identified as having been stolen. Turk was scalded in this back yard, and died of his burns. 

Ben often went on hunting trips lasting one and two weeks. He hunted a great deal in the Panhandle section of Texas, and in the region around Coalgate, Oklahoma. He was a hard hunter. Even after he had passed his seventieth birthday he could walk down many a younger man, and that in spite of the fact that he was always what his friends thought of as a little plump. All his activities show that same astonishing quality of tireless, indomitable energy. 

Once Ben and a small party were on a hunting trip at Caddo, Oklahoma. There were lots of birds, and Mr. Handy's dog, old 

Spring, was doing wonderful work. Spring was pointing the birds; the hunters were kicking up the birds which were then scattering in the tall grass. Ben began to shoot badly. After he had missed. four birds in a row, he turned to Bill Green. "Bill, why in the hell don't you shoot these birds?” "Mr. W.B., I wanted you to have some shooting." "Wanted me to have some shooting!" Ben exploded; "why, hell, I couldn't hit a flock of barns. You shoot and let's get some meat.'

On a real hunt Ben was the best of companions. He loved to 

---

[188] 

listen to, and tell, long stories of the trails over the campfires. Although the party generally carried along a cook, Ben was himself considered an excellent camp cook, and was always ready to lend a hand to the many little tasks calculated to make himself and the others more comfortable. Like many out-of-doors men, he thought the coffee he made was better than anybody's. He was the first one to roll out of his blankets on such expeditions, and never drank but one cup of even his own superlatively brewed coffee. He drank, moreover, nothing stronger than this. Four of his favorite expressions were: "shoot, Luke, or give up the gun”; “an educated damned fool"; "not as much sense as a last year's birdnest"; and "you damned turkey." 

Ben was a fair shot. He owned a double-barrelled shot gun, 12gauge; his brother, J.T., used a 10-gauge double-barrelled Claborough. They were conventional in their choice of shot: number 

8s for birds, 4s for ducks. On his deer-shoots he used a 30-30 Remington automatic. Most of Ben's deer-shooting was done in southwest Texas, in the vicinity of Lasalle County; and although he shot birds extensively in the Coalgate section of Oklahoma, bobwhite were, at that time, scattered over most of north-central Texas.

His longest and most carefully planned duck-shoot took place near Matagorda, Texas. The men established their blinds on an island which abounded in quail, but was so thick with rattlesnakes that even the natives did not hunt there.

CHAPTER 23 A Penchant for Versification 

HERE IS one striking talent and characteristic of Ben Munson Tthat has been no more than mentioned so far; his penchant for versification. He had a good ear for metre and rhyme, and occasional verses came to his mind with great ease. He was, at one time and another, in correspondence with several newspaper editors over the country, and these men often published verse that he sent them. One of these was S. A. Jones, who edited a paper in 

Mississippi. Ben sent this editor - and the latter published it — an extensive poem called All Matter Alive. This composition runs to forty stanzas. It rhymes, and is simple in scheme, and contains 

Ben's ideas of what we speak of as life, as life is placed in what we know as the universe. The didacticism detracts a bit from its lyric qualities, and Ben was doubtless as well aware of this objection as anyone else. But it is full of ideas that are expressed in a form that would be likely to impress. It is undoubtedly true, for instance, that his own children and grandchildren could have learned some fundamental truths by reading this poem. A stanza can be chosen at random which will illustrate this work's special quality: 

In soft summer nights as we gaze at the moon 

We hear not her voice as she speaks to the waves;

Each atom of water while marching in tune 

Obeys the fair queen and politely behaves. 

This is a way 

that is certainly not without a solid charm - of instructing a reader in the influence of the moon on the tides, and

TWO CHRISTMAS VERSES WRITTEN TO MARY ELLA

A Penchant for Versification 

[191] the adverb in the last line will please a young mind, or a mind that will not grow old.

Ben was a great man to observe birthdays and anniversaries. 

He never forgot his children's birthdays, and he always appropriately remembered the anniversary of his wedding to Mary Ella.

It was his invariable and endearing custom to mark these days, and also Christmas, with presents, and the presents were accompanied by verses. Many of these presentation verses are extant, but of course they are too numerous to do more with than to quote one or two to show what they were like. It should be said in passing that the gifts were always considerable, never gewgaws; and if the recipient was ungracious enough to question the lyric fire and inspiration of the poetry, such a complaint would be smothered beneath solid materials. 

My charming daughter Eloise 

It is a pleasure you to please.

I hope this scrap of cotton stock 

Will help your wants to fully block, And serve to keep your mind at ease.

P.S. You have been fined one thousand dollars for failing to keep the command "Multiply and replenish the earth." 

Here are three more:

TO ELOISE 

Old Christmas with its joy is here 

To fan the flames of pleasures sweet,

So now accept this bond for cheer 

And know it comes as Daddie's treat. 

May life for you be full of love, And choicest blessings ever flow 

Along your path from shores above, And make your life a constant glow.

[192] 

TO MARY JEFFREY TONKIN 

To Mary dear, Bright as a daisy and as sweet as a pink, With a heart full of love and a brain to think. 

This paper expresses your grandpa's love. 

May you long be as happy as the cooing dove. 

To lovely Mary, grandchild dear, Enclosed you'll find a Christmas cheer. 

And may your life be pure and bright, And sparkling as the rays of light. 

It is but natural that the verses that Ben wrote to his wife whom he fully appreciated for all her rare qualities of mind and spirit — should have an extra depth of feeling. Here follow several that were written to Mary Ella, sometimes out of what we speak of as a clear sky, sometimes to celebrate particular days or events: 

With time's eternal, rapid flight, Without a halt by day or night, We come again to Christmas Cheer, The brightest season of the year.

Through all the years since we were wed Many happy hours and years have sped, And through these years all else above 

Has ruled the charming thought of love.

Accept this tribute from my heart, As testimonial on my part, Of all the joys that life can give, And may they last while yet we live. "November roses" bright have just renewed the past 

And all the charming hours of long ago recast

The social pleasures and intellectual charms Like resurrected flowers fill memory's happy arms. 

The brilliancy of thought and purity of heart 

That held your early friends adorns poetic art.

A Penchant for Versification 

[193] 

And makes "November roses" a treasure unalloyed 

To be forever kept and thankfully enjoyed.

As biting frosts of age reduce our mental powers We more and more enjoy fond memory's happy flowers The brightest of them all with colors soft and fragrance sweet 

As by "November roses" brought from their retreat.

We thank you most sincerely for this surpassing pleasure 

And wish you future blessings' overflowing measure. 

To one whose faithful love and character bright 

Has filled my life with unqualified delight, This little package testifies your priceless worth —

The loveliest wife on the face of this whole earth.

Dearest Mama and Wife: 

In hopes this chair will ease your feet 

And cause your pace to be less fleet 

We ask you now to take a rest 

For all your family you have blest.
