CHAPTER 24 A Little Humor Is a Valuable Thing 

HERE IS another element in Ben's nature that makes practically Tno correspondence kendincticalit no appearance in his business correspondence - and indeed it would have been difficult for this to have been so. This is the fun that he had in him. As has already been shown in this section, he was something of a practical joker, but he was also possessed of a keen sense of humor; and it is not often that these two are to be found in the same person. There are several anecdotes which should find their place here, and which illustrate both the propensities mentioned above.

For one thing he was always trying to rib his friends into doing something absurd or foolish. Once he shaved off his own mustache and beard, went down town and confronted his friends, none of whom recognized him. "You know where Munson's office is?" he would inquire of each one of them as he met them.

One of the earliest of the real anecdotes is concerned with a shopping trip that Ben once made when he was in New York.

The thing about this particular shopping trip was that he was not out to make purchases for himself, but for his wife; and what complicates it still further is that the object of his invasion of the stores was not just an ordinary article: he had determined to make his wife a present of a new fur coat. 

Man that he was, with railroad tracks running through his mind and the sound of pistons beating in his ears, it had not occurred to him that he would have to know the size that would fit Mary

A Little Humor Is a Valuable Thing 

[195] Ella. But, since he had great tenacity of purpose, he was not to be balked by any such trivial detail. "I want to see a fur coat, Madam," he told the saleswoman. "Yes indeed; for a lady, Sir?" "I should say, and a very fine lady at that." "Then you will want something rather good, I take it." "That's exactly right; something good and stylish. But I'll have to depend on you for the quality and the style." "I think we have just the thing that you are looking for. And now, Sir, what size does the lady wear?" "I don't know," Ben grinned slyly. "You don't know! Then how, Sir, can you expect. "Oh but I do expect a fit," Ben interrupted her pleasantly; “and there is always a good way - "But I don't think I understand," the woman said, puzzled. "Madam, if you'll be good enough to just trot out four or five models of different sizes, and let each one of them try on the coat that you have in mind for me, I think I can do the deciding." 

The woman, nonplussed but sensing a good customer, went away, soon to return with several models. 

Ben stood back, eyeing each one critically and in turn. Finally he moved into the group and began to hug each of the models. 

At last he hit on one that "felt" just right. "The size that will fit this girl," he announced positively, pointing to the mildly embarrassed manikin, “will fit my wife."

In a small riot of good humor, induced by his most original method, he made his purchase and bore it proudly away. The coat was exactly right for Mary Ella, though she says it was not real mink.

A man of such resources would be able, without the benefit of Boy Scout training, to make a spark and start a fire from a piece of flint and dry wood, and would know exactly why God made

[ ] 

the traveler's palm, the first time he clapped eyes upon such a tree.

Once, when Ben and his wife and three children were in Colorado, at Wagon Wheel Gap, they made the acquaintance of a gentleman who had the reputation of being almost morose and forbidding, so unsociable was he. He found children especially distasteful to his crusty bachelorhood, and looked with jaundiced eye at the three young Munsons who were doubtless no worse nor any better behaved than the young children of any careful and superior parents. 

No passive, ordinary approach would bring this gentleman (a 

Mr. Dunn, of St. Louis) around. Mary Ella - she admits — had to initiate something in the nature of a campaign to win him over. She finally succeeded enough to satisfy herself, and to get him to look with a kinder eye upon the children; but she managed to arouse in the bachelor a degree of courtesy and consideration that Ben, at least, found annoying and disturbing - in the literal sense.

The day came for the St. Louisan to depart for home. He was to leave Wagon Wheel Gap at four o'clock in the morning, and so he made his adieux the evening before. He was particularly tender at parting from the Munson children. 

Ben and Mary Ella retired to their cottage and went to sleep.

Their rest was broken along about three-thirty by a discreet but persistent rapping on the door. Mary Ella, in a hastily donned kimono, went to see who it was, and there, in the pre-dawn blackness, stood the old bachelor.

He had wanted just to speak to her once more; that was all. 

Any woman would be flattered by such attention. Ben made a grumbling facetious comment, and one in high good humor. "I don't care how much Mr. Dunn loves you," he complained; "I don't want him waking me up at three-thirty in the morning."

There was not a trace of real annoyance in this remark. He was

A Little Humor Is a Valuable Thing 

[197] ut ed -k ea Or ed; was jealous all right, but jealous only of the time she spent at something else when he was around, and when she, otherwise, could be entertaining him. He loved to be entertained, in fact. He loved to go to card parties, or any kind of party at all, and was adept at all kinds of games, even billiards and pool. 

But as for his feeling any irritation with his wife that Mr. Dunn had waked him out of a sound sleep, such a thing was unthinkable. 

How could he have been annoyed, even for an instant, with this remarkable woman whom he consulted about all his business deals; who understood so well Ben's hatred of mosquitoes that she would put her bare foot from under the bar on the far side of the bed in order to entice the pests from the side where he slept. 

Moreover, he could remember at any time of the day or night that, when it had been necessary, Mary Ella had taken off her shoes and stockings and had helped him seine for minnows in the icy waters of the mountain streams. And anyone who has ever, barefooted and on boulders, held down the "lead-line," knows what a sacrifice and a service seining can be under such circumstances.

In Ben's love of a good joke, he spared no one; not even his adored wife.

Once, when he and Mary Ella were going down to Dallas on an interurban parlor car, a gentleman boarded the car at Sherman whom Mary Ella did not recognize. This took place at the time 

Jim Ferguson had so violently antagonized the University of Texas and its partisans. Ben was reading one section of the Dallas 

News and his wife another. Mary Ella, in the midst of reading about the Ferguson affair, suddenly burst out against the Governor and, with her not-unexpected vociferous frankness, gave the Governor a verbal hiding. Ben kept his face hidden behind his paper. When the third occupant of the car got off somewhere down the line, Mary Ella inquired:

[198] 

"Ben, who was that gentleman?" "That was Jim Ferguson's brother," was the cool, crinkly-eyed answer. 

Mary Ella exploded. "Ben, how in the world could you treat me that way! You could at least have nudged me, or tried to give me some little sign!" "Oh,” he rejoined with lazy amusement, "I just knew that if 

I gave you enough rope you would hang yourself." "Ben Munson, I'll never forgive you for this as long as you live!" But he knew that her anger was trumped up, and that the vexation would pass. 

Ben had early been an admirer of Jim Ferguson, and had admired the latter's common sense. But later, when Ben's children had been to the University, he felt that the Governor had gone too far.

CHAPTER 25  

EN'S FAVORITE novelist was Dickens; and he was a good enough scholar in Shakespeare and Bacon to take a great interest in that old controversy, and to have a definite leaning toward giving Bacon more credit than was commonly accorded him. Ben was an omnivorous reader, as will be revealed by a glance at a list of some of the titles in his personal library (Appendix I). From his wide reading, and his even wider experience, he had definite and clear-cut ideas and theories about everything of importance that engaged the interest of the public mind during the times of his activity. 

As to financial panics, he had a theory that the bad ones came along about every thirty years; and that these panics were largely due to wild speculation in the big money centers, and to improvidence in governmental administration. He was a great admirer of William Jennings Bryan, and this gives us a good clue - which has already been mentioned in the second section — as to some of 

Ben's ideas on the monetary question. 

Ben, born and reared in the northern Middle West, considered himself and his friends so considered him to be without race prejudice. He must have been greatly helped in this attitude by the long and satisfactory experience he had with his beloved negro manservant, Shepherd M. McColums. "Shep" came to work for

Ben and Mary Ella on March 20th, 1920; and remains today, more

[200] 

than twenty-six years later, one of the mainstays of the family, and an integral part of the Munson household. In Ben's later years, Shep was his constant hunting and fishing companion; and, at the end, it was the faithful, gentle Shepherd who prepared his master with great and understanding tenderness for his entry into the happy hunting ground.

Ben had a very high opinion of Thomas A. Edison, and once, when he was in New York on business, he made a special trip over to New Jersey to see the great inventor. Ben ventured brashly into the laboratory, and so astonished the wizard that the latter gave him the opportunity to speak out for himself. When he had finished, Edison told him he was the most interesting man he had talked with in a long while, and backed up this high praise by taking Ben on a tour of the plant, explaining the entire operations in detail. He then asked Ben to lunch, an invitation which was eagerly accepted.

Ben was a Jeffersonian Democrat, and in each of the campaigns where Bryan was a candidate he confidently expected the Great Commoner to win. Ben's admiration for Woodrow Wilson was more restrained, and in this he was at loggerheads with his brother, J.T., who fairly worshiped the Princetonian. Ben's personal opinion was that Wilson had been considerably to blame for the entrance of the United States into the World War. Although 

W.B. was, in his mature life, bitterly opposed to American Imperialism, he heartily approved of the construction of the Panama Canal, and followed the stages of its building with keen interest. 

He was especially interested in the many troubles that beset the engineers, for this involved a knowledge of the geological formations of the Canal Zone, and of course Ben never lost his affection for this, his first love, geology.

When the Maine blew up in the harbor of Havana, he believed firmly that it had been the work of the Spaniards, and it was his

[201] conviction that, after this, there was nothing left for us to do but to go in and clean up the situation. He was tremendously excited over the news of Admiral Dewey and the battle of Manila Bay, and read with avidity every dispatch from headquarters. He was particularly proud of the way the English admiral conducted himself shortly before that battle was fought, and bragged to his friends that the English had shown their true colors and come through as he had known they would. This fact confirms the general impression that he had great admiration for the English as a race, and his love and respect for their literature will be manifest from a glance at the number of British titles in the library list to be seen in the appendix. 

Before ever the Maine was sunk Ben felt very strongly that something should be done to help the Cubans escape from the tyranny of Spain; and we know, from having already enjoyed a speech he wrote on the subject as a boy, that he had always had ideas about American responsibility toward Cuba. He did not, however, approve of the annexation of the Philippines. This came, probably, as a result of the strong feeling that he had about the rights of minorities. All during the prosecution of the World 

War he talked frequently and with wide information of these submerged minorities, and followed with constant concern the progress of the treaties that dealt with these questions. His loyal 

American heart and head were never troubled by doubts as to Germany's instigation of the war: he thought her completely guilty.

Although there is nothing in his private library to bear this out, there is other evidence that the French Revolution was one of his favorite historical subjects. He applauded the principles of this Revolution but, of course, deplored the excesses that took place before the regime was absorbed and curbed by Napoleon. 

He was too far removed from - and at the same time too close to

[202] 

- the Russian Revolution to appreciate exactly what was happening in that country; but he read with eagerness all that came to hand on the subject, and did think that the fate of the Czar and his family was cruel and unjustifiable. This ghastly event permitted him to think that no good could come of a movement that had started out under such bloody auspices. Further, as witness to his tolerance and real democracy, he took no part in the Klan activities, and refused categorically to participate in the anti-Catholic feeling that the Klan sought to foment. Ben did, however, believe ,firmly in the separation of Church and State, and disapproved of any religion's meddling in politics. His words on this subject were: "I don't think religion and politics can sleep in the same bed"; and it is not hard to guess what he would have thought of 

Taylor's having been sent to the Vatican. 

Ben's own personal interest in politics extended no further than that of any good citizen. He never sought an office for himself. But he always voted, and considered voting the sacred duty of every citizen. He was a devoted husband and father, and always had too hot irons in the fires of commerce, to lend himself to the life of a politician. many

Ben constantly told his wife, and others as well, that he considered her the most remarkable woman in the world. He said quite frankly that he attributed to her a large part of his financial success. He knew the value of economic stability. He frequently gave his children serious talks on the subject of business, but never attempted to interfere in what he considered their personal lives, and was specially hands-off in matters of faith and religion. 

J. R. Handy is the authority for the statement that Ben did not know what personal fear was, and that he was a man of vast tenderness. On his early, dangerous trips into the wilds of West

Texas he always went armed, but never carried a pistol in Denison, although that town was for many years anything but a Sunday

[203] School picnic. Ben had a personal acquaintance with the famous and notorious Sam Bass in the free and easy days of the nearfrontier, and often expressed considerable sympathy for the James brothers in the belief that they had been so hounded and persecuted by the soldiery that a life of outlawry was practically forced upon them. He was sympathetic with, and also knew, Bob and 

Cole Younger up in the country where they operated. He felt that they, too, had been driven to their life. He was open in his condemnation of lynch law.

Ben was tenacious in argument and occasionally lost his temper at such times. Witness the following story: Ben and J. T. Munson got into a very heated discussion in the partnership office in about the year 1897. It was the kind of dispute that only brothers or close friends would engage in, for the subject of it was the length of the Mississippi River. Mr. J. T. had the ill luck to shake his fist in Ben's face, and Ben knocked his brother down. By this time he was fighting mad. Mr. Handy and Lee French, at that time a constable (this gives the affair a serio-comic turn for the “law” intervened purely as a private citizen), had to pull Ben off Mr. 

J. T. The two brothers quickly calmed down, regained their tempers and never, from that day forward, did either one refer to the fight.

As to the point of the argument, Ben was right and Mr. J. T. was wrong.

By the time the family moved from the old brick house into the new home the grandchildren were already multiple, and this second dwelling was amply spacious to gather them all into her hospitable arms at one time if necessary; and she frequently did, particularly at Christmas. 

Everyone would be there. Maud and her husband from California; Theda and Roy with the five “Kinney" boys from Little 

Rock; and Linley with her three children from McAlester. The

[204] 

sleeping-porch would be lined with cots for the children, and the atmosphere was one of tense expectancy and ill-subdued excitement. Christmas for them all was Christmas Eve, and everything about it was beautiful. Everyone dressed for the occasion. 

Mamas and aunts had their hair piled high and caught with glittering pins; their gowns were often décolleté and so very fragrant. 

The men, too, looked the parts they were to play; scrubbed and shined and brushed, immeasurably pleased with their role in this story-book world, playing a one-night stand of MAKE BELIEVE but what a night!

It was very fitting that the celebration should be "heavenward," right in the topmost part of the big house - the attic. The 

Christmas tree was always enormous, inevitably touching the high ceiling. Surely it could not have been decorated by anyone but old St. Nick himself. One glance at its shimmering beauty, one whiff of its pungent aroma, was enough to cause a heady, exultant intoxication. Ben himself, "granddaddy," looked not unlike Santa 

Claus; a very spruced-up Santa who had snitched just a whiff of 

Mary Ella's toilet water- short, stocky, his grey hair brushed until it glistened like Christmas snow, his round face flushed with pleasure, chuckling at the rapt countenances and delighted squeals of the children - his Christmas, his house, his family, all built through his sweat, toil and tears — and he loved every bit of it. 

In his hands he clutched a stack of white envelopes with Christmas seals on them. Suddenly he would clear his throat — a signal for silence — Granddaddy was going to present his gifts. Mary 

Ella came first, and even the littlest tot sensed that Grandmother's envelope was the most special; then came the children, and lastly the grandchildren. Every envelope with its crisp bond or stock certificate was accompanied by a verse that he had composed, and that he would read aloud upon presentation.

[205] The house was never without children, of course, for it presented abundant opportunities for imaginative, tireless youngsters to explore. There was the hayloft that could be turned into a thrilling slide, or a made-to-order "set" for cops and robbers.

There were trees to climb, the attic to romp in on rainy days, and today the balustrade, curving from the attic all the way to the ground floor, trembles with the memory of so many young hoodlums scooting down its polished railing with shouts of "watch out!" "look at me!" "toot-toot, here I come."

But the high spot of each grandchild's existence came when he was lucky enough to be on hand for “parade day." The very anticipation of it was something infinitely precious, and as the children stood on the curb in the rays of a relentless summer sun they were torn between an anxiety to hear the first roll of drums and the painful realization that it would be over all too soon for their young desires. Of course Granddaddy was there, in his white linen suit and battered straw hat, nodding to all his cronies and looking very much as though this, too, were his show. 

Suddenly a hush would fall; the crowd would lean as one man farther out across the curb, all eyes on the negro band that, with measured tread, was leading the parade ever closer. "Here he comes!"

Granddaddy would grin, and this utterance of his would be taken up with slight variations by Mary Ella and all the grandchildren. "Look! Can you see?" "There he is. Here comes Big Boy!"

And feeling very much as though they were going to vomit or burst into tears - something they would never have done in public — the children would fasten their worshipful eyes on Shep. 

Shep way out in front, his six feet further heightened by an enormous, black fur shako, his long legs encased in glistening black 

---

TEXAS SHEPHERD M. MCCOLUMS ("BIG BOY") Lithograph by Linley Munson Tonkin

[207] patent-leather boots, the sun playing upon all the gold braid across his chest and setting his drum major's stick ablaze. Shep, a symphony of black and gold! Here he comes, prancing, almost dancing on the very tips of his toes, one hand twirling, tossing his stick with effortless grace, the other resting lightly on his hip. He is coming abreast of his "folks" now, his eyes searching them out, and with one sidelong glance of recognition he pivots and strikes up the band! His band — the band that carried off prizes in Kansas City, Denver, Oklahoma City, and San Antonio. What a performance! What a thrill! 

But the truly great event that Ben, Mary Ella, the children and grandchildren all celebrated was on the Friday evening of September 3rd, 1926. Ben's and Ella's Golden Wedding. Many times during those fifty years Ben had good-naturedly chided his wife about her love for entertaining, but never before had such a golden opportunity presented itself to challenge Mary Ella's skill as a hostess. Ben was now physically and mentally weary, and this time he did not chide his wife's elaborate plans he shouted "damned nonsense!" and balked with all the tenacity of a "Rocky 

Mountain Canary." However, the evening of September 3rd found that the east lawn of the Munson home had been artistically adapted to stage an outdoor wedding, with the pergola and its background of golden-yellow flowers making an effective frame for the improvised altar. There were chairs to accommodate approximately three hundred people, and as the guests started arriving they were seated by six young ushers, all identically dressed in white linen suits with yellow boutonnières: they were the bride's and groom's grandsons the ones, that is, who were old enough to be eligible. Actually, there should have been seven, but a very observing guest would have noted that young Curtis McKinney, who would have been the seventh groomsman, was watching the 

---

[208] 

activities from a chaise placed on the deck of the porte cochère. 

The indignities his digestive track had been guilty of were real enough, but there was little doubt in the minds of those who knew and loved him that the illness had been induced by his contemplations of facing three hundred people, many of them complete strangers to him. As it was, he enjoyed the ceremony immensely from the height of his vantage point. The ushers most of whom were his brothers themselves provided plenty of interest. His red-haired cousin Allen, who was twenty-one and the oldest of the group, looked as though he were sharing his grandfather's opinion and verdict of “damned nonsense,” and he was just as obviously separating what he considered the sheep from the goats. Old Munson (eighteen and of an enviable age to his young brother Curtis) on the other hand had a certain gleam in his eye, and was working feverishly at trying to beat Allen to the draw when it came to offering an arm to a pretty girl. Because it was a party, Leighton was automatically having a beautiful time and dancing attendance on one and all as busily as a restaurateur on opening night. Dick was finding plenty to chuckle over, and several of the ladies looked perilously close to chucking him under the chin. "Wish they would," thought Curtis, "just to see him chuck them right back." Eleven-year-old Ben III was very handsome in his white linens, studiously trying to follow all the rules his mother had laid down for the conduct of a groomsman. That he felt his responsibilities was evident by the slight furrow between his eyes, but he was careful to smile at each guest who approached, and he tried to make pleasant small talk as he escorted them down the aisle. Where was John? Must be around somewhere, though probably not feeling too "groovy" after his recent bat with scarlet fever. Finally, Curtis spotted him, chatting animatedly with his dad; a small, nine-year-old boy whose red hair and freckles stood out in sharp contrast to his white linen suit.

[209] Sort of a flat-faced kid with a pug nose, but the kind of real American boy that is every illustrator's dream for a magazine cover, typical of the expression "one hundred per cent wool and a yard wide."

Looking up suddenly, John spied Curtis and moved closer to call: "Hey Curt, how are you, fella? Why aren't you down here?" 

Curtis shouted back: "You'll have to tell 'em Bradstreet, I'm

Dun." 

John and his dad both grinned and waved as they walked away into the crowd. The father and son were great friends, real men friends, with neither of them making much concession to the some thirty years' difference in age. "Gee," thought Curtis, "it was swell to have John calling up that way." John was everyone's friend and somehow a fella felt proud to know him.

The hum of conversation started to fade as Theda took her place near the orchestra. Her yellow gown was an echo of the flowers in the garden and the bowls of yellow roses that were in such profusion all over the house. As she sang Love's Old Sweet 

Song her voice stirred up golden memories in many hearts, echoes from wedding bells that had rung in the past. The orchestra slid into Lohengrin's Wedding March and the guests rose to their feet; all eyes turned toward the east entrance of the house where the bridal party was descending. The young groomsmen were very solemn at this point, and Mary Ella's namesake, twelve-year-old Mary Tonkin, looked just a trifle nervous and was doubtless grateful that her period costume of lace pantaloons and hooped skirt would hide her shaking knees. The exquisite lace at her wrists was from Mary Ella's original wedding gown. Next came the maid of honor, Jane Tonkin, in modern dress but of the same shade of gold; her eyes were bright with excitement and she walked in an aura that the lovely age of seventeen feels privileged to create for itself alone. Behind her was trailing chubby little Betty Mun-

[210] 

son escorted by her cousin, Ben Miller McKinney, both of them all of six and both carrying a golden basket of flower petals that they were tossing rather hit or miss in the path of the bride and groom. W.B., looking very scrubbed in his white linen suit, reminded one of a recalcitrant schoolboy, but he was not totally unaware, even after fifty years, of the charm and nobility of his bride. Mary Ella, in her gown of white satin with Ben's golden wedding gift — a platinum and diamond necklace and pendant around her throat, her arms filled with roses, and her light-brown hair (that had defied time by refusing to fade or turn grey) adorned by a bridal veil of shimmering gold net, was every inch a queen. No queen ever stood more erect than Mary Ella that evening, despite her seventy-four years; and no queen was more determined to have her wishes carried out than Mary Ella was to see that this event in her life and Ben's was the biggest party on the calendar. Everything must be exact, from the solid-gold favors in the wedding cake to the mementos that she and Ben presented their attendants. The bridesmaids and groomsmen each received the same gift — a square, silk moire box inscribed in gold with the dates 1876 - 1926 and containing a five-dollar gold piece. 

The golden ice cream must be in lady-slipper molds, and the cakes fashioned like miniature wedding cakes, each a confectioner's gem of orange blossoms and lilies-of-the-valley. 

As the bride and groom stood at the altar, Dr. J. S. Hodges of the Presbyterian Church started to read: 

It's more than fifty years ago 

Since Cupid drew his naughty bow 

And sent a shaft upon the breeze 

Aimed direct at hearts like these.

Unconsciously, Ben started to flex his shoulders and relax a little. 

Two victims fell upon the spot, As trophies to that deadly shot.

To celebrate this golden rite. gave his still-lovely bride a shy, self-conscious embrace. Now he could talk to Judge Head whom he had spotted in the audience on his way up that infernal aisle. 

Much later that night, as they were retiring, Mary Ella put her hand on Ben's arm "Fifty years is a long time, Ben. Not many couples are so fortunate in every way as we've been. You've made history in these fifty years while I've made memories; tonight was my last fling, it will make a bang-up memory for me and all the children. I'm proud of all of them and I'm proud of you. You've made me everything 

I am or ever will be."

Ben was eighty at the time of this anniversary and fast growing oblivious to the strident urgency of the world where he had striven so continuously and successfully. He was content now to sit on his porch watching the robins and mockingbirds on his own broad lawns. In 1929, even the death of one of his adored children, his daughter Maud, failed to cause more than a momentary ripple of pain in the deep anesthetized calm of his meditations on the past. As the shadows closed in about him the darkness found him clothed in deep and various satisfactions, garments that he wore with his usual and famous modesty. He had been an important man in an all-important section of an important state; he, together with his associates, had been the biggest landholder and trader in the whole of this part of the country; he had "swung" deals

[213] without number; he had worked tirelessly and unselfishly for the good of his city and his community, and had spent a large fortune in this endeavor. He had been just and kind, tolerant and tender, a perfect husband and a perfect father. In his own words he had been like an oak that had grown through all weathers under the sky to lift his head into that freer, upper air where all men are not privileged to climb. The oak, he said, looking affectionately at his children, had let fall its precious acorns.

Just a month after his eighty-fourth birthday this long and brilliant life came to an end, quietly at his home in Denison. 

On the 6th of February, 1930, the stout oak fell; fell in that last storm from which "none may hope to hide."

EPILOGUE

EPILOGUE BY 

Mary Tonkin Smith

T NUMEROUS times during his life Ben Munson expressed the belief that all men were reflected in the lives of their children and that it was through his posterity that a man continued to live, both physically and spiritually. Even generations later, by some strange twist of the chromosomes, a child will emerge with all the physical characteristics and even personal traits of his greatgreat-grandfather. Herein lies the necessity for a man to so conduct his life that if his descendants should chance to look over their shoulders into the years that belonged to their predecessor they would marvel at a rainbow of triumph and promise, not shiver in the murky atmosphere of a reputation that could rise out of the past like fog to confuse and impede any attempts at progress.

This thought is again apparent in the following excerpt taken from a letter that Ben wrote to his eldest child, Linley, on January 13, 1918. He was then seventy-two years old. 

Your birthday remembrance . . . reminds me that I have but a few more years at most to wrestle with the problems of life individually, but my thoughts, feelings and impulses, together with the living cells of my body have passed into my children and through them into my grandchildren so that I am enjoying myself in them and again living the happy days of childhood. Thus my life and happiness have expanded and promise to continue down the coming ages as they carry the impress of my life and its activities on and on.

I can, therefore, look upon the prospect of an early termination of my

[218] 

bodily activity and life with perfect indifference as it will live untouched and still growing, and ever expanding my wider field of life and pleasure. 

My chief concern now is over the surroundings and prospects that the future may bring to interfere with my continued happiness in and through my posterity. I trust that the condition of our country as a whole, and the conduct of my posterity as individual citizens, may combine to carry my happiness on through many generations and that no act of any of my descendants may cast a reflection on the family's fair name. 

It is the purpose and intent of this epilogue to show that Ben's five children did manage fairly well to attain his rather high expectations for them as private citizens and parents. In each case 

Ben's imprint is clearly discernible; it is as though he were mirrored from five different angles so that the physiognomy would not appear the same in any two reflections, or, if you prefer, as though five highly individualized adaptations had been made from one basic pattern. 

The first child, a girl, was born to Ben and Mary Ella, August 12, 1877. She was named Linley Allen for both of her grandmothers, Maria Linley and Sarah Allen. The name itself was a good start, for they were both estimable women and worthy of emulation. 

Linley was a quiet, thoughtful person whose solemnity was saved and even enhanced by her ability to find something amusing in nearly all things. Her thin line of a mouth was perpetually curved in an all-knowing, Mona Lisa sort of half-smile. It was typical of the child that, on one of the first visits which Charles Goodnight paid in the home of her parents, she pleaded that she was weary and wished to retire early, though all of her life she loved to sit up into the small hours of the morning. The request granted, she appeared a few moments later in a voluminous white nightgown and, half smiling, her eyes alight with mischievous gaiety, she made a sweeping, dramatic bow and very solemnly said: "Good night, Mr. Goodnight."

Epilogue 

[219] The little red-haired girl, always more woman than child, grew up with a passionate love for beauty; for beauty that was both tangible and intangible. She went away to boarding school, but 

Ben stubbornly refused to permit her to study Latin - something she never quite forgave him, for it meant that she was not equipped to take the college entrance examinations in that one subject, and she so wanted a college education! Her father's hardheadedness in this instance proved of real benefit because, having lost a few years of college, she felt the need to study and read extensively for the remainder of her life. Her favorite authors were Nietzsche, Blake, Whitman, and Emerson, and she herself gained a great facility with accurate words. Nearly all of her nights she spent reading and writing, for she carried on an extensive correspondence with persons both in this country and abroad who interested her, and whom she in turn interested. 

Linley loved to travel, and while her dread of the water kept her from braving a European trip, she did make extensive journeys in this country, and would go to a great deal of trouble and inconvenience to look up people whose accomplishments and reputation appealed to her. She was always cordially received and many of these "pop calls" led to strong and lasting friendships.*

But her real love was art, and in this she was encouraged by both parents who sent her to New York and the Art Students' League.

It was here that she met Fan Cory, a lighthearted, gay, and extremely capable young woman whose work had an elfishly illustrative quality that always managed to catch that "something" about babies and children. She later became known through her syndicated Sonny's Sayings, and she and Linley were lifelong friends, although the latter was rearing babies in Oklahoma at the time Fan was bringing hers up in Montana. * Here it is interesting to recall Ben visiting Thomas Edison in his laboratory and staying for lunch apparently father and daughter were much alike in this 

respect.

[220] 

That Linley pursued her talent at all is remarkable, for she paid close attention to the management of her household and the rearing of her children, and her art received little or no encouragement from this quarter. Undaunted, however, she built a small studio in a wooded lot next to her home, where she still could keep an eye on the activities of the three children and hear the "plaints" of the cook. Her work regularly appeared in exhibits in 

Oklahoma City, Kansas City, and various parts of the South. 

Strangely enough she rarely exhibited in Texas. Her summers were spent in one art colony or another, always with the children, and she eventually bought a home in Taos, New Mexico, and there spent the months of June, July, and August.

It was difficult for her to understand how anyone could be guilty of ennui or despair as long as there were other people about, people walking up and down the streets, crowding into trolleys or buses or filing out of the movie houses. People, thousands of them, all so much alike, yet no two identical, all with similar problems and foibles, all cloistering dreams and aspirations that would never see the light of day. Thousands of little Cristos, crucified on the Tree of Life, who would rise eternally triumphant in the realm of their dreams, if not actually in the achievement of their ambitions. When she could, Linley liked to give these aspirants a material boost, and found many kind, unassuming ways of helping with a girl's or boy's college education, furthering a young artist's career, or seeing that a friend got a fair chance at a new and better job. She was a sensible person with a well-defined code of ethics and decency, but she had little patience with prudery and squeamishness. Everyone from garbage collectors to fashionably dressed women found relief in talking with her, and, bearing this in mind, it is small wonder that in art she should lean toward portraiture, particularly character sketches. 

Her most cherished hobby was collecting etchings and prints

Epilogue 

[221] and she acquired some exceptionally fine ones. In Dallas, after a rather notable showing of her prints (and at which showing she lectured on the Graphic Arts), she telephoned her family in Denison to ask that they join her there for a concert that was to be given the next evening. Several times in the course of the conversation she reiterated: "I'm sure this has been the happiest day of my life."

In these words was a prophetic quality of comfort to her family. She died that evening, November 23, 1932, from a heart attack, leaving behind her own work, and admirable examples of the work of other artists to remind one of the inscription on her studio fireplace: "In Life beauty perishes, not in Art.' 99 Maud made her appearance in the Munson household on August 27, 1879. Ben, of course, named her, and though he never bothered to explain his choice in this instance, Mary Ella realized that here was a living admission of Ben's extreme love for horses. 

The thoroughbred, Maud S., was admired at that time by all who knew horses, and her every achievement would nuzzle Ben's heartstrings. The name pleased him and seemed appropriately auspicious for his second filly. Certainly the raven-haired, black-eyed child was as high-strung and intractable as a race horse. Her will was inflexible, and all of her life she required expert "handling," but, given her head at the right moments, she would run Life's course and take the hurdles with breath-catching speed and enviable ease.

Maud viewed mealtime as affording her an excellent opportunity to place her demands and expectations before all of the family, and likewise to air any grievance, fancied or real. This *Leonardo da Vinci.

[222] 

was never done in an effort at sensible arbitration, but rather in the light of an imperious declaration and fair warning of the general direction of the next violent explosion. When she was being more than usually difficult one day Ben himself exploded. "Maud, if you don't behave yourself I'll take the buggy-whip to you!" "Take it!" she snapped back.

Ben's hand had been called and he was not one to back down. 

Deliberately he picked up the whip and gave her a light, quick flick across the legs. The child's eyes were blazing, but they never wavered from her father's face and there was no visible quiver; no tears to indicate the extent of the hurt. It was then that Ben knew he could never whip his thoroughbred into submission even had he wanted to. 

This young Comanche of Ben's and Ella's had her endearing qualities too - she was as full of frolic and laughter as any normal child, and her quick mind could turn a joke out of nearly any run-of-the-mill experience. There was the day she ran into the house obviously excited, her face flushed with triumph. "Papa, papa, I'm standing second in my spelling class; aren't you proud of me!" "Yes I am, and here's a quarter to prove it." 

She thanked him prettily but was obviously teetering on the edge of a delicious toe-wriggling laugh. Ben looked at her with sharp suspicion. "Maud, how many are in your spelling class?" "Two!" she screamed, and ran from the room, sending her laughter back to remind Ben that he had been "rooked."

The woman that grew from this child was forthright, determined, and mentally alert. If she saw something that needed altering she would effect the change immediately with no pretense at tact or patience. Diplomacy to her was an unknown quantity. Her

Epilogue 

[223] first marriage was a mistake, and she took what, in her time, were considered drastic steps to right the wrong. She secured a divorce. 

Perfection was the goal in any task she set her hand to, and she usually achieved it. Her needlework, be it a darned sock, a mended rent, or a bit of fluff and lace for a trousseau, was exquisite. She was an expert cabinetmaker, an excellent cook, and a surprisingly good electrician. When she sold her home in Oklahoma City the purchaser thought the furnishings so completely harmonious and "right" that he insisted upon buying them in toto, including the pet canary.

Eventually Maud remarried and moved to California, where she bought and successfully operated a prune orchard. There were no children, but her warm hospitality drew nephews and nieces to the "ranch" which nearly every summer was packed with the fun and nonsense of youngsters. One day she chanced to slow down long enough to sit on her porch and survey her domain with a critical eye. She turned to her husband who was close by. "Walter, I never noticed it before but the garage door's all wrong; it faces the road, and anyone passing by gets an ugly view of the tools and junk stored there. The door should be on the other side, and the spot where it is now ought to be walled up and planted with climbing roses or something."

Walter made the casual observation that it could be done without too much difficulty, and went on with his work until a great pounding and clatter jerked him up. He stared incredulously, for

Maud, in overalls and gloves, was taking the garage door off its rollers and figuring how to knock out the opposite wall. 

As she approached middle age her health began to flag, and for one so full of zest this was the finish line. Maud died in 1929, snapping the tape that separates this world from the one that stands as no more than a shadowy conjecture in the future of all men.

[224] 

On September 22, 1883, Ben and Ella lost their third child, and early the following morning the fourth little girl was born. Despite the fact that she entered the world on the heels of tragedy, Theda, of all the girls, seemed to be the one fabricated from shafts of sunlight, the laughter of Ben's trout-streams, and all the gaiety that Mary Ella had ever dreamed of. The plump, blue-eyed, brown-haired little girl was, in the words of Mary Ella, "mortally cute," and the sight of her swishing through a room or down the street, intentionally swinging her skirts to show off the homemade novelty braid of her petticoats, was more than refreshing. She was a saucy cherub, banishing care from the minds of all who observed her. When tears stained her baby cheeks they sprang from excessive laughter, and her laugh had a God-given quality.

It was full, rich, and always the leader that coaxed and urged on the laughter of other children.

In the first grade Theda acquired a beau, a golden-eyed, darkhaired youngster named Roy Leighton McKinney, who grew up with his mouth twisted in a perpetual grin and his eyes dancing with amusement. He would ride his white pony 'round and 'round and 'round the block, all on the chance of being favored with a glance from his "calico queen"; nor was it long before his presence was an accepted feature of Sunday dinner as well as the hours before and after school. From the years of licorice shoelaces, to those of black cigars, Roy loved Theda, and together they found life a wondrous thing.

In due time Theda, like Maud, was sent to Bishop Garrett's School in Dallas, and it was here that she first studied voice. Here too the realization began to creep over her that locked in her throat was some unusual power, a gift that could be developed through arduous training and study until it was capable of carrying her to the fabulous heights of a schoolgirl's dream of success. She started out tone-deaf, and during assembly the head-

Epilogue 

[225] master not infrequently requested that Miss Munson remain silent so that her classmates could be heard, and in tune. However, no one doubted the amplitude of her voice nor the heart that inspired it, and she was sent to New York to study music. Roy put his heart in his letters and made them so frequent that all the excitement of a city, and the growing consciousness of new worlds to conquer, could not compete with his demands for attention. 

It was a thrilling two years, rich in accomplishment, full of promise for the future and so glowing were her accounts to Roy that, half jokingly and half in desperation, he sent a letter addressed to Miss Theda Munson, New York City. Inside he quipped: 

"I've no doubt but what by this time you've become so famous and well known that New York City is the only address necessary.' 

Probably there was another letter in the same mail that insured the prompt delivery of Roy's; but whatever the reason, Theda did get it, and she lost no time in replying. "You're quite right; I received your letter immediately upon its arrival in New York."

Roy and Dan Cupid finally conquered, for though Theda did not for years completely give up her music, she did realize that, for a woman, there could be no equivocation between a real career and a normal home with a husband and children. That these two inalienable passions should become a confused issue was natural, for either road that she chose would be shadowed with speculation on the way of life that had been rejected. She wrote her mother: But when I think of stopping [studying voice] ever I feel as though the biggest part of me were dying and being buried. It almost chokes me but on the other hand I've a horror of being an old maid and I fear I've a love for somebody else. I'm sure I don't know what's the matter with me; 

I've cried for a month . . . I'll always do as you say, always, always for 

I worship you . . . I don't know what to do. Can you understand this confused stuff?

[226] 

Theda and Roy were married on the evening of October 10, 1906, and left immediately for Little Rock, Arkansas, where they made their home. The passing years were filled with song, laughter and good times, for Theda and Roy took the rearing of five boys in their happy stride. There was time too for Theda to spend two summers in Chicago, studying with her former teacher, Herbert Witherspoon - she was doing her best work then, and Witherspoon proudly proclaimed hers the finest contralto in the country. He urged an operatic career, but by this time she answered unhesitatingly that she had chosen her career years ago, and now had her hands happily full. She did give generously of her time and talents in her home city of Little Rock, and she sang with two different church choirs for eighteen years. 

Roy's tragic death in 1932 stilled the song in Theda's throat. 

Quite simply she said: "I'll never sing again."

This is a vow that she has kept, but for the sake of her boys she did not, to all outward appearances, renounce the joie de vivre that they had learned to associate with her. In time she returned to Denison, installing her most beloved lares and penates in a very comfortable little country house set upon a great sweep of wide lawn. It is a fine place for the neighboring grandchildren to play, and for the more distant ones to visit; a gay, carefree home completely, wholeheartedly Theda. 

New Year's Day of the year 1886 witnessed the culmination of Mary Ella's secret hopes and Ben's fondest dreams: they had a son, William Benjamin Munson, Jr. Before he could see well,

Mary Ella cradled him in her arms and resolved that here was one boy who would not grow up to be dubbed a "Southern wastrel, overly fond of Bourbon and petticoats." She smiled happily at her husband.

Epilogue 

[227] "You can just bet your bottom dollar I won't spoil him; he'll be a fine boy." "H'mm," grunted Ben Sr., "he is a fine boy!"

Seemingly it was not long before Mary Ella realized with a start that her blond, blue-eyed, curly-haired son was no longer a baby. He was a real boy, uninhibited by the august presence of three older sisters, and already an accepted member of the neighborhood gang. An instructive, sensible amount of responsibility was in order. "Son, how'd you like to go in business with me?"

Silence, like darkness from a window shade whose spring has suddenly snapped, cascaded over the room, cutting off Theda's laugh, Maud's taunting remarks, and the scuffle of Ben's feet as he danced away from her after delivering a short jab in the direction of her ribs. Linley's voice trailed off in the middle of a sentence, and five-year-old Eloise looked up wonderingly from her picture book. The children stared stupidly at their mother and then all eyes came to rest on Ben. He shifted his weight, then spoke in an unnaturally loud voice. "What do you mean, mother?” "I mean would you like to go into partnership with me? I'll give you some chickens and it will be your job to feed and care for them. I'll pay you the market price for your eggs and we'll bank the money under both our names; every time you fail to care for the project you're to pay me." "Sure! Sure, I'll do it; that's a bargain!" "Oh pooh," said Maud, coming out of her trance; "probably all you'll get out of it is the chicken pox." 

Theda threw back her head and screamed with laughter; Linley too was laughing, but Ben flung out of the room. "Smarties! You'll see; I'm going to make some money." 

Mary Ella smiled indulgently and went to see about supper.

[228] 

The partnership of mother and son lasted a good many years in fact they built and sold a house together after Ben was married. 

And Maud was to hear of his bank account.

Mr. Fry, the violin teacher, was on hand to give Maud her music lesson when Ben, coming in from play, burst through the room and yielded to the temptation of giving her his customary poke. 

Maud flung her violin up as a shield and Ben's fist went through it. Mary Ella appeared dramatically in the doorway. "We will replace your violin, Mr. Fry; Ben will pay for it out of his own funds." 

Ben's face became a dull, angry red. "I will not!" he screamed. “Bust my bank account for an old fiddle? Not if I drop dead this instant and you have to bury me without a coffin! I won't bust my bank account, not for anything!"

In order to supplement the bank account he was given the job of painting the back fence. Going out to check on his progress, Mary Ella discovered that all the boys in the neighborhood were assembled. They were obviously divided into two teams, with each team trying to get its side of the fence painted first. Astride the fence was Ben, and he was shouting. "Hurry, John; Henry's beating you on this other side. Say, Henry, you'll have to paint a little faster; John's catching up. "Well, Tom Sawyer!" snapped Mary Ella; "is this the way you paint a fence?" 

Ben grinned, obviously pleased with the comparison. "Well, it's one way of getting it done in a hurry."

Like most boys of his time, Ben learned to ride young and, like his father, he loved horses. His first mount was a tan pony named 

Lexington. It had been a gift from Ben Sr., who brought it back from Lexington, Kentucky, when he went there with Mary Ella to deliver an address at the commencement exercises of the University of Kentucky. With a fond eye on Lexington's glossy tan

Epilogue 

[229] coat and young Ben's skillful handling of the horse, Ben Sr. in time contributed such accessories as a pair of high soft leather riding boots that came above the rider's knees and were the exact shade of Lexington, a matching riding cap fashioned like a jockey's, and finally a pony cart trimmed in tan to match the tan leather harness. 

Some years later Charlie Newton gave Ben a spirited horse named Houston, after the Houston Ranch where it was foaled. By this time Ben really needed a horse, for not only did he have a paper route, but every evening he would help round up all the milch cows and herd them to the pasturage outside the city limits in accordance with the law of that day. It was not uncommon for 

Houston to buck for blocks on end, and Ben was one of the few people who could ride him. Among the rare occasions when Mary 

Ella had to reach for the smelling salts was when J. T. Munson came hurrying into the house and delivered a drawling obituary. "Ella, I hate to have to tell you this but, well, I'm afraid I've got some bad news for you; at least, well... "Well, tell it then!" said Mary Ella, stamping her foot. "What's happened?" 

"Seems Ben was riding over by the railroad tracks when Houston stepped in a gopher hole and fell with him and broke his neck." 

Mary Ella's face was ashen. "Ben? You mean Ben broke his neck?" "No, Ella, Houston broke his neck."

After four years in the University of Texas, Ben entered his father's beloved cotton mill in 1906. Now W.B. could view his son in a man's world; was close enough to gauge his progress and to judge his ability. He was not disappointed. The young man was obviously able and discerning. In time he became secretarytreasurer of the mill and with ever-increasing confidence W.B. Sr.

[230] 

slowly placed the reins of his busy, active life into the capable hands of his only son. Ben, like his father, was widely interested in civic affairs and the general advancement of Denison. As president of the school board for twelve years he saw to it that the schools of Denison experienced their greatest expansion in facilities and organization. He was elected president of The Citizens' 

National Bank, and was director and twice president of the Chamber of Commerce. It was in this capacity that he engaged in the promotion of good highways. He was a member of the State 

Y.M.C.A. Executive Committee, and waged a valiant though losing battle for the survival of that organization in Denison. 

In January, 1912, Ben became general manager of the cotton mill, and gained distinction in textile circles, being president of the duck manufacturers' national organization, vice president of 

The Texas State Manufacturers' Association, and director of the 

Cotton Textile Institute of New York. Upon his father's death in 1930 he was made president of the mill. While en route to 

Dallas to attend a meeting of The Texas State Manufacturers'

Association as chairman of the Resolutions Committee - he was stricken with a heart attack, and died a week later on February 25, 1936. 

So wholehearted and purposeful were his efforts that time has not obliterated them; they still stand as a mighty link in the chain of his city's progress. 

Ben's and Ella's last child was a girl, born September 20, 1889.

She was the only one of Ben's youngsters whom he himself did not name, and, ironically enough, she was the one who in years to come was closest to him. One of Ella's dearest friends, Louise

Perry, heard Ben growling about the difficulty of finding a name for a fifth girl and offered her advice.

Epilogue 

231] "I'm going to name this baby; her name will honor both Ella and myself - Ella and Louise. Put them together and you have 

Eloise. Eloise Munson! Did you," she inquired teasingly of Ben, "ever think up a prettier name than that?"

Ben grinned. He liked the idea of paying homage to Ella in this way, and he shared the latter's fondness for Louise Perry. "Eloise," he mused; "it's rather unusual, but then of course she'll be named for two unusual women. Unusually nice, I might add." "You approve, then?" "Approve? I applaud.”

And Ben went right on applauding Eloise for the remainder of his life. 

This tiny, red-haired, hazel-eyed girl was the essence of femininity, with exquisitely small hands and feet; there was usually a puzzled expression around her eyes, and her mouth was inclined to pout. What really fascinated Ben, and awed everyone else, was the rapier speed of her mental processes. The essential character of the child was so evident that its analysis was ridiculously simple, but that did not make it any easier to believe - utterly feminine with the most masculine mind God ever wished upon a female. Years later, after Ben's death in fact, a family friend and close associate of Ben's, Judge F. C. Dillard of Sherman, summed it up very succinctly in a statement he made to some sales promoter in his office who asked whom in Denison did the Judge consider as one of the most capable businessmen. The reply was instantaneous. "The most capable businessman and the smartest I know, in or around Denison, is Miss Eloise Munson."

She was a demure, quiet child with her head usually tucked in a book. At twelve she claimed Charles Dickens for her favorite author and knew whereof she spoke, for she had read his com-

[232] 

plete works and was already rereading the tales she liked the best. 

Ben frequently waylaid her to make a fourth at whist, for her analytical mind made her good at cards. 

It is small wonder then that, after her graduation from the 

University of Texas and with the first World War playing havoc with his office force, Ben should hit upon the idea of "getting Eloise down there to help out." She had taken her degree in fine arts and walked into her father's office totally unschooled in the ways of business; but she procured books on shorthand and typing and set about teaching herself, studying at night and practicing by day, and actually made opportunities to further her knowledge of anything in any way connected with the office. She saved most of her early salary and bought some stock with it. When Ben discovered this he exploded. "Eloise, what in sam hill did you do that for! You don't know straight up about such things; why didn't you come and ask me?" 

Eloise bridled. "Because I'm here to learn, and I can't do that by running to you for all the answers; it's my money, and if I lose it it's nobody's tough luck but mine.” “Humph!” snorted Ben; "you deserve to lose your shirt, but if it's any comfort to you I don't think you will."

Eloise, hurt and defiant at the outset of the conversation, snatched at this final crumb and fed upon it long after her father had stomped out of the office. It was not long after this that Mary 

Ella made a natural inquiry. "Ben, is Eloise really a help to you in the office?”

Ben, loosening his necktie, regarded his wife speculatively for a moment. "Help to me? She's the smartest thing that ever made tracks in my office."

As years went on, Ben's dependence upon this child grew, and

Epilogue 

[233] he shared with her a mutual respect and admiration that none of the other children could know. He was, of course, inordinately proud of them all, and he found a special kind of comfort in the thought of his son's being more than usually capable of carrying on his name and his ideals; but to have a daughter sharing his day-to-day experiences in the business world, and materially lightening many of his burdens - well, that was the kind of bonus that served as a tonic every day in the week. This closeness of father and daughter is clearly reflected in the following poem and letter written by Ben to Eloise while the latter was vacationing. 

She was of high school age, a sophomore to be exact, when the poem was written, and at the time of the letter she was wellalmost a junior in the Munson Realty Company. The one is affectionate and teasing, the other admittedly dependent. ―― - 

July 16th, 1903

MY DEAR ELOISE: 

Your letter of tenth so bright and cute

With pictures by pen of the City of Butte 

As swift as the flight of the beautiful dove 

It came with its treasure of dutiful love. 

How happy it made all your people at home 

To know that your heart remains, though your body may roam

In lands that are distant and grand to behold 

Where mountains are high and the breezes are cold. 

We hope that you found dear Miss Cory quite well 

And trust she'll enjoy your and your sister's visit a spell 

That mountain and stream and hillside and glen 

May ring with the laughter from the artist's den.

[234] 

Your sister Miss Maud and her uncle J.T.

Have flown to the mountains from heat to be free 

Your mama and I with Theda and Ben

Still hold the hot fort as best we can. 

Aunt Sallie and Theda are planning a trip 

To see Uncle Charlie so off they will slip 

And leave Mammie and Jessie, your mama and I 

With Ben and the dogs in the heat to fry.

Poor Don has been hunting the place over for you 

And finding you not, he is looking for something to do.

He comes to the office and tries to keep books 

But thinks of the absent and lonesomely looks.

The weather is hot and the flies are a sight 

But breezes blow gently in evening and night

The grapes are now ripe and deliciously sweet While melons are plenty and luscious to eat. 

I know it is time to suspend this effort at rhyme 

So love and good wishes to all in your party and clime 

And kisses for you and dear Linley too are enclosed 

I hope to hear often from one so sweetly disposed. Excuse,

PAPA 

Denison, Tex. 10-9-22 

MY DEAR ELOISE: I am wondering whether or not you are soon to return home, so that I can have at least a day or two as a vacation. 

It has now been more than a year since I have enjoyed even one whole day's vacation from the drudgery of business. You know your vacations have been long and numerous and I have been pleased to have you enjoy them.

I should like to have you remember that throughout this long almost unbearable summer I have roasted, fried, and baked during the day in the office and served as bait for mosquitoes during the sweltering nights of * French poodle.

Epilogue 

[235] August and September. While I am tough and patient there is a limit to my capacity to endure hardships. 

The duck season opens on October 16th and I am hoping that I may be able to take a few days holiday in order to get the cobwebs of business out of my mind and enjoy a much needed relaxation. You know I am now nearly 78 years old, my hearing almost gone; my sight badly crippled and my memory a thing of the past, but I am still able to appreciate enjoyment when I get a chance.

Think it over and see if you can arrange to be home by the 16th. Everything running smoothly. 

Yours lovingly,

РАРА Like her father, Eloise was always good at games, but her favorite recreation was golf; and tucked in little-used closets and cabinets of the family home are silver loving cups and trophies to prove her skill in this sport. She could hold her own in nearly any sport, however, and it is easy to imagine that she made the most of her hard-earned vacations. 

Eloise, her father, and Ben Jr., were actually a triumvirate, but as time sapped the elder Ben's strength and energies, Eloise and young Ben joined hands in taking over the myriad responsibilities of the mill, the Munson Realty Company, and the Nueces Land & Live Stock Company. They worked in complete harmony, and no brother-and-sister relationship could have been welded with stronger affection. When Ben Jr. realized that his own death was going to sever this coalition he, of course, feared that the responsibility was too great for any single pair of shoulders and urged Eloise to lighten the load somewhat by selling the mill. She humored him with a halfhearted promise, but the mill was not sold. It was W.B.'s mill, his son's mill, and the mill was why 

Ben III was studying textile engineering. Ben Jr. had counted on training his eldest son and namesake in the ways of the mill, developing him until his stride was capable of filling the tracks

INTERIOR OF THE MUNSON HOME 

Hooked rugs shown were made by Mrs.

Munson

Epilogue 

[237] his predecessors had made, and of advancing the path that they had beaten out. This too was Eloise's responsibility. She reasoned that a plant was either capably managed or it was not — whether the manager was a man or a woman had nothing to do with it. 

With grim determination, and her eye focused on the future, she "took on" the presidency of the mill, and her tiny frame has proved a mighty bulwark in this organization. With terrific drive and force she has profitably steered the mill through uncharted and ever-expanding lanes, weathering the hazardous and unpredictable years of government regulations that have so unmercifully buffeted all small business. Nor has she ever given anything but studious attention to the schooling of Ben III in the ways and operation of the business. It is rather nice to think that perhaps the other two Bens are really close at hand, cheering from the sidelines.

Thus Time rolls on, with each man's destiny shaped largely by his own energies. Ben Munson proved a skillful potter, and bequeathed good clay to the line succeeding him. May each descendant gain renewed strength of purpose from the light shed by their mighty patriarch.

JOHN GODFREY SAXE. Progress and Other Poems. (Burlington, Vermont, 1849) 

We quote the opening lines:

PROGRESS, A SATIRE 

In this, our happy and "progressive" age, When all alike ambitious cares engage; 

When beardless boys to sudden sages grow, And "Miss" her nurse abandons for a beau;

When for their dogmas Non-Resistants fight, When dunces lecture, and when dandies write;

When, martial honors to the children thrown, Each five-foot minor is a "Major" grown;

When matrons, seized with oratoric pangs, Give happy birth to masculine harangues, And spinsters, trembling for the nation's fate, Neglect their stockings to preserve the State; 

When critic-wits their brazen lustre shed 

On golden authors whom they never read; 

With parrot praise of "Roman grandeur" speak, And in bad English eulogize the Greek .

APPENDIX II SOUTHERN RAILWAY AND CONSTRUCTION COMPANY 

This company drew up a Certificate of Dissolution on the 20th of April, 1912. J. T. Munson was President at this time. Munson & Bro. owned, at this time, 650 shares of stock; W. B. Munson owned 106 shares; Ed. Steger owned 359 shares; Mrs. R. C. Foster 219 shares; Hugh Halsell 101 shares; and J. W. Russell 100 shares.

Bill of Sale dated 20th of May, 1911, $325,000 worth of bonds to Ed. Steger, which were all of issued and outstanding bonds. These bonds were up as collateral for $50,000 note made to National Bank of Commerce of St. 

Louis; also sold to Steger 500 shares of Denison, Bonham & New Orleans

Railroad Co., all of issued and outstanding stock of road, represented by 

13 certificates in National Bank of Commerce of St. Louis as collateral.

This bill of sale was pursuant to terms of a contract signed May 16th, 1911. 

The Southern Railway and Construction Company was owner, on May 

20th, 1911, of all bonds and stocks of D.B. & N.O. Railway. The Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railway Company of Texas had operated the D.B. &

N.O. under a contract agreement that had been signed on April 9th, 1901. 

At an annual meeting of the stockholders of the D.B. & N.O., on January 28th, 1896, the stockholders resolved that the directors be authorized to go ahead with the construction of the line from Denison to Bonham, and on to make a connection with the Gulf, Colorado & Santa Fe. Also authorized issuance of bonds and borrowing of money. Bonds were to be issued at the rate of $12,000 per mile. 

The D.B. & N.O. was chartered January 12th, 1887.

The line was 24 miles long, Denison to Bonham. 

DENISON & WASHITA VALLEY RAILWAY CO. 

This company was organized January 7th, 1886, and secured its charter on July 1st of the same year. The charter was granted by an act of Congress, to construct a line through the Indian Territory. The line was later to be

(Courtesy of Missouri-Kansas-Texas Lines) 

33

Appendix II 

[247] operated by the Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railway Company of Texas, under the terms of a 50-year lease, beginning January 1st, 1894. 

The directors of the company were W. B. Munson, W. S. Nevins, J. T. 

Munson, J. C. Field and R. C. Foster, all of Denison; and H. C. Rouse, Colgate Hoyt, C. W. Wetmore of New York; and T. C. Purdy of St. Louis.

In 1902 C. G. Hedge of New York and A. A. Allen of Dallas replaced Purdy and Wetmore.

The capital stock was $305,000. 

In 1894 the officers were: W. B. Munson, President; T. C. Purdy, Vice 

President; E. D. Halsted of Denison, Secretary-Treasurer; C. G. Hedge, Assistant Secretary-Treasurer. Attorneys and General Counsel were Foster and Wilkinson of Denison. In 1902 J. R. Handy was Secretary-Treasurer. 

On February 2nd, 1889, there were $305,000 bonds, due in 1929.

On July 6th, 1893, bonds were $695,000, and due in forty years. 

The two largest stockholders were the Southwestern Coal & Improvement Company, which owned 2635 shares, and W. B. Munson, who owned 

335 shares. This was an interlocking holding, however, for Ben owned stock in the Southwestern Company, and others who had stock in the railroad also owned stock in the same company. 

The Baldwin people built the first locomotive for the railroad. There were 16.53 miles of trackage in the Territory, and 4.86 miles of trackage in Texas, although the latter figure had originally been 5.25 miles. The company owned one locomotive, freight, and one caboose; it also owned 

119 coal cars.

The total receipts of the road from July 1st, 1892, to June 30th, 1893, were $35,564.64; and the total outlay for the same period of time was $20,089.51 - a profit of $15,475.13; but there is no record as to what disposition was made of this money. 

The passenger revenue for the first month - passengers were glad enough to ride in the caboose, we suppose was $90.15, and the freight revenue was $3838.29. 

THE DENISON BUILDING AND LAND COMPANY 

In 1904 the company paid a dividend of $6776, which left a balance on hand of $3085.

In 1905 the company paid a dividend of $3388, which left a balance on hand of $1327.

In 1910, of the stockholders, 23 lived in Vermont, 9 in New Hampshire, 9 in Texas, 10 in Massachusetts, 1 in Oklahoma, 2 in Rhode Island, 2 in

[248] 

Kansas, 1 in New Jersey, 1 in Maine, 1 in Connecticut, and 1 in Pennsylvania. 

An agreement of dissolution was reached on the 30th of April, 1910, and at that time the Munsons owned 2300 shares of the total issue of 3388 shares. 

NUECES LAND & LIVE STOCK COMPANY 

This company, as has already been explained in the body of the biography, was organized after the death of Mr. Jot Gunter. Ben perfected the organization on July 16th, 1909, and the whole idea behind the company was to relieve Mrs. Gunter in a pressing situation. The original incorporators were J. T. Munson, L. B. Moore, M. T. Mathes, C. J. O'Maley, T. L. Clark, and E. C. Sturgis. Although Ben was not at first a stockholder, a later distribution of the stock included: W. B. Munson, 118 shares; Charles 

Jones, 47 shares; J. T. Munson, 71 shares; L. B. Moore, 95 shares; Sturgis, 95 shares; and J. R. Handy, 95 shares.

The land controlled by this company is approximately 73,000 acres, located in Lasalle, McMullen, and Duval Counties. At one time bills payable amounted to over $200,000, but this amount has been greatly decreased, largely due to skillful management in the hands of the two Ben Munsons and, in later years, those of Miss Eloise Munson, the present manager.

The good management was helped on its way to success through the medium of oil leases, and the royalties from a small field that was discovered in 1937 within the confines of the company's holdings. The lands are also covered by grazing leases, the average rental over a number of years being about 25¢ per acre.

AMERICAN COTTON SPINNING COMPANY 

The first meeting was held August 19th, 1899; C. S. Cobb was Chairman of this meeting and B. M. Seward was Secretary. The earliest directors were: 

C. S. Cobb, W. B. Munson, J. A. Smith, Hiram Brooks, B. M. Seward. The charter had already been secured at this time, and the incorporators were the same as the directors. Mr. Smith was of Bessemer City, North Carolina, and the other men were from Denison. The capital stock was $100,000, divided into 1000 shares. J. A. Smith was President, Brooks was Vice President, and Seward the Secretary-Treasurer. J. T. Munson, for self and M. F.

Foster, agreed to sell to this new company the grounds, buildings and machinery of what was known as Denison Cotton Mill property, for $50,000 in gold bonds which were to be payable on September 1st, 1904, and these

Appendix II 

[249] were secured by deed of trust. The President and the Secretary each received a salary of $100 per month. The company borrowed $5000 in Philadelphia 

October 1st, 1899, and twelve days later borrowed $3000 more from the same place. On December 4th, 1899, they sold $5000 worth of bonds for $4000 with the idea of buying a new engine. On January 6th, 1900, the 

President reported to the directors that the company had such a poor reputation that it was impossible for him to borrow any additional money. 

Brooks retired in April 1900, and Smith got out the following month.

DENISON COTTON MILL COMPANY 

This company incorporated August 5th, 1905. The incorporators were 

W. B. Munson, J. B. McDougall, S. V. Ancker, J. R. Handy, and T. L. 

Clark (Mr. Clark was from Fannin County). The capital stock was fixed at $150,000. The directors were the same as the incorporators, and 15 shares of stock were issued. W. B. Munson was elected President, Ancker 

Vice President, J. R. Handy Secretary, and R. W. Munson Treasurer. The company issued bonds for $150,000. The original stockholders were: W. B. 

Munson, 901 shares; Munson & Bro., 325 shares; J. T. Munson, 152 shares; 

McDougall, 54 shares; Ancker, 63 shares; Clark, 4; and Handy, 1. 

On November 4th, 1908, W. B. Munson, Jr., was appointed Treasurer at a salary of $100 per month, and in January, 1912, was made both Manager and Treasurer. The President's annual report on January 12th, 1909, showed resources and liabilities at $625,098. The capital stock was increased to $350,000 on June 15th, 1909. On January 1st, 1910, the resources and liabilities were at $750,022; and on the same date of the following year this figure had risen to $765,887. In January, 1912, Ben owned 2467 shares of the stock, and the company showed a surplus and undivided profits of $171,209. 

On January 1st, 1914, the totals were $843,697; by January 1st, 1916, they were $943,370; on January 1st, 1917, they were $1,072,002; and on December 31st, 1917, the figures had risen to $1,398,982.

On January 12th, 1926, the charter was amended and the capital stock was increased to $700,000 by a 100 per cent stock dividend. On the 7th of 

January, 1931, Ben Munson, Jr. was elected President on the motion of 

Mr. J. R. Handy.

On the 17th of June, 1936, Eloise Munson was elected President and 

General Manager. On the 2nd of November, 1940, Ben Munson III was elected Vice President.

After Pearl Harbor the cotton mill rolled up its sleeves for a 100 per cent war production schedule. On March 26th, 1945, the mill received the Army-

[250] 

Navy “E” award for excellence, the first mill west of the Mississippi to be so honored.

MUNSON REALTY COMPANY 

This company was organized and incorporated on February 5th, 1915. The incorporators were J. T. Munson, W. B. Munson, and W. B. Munson, Jr. 

The capital stock was fixed at $150,000. Each of the brothers held 747 shares of stock, and Ben Jr. held 6 shares. J. T. Munson was President, and 

W. B. Munson was the Vice President. On January 1st, 1918, W. B. became 

President; on January 13th, 1931, W. B. Munson, Jr., became President; and Eloise Munson was made President, January 12th, 1937. A few sample figures will serve to show the growth of the company: 

January 9th, 1917, resources and liabilities $188,000 

January 11th, 1921, January 13th, 1925, 66 66 66 $246,000 66 66 66 $383,000

THE DENISON ROD AND GUN CLUB 

"A meeting of gentlemen interested in piscatorial pursuits was called to meet at the Philosophical Hall on Wednesday, May 14th, 1890. Quite a number of gentlemen assembled at the appointed hour. Mr. J. J. Fairbanks was chosen to occupy the chair, and Mr. A. F. Platter to take minutes of the proceedings. The Chairman then briefly stated the object of the meeting, explaining that it was the desire of a number of their angling citizens to procure and purchase a resort where they could enjoy to their hearts' content the sport to which they had a natural or cultivated propensity. He stated that such a place had been procured in Antelope 

Park, San Juan, Colorado."

The above is an excerpt from the first minutes. The membership was to be held strictly at not more than 50 gentlemen, and the yearly dues were fixed at $100. W. B. Munson was the instigator of this plan and was, for many years, the actual manager. The club later bought property in the environs of Denison, where they made a lake and built a very attractive clubhouse. The site was selected by W. B. Munson and the golf course was laid out by his son, W. B. Munson, Jr.
